The critical disordered pinning measure
Ran Wei, Jinjiong Yu

Abstract
. In this paper, we study a disordered pinning model induced by a random walk whose increments have a finite fourth moment and vanishing first and third moments. It is known that this model is marginally relevant, and moreover, it undergoes a phase transition in an intermediate disorder regime. We show that, in the critical window, the point-to-point partition functions converge to a unique limiting random measure, which we call the critical disordered pinning measure. We also obtain an analogous result for a continuous counterpart to the pinning model, which is closely related to two other models: one is a critical stochastic Volterra equation that gives rise to a rough volatility model, and the other is a critical stochastic heat equation with multiplicative noise that is white in time and delta in space.



MSC 2010.
Primary: 82B44, Secondary: 35R60, 60H15, 91G20.

Keywords.
Marginal relevance, critical temperature, disordered pinning model, directed polymer, stochastic heat equation, rough volatility model.

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Background

In this paper, we study the
disordered pinning model
(abbreviated as the
pinning model
hereafter), which is also known as a model of
pinning on a defect line
(see, e.g., the monographs of Giacomin
[
Gia07
,
Gia11
]
and den Hollander
[
dH09
]
for a comprehensive introduction). This model holds significance in statistical mechanics, serving as a disordered system that is both interesting enough to have a phase transition and simple enough for extensive mathematical analysis.

The model is comprised of a renewal process and a random field on
\mathbb{Z}
, representing the polymer chain and disorders on the membrane, respectively. Let
\tau=\{\tau_{0},\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\cdots\}
, where
\tau_{0}=0
and
(\tau_{n}-\tau_{n-1})_{n\geq 1}
are i.i.d.
{\mathbbm{N}}
-valued random variables, be the renewal process with probability law
{\mathbf{P}}
.
Let the disorder
\omega:=(\omega_{x})_{x\in{\mathbbm{Z}}}
be a family of i.i.d. random variables with probability law
{\mathbbm{P}}
. Expectations with respect to
{\mathbf{P}}
and
{\mathbbm{P}}
are denoted by
{\mathbf{E}}
and
{\mathbbm{E}}
, respectively. We assume independence between the renewal process
\tau
and the disorder
\omega
, with
\omega
satisfying

(1.1)

{\mathbbm{E}}[\omega_{0}]=0,\quad{\rm Var}(\omega_{0})=1,\quad\text{and}\quad\lambda(\beta):=\log{\mathbbm{E}}[\exp(\beta\omega_{0})]<+\infty,\quad\forall\beta\in(-\beta_{0},\beta_{0}),

where
\beta_{0}
is some positive constant.
The model is then defined via a Gibbs transform

(1.2)

\frac{{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{P}}_{N,\beta}^{\omega,h}}{{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{P}}}(\tau):=\frac{1}{Z_{N,\beta}^{\omega,h}}\exp\Big(\sum_{n=1}^{N}(\beta\omega_{n}+h-\lambda(\beta))\mathbbm{1}_{\{n\in\tau\}}\Big),

where
\beta>0
is the inverse temperature,
h\in{\mathbbm{R}}
denotes an external field,
n\in\tau
means that
n
is a renewal time, and

(1.3)

Z_{N,\beta}^{\omega,h}:={\mathbf{E}}\Big[\exp\Big(\sum_{n=1}^{N}(\beta\omega_{n}+h-\lambda(\beta))\mathbbm{1}_{\{n\in\tau\}}\Big)\Big]

is the partition function which makes
{\mathbf{P}}_{N,\beta}^{\omega,h}
a (random) probability measure.
When
\beta=0
, signifying the absence of disorder, the resulting model
{\mathbf{P}}_{N}^{h}:={\mathbf{P}}_{N,\beta=0}^{\omega,h}
is referred to as the
pure pinning model
.

A particularly interesting setup is to assume the renewal
\tau
is recurrent, i.e.,
{\mathbf{P}}(\tau_{1}<\infty)=1
, and

(1.4)

K(n):={\mathbf{P}}(\tau_{1}=n)=\frac{L(n)}{n^{1+\alpha}}

for some
\alpha\geq 0
and slowly varying function
L(\cdot)
. Examples of slowly varying functions include constant functions and logarithmic functions (see
[
BGT89
]
for details). For different values of
\alpha
, determining whether the disordered measure
{\mathbf{P}}_{N,\beta}^{\omega,h}
significantly differs from the pure measure
{\mathbf{P}}_{N}^{h}
for arbitrarily small
\beta>0
becomes a subtle problem, which we will elucidate below.

We introduce the
free energy
of the system, defined as

(1.5)

F(\beta,h):=\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\log Z_{N,\beta}^{\omega,h}{\mathrel{\to}^{{\mathbbm{P}}\text{-a.s.}}}\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}{\mathbbm{E}}\big[\log Z_{N,\beta}^{\omega,h}\big].

The existence and non-randomness of the limit are proven in
[
Gia11
]
. Moreover, it shows that for any
\beta\geq 0
fixed,
F(\beta,h)
is non-negative, convex, and non-decreasing as a function of
h
. Thus, the system undergoes a phase transition in
h
at

(1.6)

h_{c}(\beta):=\inf\{h:F(\beta,h)>0\}.

The phase
\{(\beta,h):F(\beta,h)>0\}
is called the
localized regime
, wherein, as
N\to\infty
, the number of renewal times under
{\mathbf{P}}_{N,\beta}^{\omega,h}
is proportional to
N
. This signifies that the energy gain dominates the entropy cost for the renewal process to visit the origin with atypical high frequency. Conversely, the phase
\{(\beta,h):F(\beta,h)=0\}
is called the
delocalized regime
, where the number of renewal times is
o(N)
, as the energy gain at renewal returns is less attractive to the renewal process.

It can be seen by Jensen’s inequality that

(1.7)

{\mathbbm{E}}\big[\log Z_{N,\beta}^{\omega,h}\big]\leq\log{\mathbbm{E}}\big[Z_{N,\beta}^{\omega,h}\big]\quad\text{with}\quad{\mathbbm{E}}\big[Z_{N,\beta}^{\omega,h}\big]={\mathbf{E}}\Big[\exp\Big({\sum_{n=0}^{N}h\mathbbm{1}_{\{n\in\tau\}}}\Big)\Big]=Z_{N}^{h},

where
Z_{N}^{h}
is the partition function of the pure pinning model (
\beta=0
). Note that the pure free energy

F(h)=F(0,h)=\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\log Z_{N}^{h}

is deterministic and has an explicit form. It is well established (see
[
Gia11
]
) that
F(h)=0
if and only if
h\leq 0
, i.e.,
h_{c}:=\inf\{h\in{\mathbbm{R}}:F(h)>0\}=0
, and moreover,

(1.8)

\lim\limits_{h\downarrow h_{c}}\frac{\log F(h)}{\log(h-h_{c})}=1\vee\alpha^{-1}.

From (
1.7
), it is evident that
F(\beta,h)\leq F(h)
. Hence, we must have
h_{c}(\beta)\geq h_{c}(0)=0
.

The key question for the pinning model is: does arbitrarily small disorder (
\beta>0
) qualitatively change the critical behavior of the system? In particular, one may ask whether for arbitrarily small
\beta>0
, the
critical exponent
for
F(\beta,h)
differs from
1\vee\alpha^{-1}
that appears in (
1.8
) for the pure model.
It turns out that this can be equivalently characterised in terms of
critical point shift
, namely whether
h_{c}(\beta)>h_{c}(0)=0
for arbitrarily small
\beta>0
, or there exists some
\beta^{\ast}>0
such that
h_{c}(\beta)=0
for
\beta\in[0,\beta^{\ast}]
.
The answer hinges on the exponent
\alpha
in (
1.4
). It is predicted by the
Harris criterion

[
Har74
]
that the disorder is relevant (resp. irrelevant) if
\alpha>1/2
(resp.
\alpha<1/2
). Indeed, it is now known that

\bullet
For
\alpha<\frac{1}{2}
, by
[
Ale08
,
Ton08
,
Lac10
]
the critical exponents for both the disordered model and pure model are the same, and
h_{c}(\beta)\equiv 0=h_{c}
for
\beta>0
small enough. This regime is known as the
disorder irrelevant regime
.

\bullet
For
\alpha>\frac{1}{2}
, it was shown by Giacomin and Toninelli
[
GT06
]
that the critical exponent for the disordered model is at least 2, which is strictly larger than
1\vee\alpha^{-1}
in (
1.8
). Furthermore,
[
AZ09
,
DGLT09
]
established that
h_{c}(\beta)>0
for any
\beta>0
, and later
[
BCP+14
,
CTT17
]
obtained the sharp asymptotics for
h_{c}(\beta)
as
\beta\to 0
. This is called the
disorder relevant regime
.

\bullet
For
\alpha=\frac{1}{2}
, the situation becomes intricate, and it is out of the scope of the Harris criterion. A crucial quantity is
R_{N}:=\sum_{n=1}^{N}{\mathbf{P}}(n\in\tau)^{2}
, the expected number of overlaps for two i.i.d. copies of the renewal process
\tau
. It is shown in
[
BL18
]
that
h_{c}(\beta)>0
for any
\beta>0
(and hence the disorder is relevant) if and only if
R_{N}\sim\sum_{n=1}^{N}1/(nL(n)^{2})\to+\infty
. In this scenario, it is called the
marginally relevant regime
, and
[
BL18
]
provides the sharp asymptotics for
h_{c}(\beta)
as
\beta\to 0
.

In a nutshell, for
\alpha\geq\frac{1}{2}
and when the disorder is relevant, we observe significantly different behavior of the pinning model near the critical curve
h=h_{c}(\beta)
at
(\beta,h_{c}(\beta))=(0,0)
. The disordered system and the pure system belong to two
universality classes
(
cf.

[
CSZ16
, Section 1.3]
): it is believed that there are different universal scaling limits for the two systems, where neither the disorder distribution nor the details of the renewal process are relevant (as long as some mild conditions hold, e.g., (
1.1
) holds and the exponent
\alpha
is fixed).

It is then interesting to study the interpolation of the two universalities.
In view of the partition function, as per (
1.5
), in the localized regime,
Z_{N,\beta}^{\omega,h}
exhibits exponential growth, determined by the exponent
NF(\beta,h)
. This exponent increases as
N
tends to infinity and decreases as both
\beta
and
h
approach 0. Conversely, in the delocalized regime,
Z_{N,\beta}^{\omega,h}
demonstrates sub-exponential growth, with
Z_{N,0}^{\omega,0}\equiv 1
.
A natural question about interpolating is: if we systematically tune down
\beta:=\beta_{N}\downarrow 0
and
h:=h_{N}\downarrow 0
at specific rates as
N\to\infty
, can we ascertain a non-trivial limit for
Z_{N,\beta_{N}}^{\omega,h_{N}}
? The affirmative answer implies the existence of a limit, termed the
weak coupling limit
, situating the model within the
intermediate disorder regime
. The inception of exploring the intermediate disorder regime traces back to
[
AKQ14
]
, focusing on the
1+1
directed polymer model. Subsequent investigations into weak coupling limits include disorder relevant models such as the 2d random field Ising model, the pinning model with
\alpha>1/2
in
[
CSZ16
]
, and marginally relevant models such as the 2d stochastic heat equation, the
1+2
directed polymer model, and the pinning model with
\alpha=1/2
in
[
CSZ17
]
.

For the pinning model with
\alpha>\frac{1}{2}
, a non-trivial weak coupling limit emerges through the choice of
\beta_{N}=\widehat{\beta}{\mathcal{P}}_{1}^{\alpha}(N)
and
h_{N}=\widehat{h}{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{\alpha}(N)
where
\widehat{\beta}>0,\widehat{h}\in{\mathbbm{R}}
, and
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}^{\alpha}(\cdot)
and
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{\alpha}(\cdot)
, both vanishing at infinity, are two regularly varying functions whose exponents depending on
\alpha
(see
[
CSZ16
]
for details). However, the scenario becomes more sophisticated in the marginal case
\alpha=\frac{1}{2}
, as illustrated in
[
CSZ17
]
. The appropriate choice is
h=0
and
\beta_{N}=\widehat{\beta}/\sqrt{R_{N}}
, where
R_{N}=\sum_{n=1}^{N}{\mathbf{P}}(n\in\tau)^{2}\to+\infty
as above and is slowly varying at infinity. Furthermore, the constant
\widehat{\beta}
plays a pivotal role. If
\widehat{\beta}\in(0,1)
, then the weak coupling limit for
Z_{N,\beta_{N}}^{\omega,0}
is log-normal, characterizing a subcritical phase. For the critical phase
\widehat{\beta}=1
and the supercritical phase
\widehat{\beta}>1
,
Z_{N,\beta_{N}}^{\omega,0}\to 0
in probability as
N\to\infty
.

While the behavior of the partition function in the supercritical phase remains mysterious, a recent breakthrough by Caravenna, Sun, and Zygouras in
[
CSZ23
]
concerning the marginally relevant directed polymer in two dimension sheds light on the critical phase. They viewed the random field of point-to-point partition functions as a process of random measures on
{\mathbbm{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbbm{R}}^{2}
, and established the existence of a universal scaling limit known as the
critical 2d stochastic heat flow
. Moreover, the limit can be interpreted as the solution of the stochastic heat equation in the critical dimension 2 and in the critical window, which lies beyond existing solution theories via regularity structures by Hairer
[
Hai14
]
and paracontrolled distributions by Gubinelli et. al.
[
GIP15
]
for subcritical singular SPDEs. Motivated by their work, our study in this paper is centered on the critical phase of the marginally relevant pinning model (Subsection
1.2
), as well as a rough volatility model characterised by the critical stochastic Volterra equation (Subsection
1.3
).

1.2.
The pinning model and weak coupling limit

Instead of the most general setting (
1.2
), we focus on a specific class of pinning models associated to a random walk
S:=(S_{n})_{n\geq 0}
on
\mathbb{Z}
due to technical limitation. To be specific, our proof requires that the renewal process is associated to a Markovian process with sharp enough local limit theorems (see Remark
1.4
and Section
4
). Noting that the times
S
visits
0
give rise to a renewal process, it naturally induces a pinning model. Denote the probability law of
S
by
{\mathbf{P}}^{x}
if
{\mathbf{P}}^{x}(S_{0}=x)=1
, indicating that the starting point of
S
is
x
. The expectation with respect to
{\mathbf{P}}^{x}
is denoted by
{\mathbf{E}}^{x}
. When
x=0
, we omit the superscripts in
{\mathbf{P}}
and
{\mathbf{E}}
to lighten the notation. The distribution of the induced renewal process is then given by

(1.9)

K(n)={\mathbf{P}}(\tau_{1}=n)={\mathbf{P}}(S_{n}=0,S_{k}\neq 0,1\leq k\leq n-1)\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathbf{P}}(n\in\tau)={\mathbf{P}}(S_{n}=0).

To avoid complexities related to periodicity,
S
is assumed to be irreducible and aperiodic throughout the paper. Additionally, we assume the following for
S
:

(1.10)

{\mathbf{E}}[S_{1}]=0,\quad{\rm Var}(S_{1})=1,\quad{\mathbf{E}}[S_{1}^{3}]=0,\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathbf{E}}[|S_{1}|^{4}]<+\infty,

where the assumptions on the higher moments are due to technical reasons (see Remark
1.4
below).

It is known (see
[
Uch11
]
) that for a random walk on
{\mathbbm{Z}}
with mean
0
and finite variance, the induced renewal process
\tau
satisfies
K(n)\sim 1/(\sqrt{2\pi}\hskip 2.0ptn^{3/2})
, which falls within the framework of the marginally relevant pinning model, since
\alpha=\frac{1}{2}
in (
1.4
). Furthermore,
{\mathbf{P}}(S_{n}=0)\sim 1/\sqrt{2\pi n}
by the local limit theorem, and

(1.11)

R_{N}=\sum_{n=1}^{N}{\mathbf{P}}(n\in\tau)^{2}=\sum_{n=1}^{N}{\mathbf{P}}(S_{n}=0)^{2}\sim\frac{\log N}{2\pi}\to\infty,\quad\text{as~}N\to\infty.

The induced pinning model is defined by

(1.12)

\frac{{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{P}}_{N,\beta_{N}}^{\omega}}{{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{P}}}(S):=\frac{1}{Z_{N,\beta_{N}}^{\omega}}\exp\Big(\sum_{n=1}^{N}(\beta_{N}\omega_{n}-\lambda(\beta_{N}))\mathbbm{1}_{\{S_{n}=0\}}\Big),

where the partition function is

(1.13)

Z_{N,\beta_{N}}^{\omega}:={\mathbf{E}}\Big[\exp\Big(\sum_{n=1}^{N}(\beta_{N}\omega_{n}-\lambda(\beta_{N}))\mathbbm{1}_{\{S_{n}=0\}}\Big)\Big].

Note that as in
[
CSZ17
]
, here we set the external field
h\equiv 0
to study the intermediate disorder regime, which is for simplicity and not essential. We are satisfied with this setting and will briefly discuss nonzero choices of
h
later in Remark
2.4
.

Our goal is to study the weak coupling limit of
Z_{N,\beta_{N}}^{\omega}
at the
critical temperature

\beta_{N,c}:=1/\sqrt{R_{N}}\sim\sqrt{2\pi}/\sqrt{\log N}
. Recall that
Z_{N,\beta_{N,c}}^{\omega}
converges in probability to the trivial limit 0 as
N\to\infty
.
The right way to study the weak coupling limit, in the same spirit as
[
CSZ23
]
, is to introduce a random measure related to
Z_{N,\beta_{N,c}}^{\omega}
and consider its scaling limit. To this end, for given starting and end times
M\leq K
, we introduce a point-to-point pinning partition function

(1.14)

Z_{M,K}^{\omega,\beta_{N}}:={\mathbf{E}}\Big[\exp\Big(\sum\limits_{n=M}^{K}(\beta_{N}\omega_{n}-\lambda(\beta_{N}))\mathbbm{1}_{\{S_{n}=0\}}\Big)\mathbbm{1}_{\{S_{K}=0\}}\Big|S_{M}=0\Big].

Note that by translation invariance of the random walk, the partition function is actually well-defined for all integers
M\leq K
. In contrast to the normal pinning partition function counting disorders from time
M+1
, in (
1.14
) we add the disorder at the starting time
M
for computational convenience later on (see (
2.9
)-(
2.10
)). Note that such modification does not have substantial impact on the results.

We can then define a locally finite random measure on the half plane
{\mathbbm{R}}^{2}_{\leq}:=\{(s,t):-\infty<s\leq t<\infty\}
through (
1.14
) as

(1.15)

{\mathcal{Z}}_{N}^{\beta_{N}}({\mathrm{d}}s,{\mathrm{d}}t):=\sqrt{N}Z_{\lfloor sN\rfloor,\lfloor tN\rfloor}^{\omega,\beta_{N}}{\mathrm{d}}s{\mathrm{d}}t,

where the choice of the scaling factor
\sqrt{N}
can be seen from the local limit theorem

(1.16)

\sqrt{N}{\mathbbm{E}}\big[Z_{\lfloor sN\rfloor,\lfloor tN\rfloor}^{\omega,\beta_{N}}\big]=\sqrt{N}{\mathbf{P}}\big(S_{\lfloor tN\rfloor-\lfloor sN\rfloor}\rfloor=0\big)\sim\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi(t-s)}}.

We equip the space of locally finite measures on
{\mathbbm{R}}^{2}_{\leq}
with the topology of vague convergence

(1.17)

\mu_{n}\to\mu\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\int\phi(s,t)\mu_{n}({\mathrm{d}}s,{\mathrm{d}}t)\overset{\rm d}{\longrightarrow}\int\phi(s,t)\mu({\mathrm{d}}s,{\mathrm{d}}t),\quad\forall\phi\in C_{c}({\mathbbm{R}}^{2}_{\leq}).

Before we state our main result, let us stress that beyond the exact critical value
\beta_{N,c}=1/\sqrt{R_{N}}
, there is actually a critical window for
\beta_{N}
that we can study, as given in (
1.21
) below. First, we introduce a rescaled and centered disorder

(1.18)

\zeta_{n}=\zeta_{n}^{(N)}:=e^{\beta_{N}\omega_{n}-\lambda(\beta_{N})}-1.

Note that
(\zeta_{n})_{n\in{\mathbbm{Z}}}
are also i.i.d. and

(1.19)

{\mathbbm{E}}[\zeta_{n}]=0,\quad\sigma_{N}^{2}={\rm Var}(\zeta_{n}):=e^{\lambda(2\beta_{N})-2\lambda(\beta_{N})}-1.

We will frequently use
(\zeta_{n})_{n\in{\mathbbm{Z}}}
in the paper. Then, recall
R_{N}
from (
1.11
) and we choose
\beta_{N}
such that

(1.20)

\sigma_{N}^{2}=e^{\lambda(2\beta_{N})-2\lambda(\beta_{N})}-1=\frac{1}{R_{N}}\Big(1+\frac{\vartheta+o(1)}{\log N}\Big),\quad\text{for some fixed }\vartheta\in{\mathbbm{R}}.

Using
\lambda(\beta)=\sum_{i=2}^{4}\frac{\kappa_{i}}{i!}\beta^{i}+O(\beta^{5})
as
\beta\to 0
, where
\kappa_{i}
is the
i
-th cumulant of
\omega
, a direct computation (see
[
CSZ19a
, Appendix A.4]
and
[
CSZ23
, equation (3.12)]
) yields that

(1.21)

\beta_{N}^{2}=R_{N}^{-1}+C_{1}(\omega)R_{N}^{-3/2}+C_{2}(\omega,\vartheta)R_{N}^{-2}+o(R_{N}^{-2}),

where
C_{1}(\omega)
and
C_{2}(\omega,\vartheta)
are constants depending only on
\kappa_{3}
,
\kappa_{4}
and
\vartheta
. As the parameter
\vartheta
only appears in
C_{2}(\omega,\vartheta)
, contributing to the second order asymptotics, the choice (
1.21
) is thus a
critical window
for
\beta_{N}
. We can now state the main result.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
S
be an irreducible and aperiodic random walk satisfying (
1.10
). Let
\beta_{N}
be in the critical window of temperature with parameter
\vartheta\in{\mathbbm{R}}
, satisfying (
1.20
)-(
1.21
). Then the random measure
{\mathcal{Z}}_{N}^{\beta_{N}}({\mathrm{d}}s,{\mathrm{d}}t)
on
{\mathbbm{R}}^{2}_{\leq}
defined in (
1.15
) converges in distribution to a unique limit
\mathscr{L}^{\vartheta}({\mathrm{d}}s,{\mathrm{d}}t)
. The limit is universal, that is, it does not depend on the law of
\omega
except for the assumption (
1.1
).

Remark 1.2
.

We call the universal limit
\mathscr{L}^{\vartheta}

the critical disordered pinning measure
(CDPM).

Remark 1.3
.

It is straightforward to see that CDPM
\mathscr{L}^{\vartheta}
is translation invariant in law,

(1.22)

\mathscr{L}^{\vartheta}({\mathrm{d}}(s+{\rm a}),{\mathrm{d}}(t+{\rm a}))\overset{{\rm dist}}{=}\mathscr{L}^{\vartheta}({\mathrm{d}}s,{\mathrm{d}}t),\quad\forall\hskip 1.0pt{\rm a}\in\mathbb{R}.

It is a corollary of Theorem
1.6
and Theorem
1.7
that
\mathscr{L}^{\vartheta}
satisfies a scaling relation

(1.23)

\mathscr{L}^{\vartheta}({\mathrm{d}}({\rm a}s),{\mathrm{d}}({\rm a}t))\overset{{\rm dist}}{=}{\rm a}\hskip 1.0pt\mathscr{L}^{\vartheta+\log{\rm a}}({\mathrm{d}}s,{\mathrm{d}}t),\quad\forall\hskip 1.0pt{\rm a}>0.

The above translation and scaling relations are analogous to those satisfied by the critical 2d stochastic heat flow
[
CSZ23
, Theorem 9.2]
, and their proofs are exactly the same, which we leave to the readers.

Remark 1.4
.

While the first and second moments assumptions on
S
in (
1.10
) are virtual, we conjecture that the assumptions of vanishing third moment and finite fourth moment are technical, which are employed to establish a more refined bound, such as (
4.25
)-(
4.27
), and could be eliminated with a more careful analysis.

A general renewal process of the form (
1.4
) can be generated by Bessel random walks, which are a family of one-dimensional nearest-neighbor random walks with inhomogeneous increments (see
[
Ale11
]
for the choice of spatially dependent transition probabilities). For the critical case
\alpha=\frac{1}{2}
with non-constant slowly varying function
L(\cdot)
, the transition probabilities are slightly inhomogeneous

{\mathbf{P}}(S_{n+1}-S_{n}=\pm 1|S_{n}=x)=\frac{1}{2}+o\Big(\frac{1}{x}\Big).

Theorem
1.1
is also expected to hold for such random walks, as long as some necessary inputs, such as relevant local limit theorems, are established.

Remark 1.5
.

For two random walks
S
and
\widetilde{S}
both satisfying (
1.10
), it is not hard to see that the difference of their expected number of overlap
R_{N}-\widetilde{R}_{N}
has a finite limit as
N
tends to
\infty
. Denote the limit
{\rm a}({S,\widetilde{S}}):=\lim_{N\to\infty}(R_{N}-\widetilde{R}_{N})
. Suppose
\mathscr{L}^{\vartheta}({\mathrm{d}}s,{\mathrm{d}}t)
is the limit w.r.t.
S
and a choice of
\beta_{N}
satisfying (
1.20
). Then, for
\widetilde{S}
and the same
\beta_{N}
,

(1.24)

\widetilde{\sigma}_{N}^{2}=\frac{1}{\widetilde{R}_{N}}\Big(1+\frac{\widetilde{R}_{N}-R_{N}}{\log N/(2\pi)}\hskip 1.0pt\frac{\log N/(2\pi)}{R_{N}}\Big)\Big(1+\frac{\vartheta+o(1)}{\log N}\Big)=\frac{1}{\widetilde{R}_{N}}\Big(1+\frac{\vartheta-2\pi{\rm a}({S,\widetilde{S}})+o(1)}{\log N}\Big),

and therefore the limit w.r.t.
\widetilde{S}
and
\beta_{N}
is
\mathscr{L}^{\vartheta-2\pi{\rm a}({S,\widetilde{S}})}({\mathrm{d}}s,{\mathrm{d}}t)
.

To show the convergence of
{\mathcal{Z}}_{N}^{\beta_{N}}({\mathrm{d}}s,{\mathrm{d}}t)
to the CDPM
\mathscr{L}^{\vartheta}({\mathrm{d}}s,{\mathrm{d}}t)
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\leq}
, by the translation and scaling relations (
1.22
)-(
1.23
), it suffices to show the convergence in a restricted domain
[0,1]^{2}_{\leq}:=\{(s,t):0\leq s\leq t\leq 1\}
. For the latter convergence, we prove it in two steps, as detailed below.

In the first step, our objective is to establish Theorem
1.6
below, which ensures that the convergence of random measures defined through the pinning model can be reduced to the convergence of random measures defined through a variant of
directed polymer model
(for more information about the directed polymer model, see
[
Com17
]
). Specifically, we recall the partition function
Z_{M,K}^{\omega,\beta_{N}}
from (
1.14
) and the random measure
{\mathcal{Z}}_{N}^{\beta_{N}}({\mathrm{d}}s,{\mathrm{d}}t)
from (
1.15
). We introduce the companion point-to-point directed polymer partition function without boundary disorders

(1.25)

\widetilde{Z}_{M,K}^{\omega,\beta_{N}}(x,y):={\mathbf{E}}\Big[\exp\Big(\sum\limits_{n=M+1}^{K-1}(\beta_{N}\omega_{n}-\lambda(\beta_{N}))\mathbbm{1}_{\{S_{n}=0\}}\Big)\mathbbm{1}_{\{S_{K}=y\}}\Big|S_{M}=x\Big]

and its related random measure

(1.26)

\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{\lfloor sN\rfloor,\lfloor tN\rfloor}^{\beta_{N}}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}y):=\sqrt{N}\widetilde{Z}_{\lfloor sN\rfloor,\lfloor tN\rfloor}^{\omega,\beta_{N}}\Big(\lfloor x\sqrt{N}\rfloor,\lfloor y\sqrt{N}\rfloor\Big){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}y,

where the disorder of the directed polymer is only located on the line
{\mathbbm{Z}}\times\{0\}
.
Similar to (
1.17
), we regard
\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{\lfloor sN\rfloor,\lfloor tN\rfloor}^{\beta_{N}}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}y)
as a random variable taking values in the space of locally finite measures on
{\mathbbm{R}}\times{\mathbbm{R}}
equipped with the topology of vague convergence.

Theorem 1.6
.

Let
S
and
\beta_{N}
be the same as in Theorem
1.1
. The following are equivalent.

(i)

The disordered pinning measure
{\mathcal{Z}}_{N}^{\beta_{N}}({\mathrm{d}}s,{\mathrm{d}}t)
on
[0,1]^{2}_{\leq}
defined in (
1.15
) converges weakly to the unique CDPM
\mathscr{L}^{\vartheta}({\mathrm{d}}s,{\mathrm{d}}t)
on
[0,1]^{2}_{\leq}
.

(ii)

The directed polymer measure
\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{0,N}^{\beta_{N}}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}y)
on
{\mathbbm{R}}\times{\mathbbm{R}}
defined in (
1.26
) converges weakly to a unique limiting random measure
\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}^{\vartheta}_{0,1}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}y)
on
{\mathbbm{R}}\times{\mathbbm{R}}
.

Moreover,
\mathscr{L}^{\vartheta}({\mathrm{d}}s,{\mathrm{d}}t)
and
\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}^{\vartheta}_{0,1}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}y)
satisfies the following relationship:

(1.27)

\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}^{\vartheta}_{0,1}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}y)=\overline{Q}(x,y){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}y+\iint_{0<s<t<1}Q(x,s)\mathscr{L}^{\vartheta}({\mathrm{d}}s,{\mathrm{d}}t)Q(-y,1-t){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}y,

where
\overline{Q}(x,y)
is the probability density at time-space
(1,y)
of a Brownian motion which starts at time-space (0,x) and does not hit
0
in the time interval
(0,1)
,
and
Q(x,s)=\frac{|x|}{\sqrt{2\pi}s^{3/2}}e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2s}}
is the probability density that a Brownian motion, which starts at time-space
(0,x)
, first hits
0
at time
s
.

With Theorem
1.6
in hand, the second step to conclude Theorem
1.1
is to show the theorem below.

Theorem 1.7
.

Let
S
and
\beta_{N}
be as specified in Theorem
1.1
. The random measure
\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{0,N}^{\beta_{N}}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}y)
, defined in (
1.26
), converges in distribution to a unique limit
\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{0,1}^{\vartheta}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}y)
. The limit is universal, that is, it dose not depend on the law of
\omega
except for the assumption (
1.1
).

Remark 1.8
.

As shown in
[
CSZ23
, Theorem 1.1]
, the above convergence can be extended to convergence in finite dimensional distributions of
(\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{\lfloor sN\rfloor,\lfloor tN\rfloor}^{\beta_{N}})_{0\leq s<t<\infty}
to
(\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}^{\vartheta}_{s,t})_{0\leq s<t<\infty}
.

Remark 1.9
.

The limiting measures
\mathscr{L}({\mathrm{d}}s,{\mathrm{d}}t)
and
\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{s,t}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}y)
corresponds to the
point-to-point
partition function. The original pinning model (
1.12
) and directed polymer (
1.25
), as well as the solutions (
1.35
) of stochastic Volterra equations and stochastic heat equations to be discussed in the next subsection, are given in terms of
point-to-line
partition function. It is not hard to see that the scaling limits of these point-to-line (and similarly, line-to-point) partition functions can be obtained by integrating out one relevant coordinate in
\mathscr{L}({\mathrm{d}}s,{\mathrm{d}}t)
or
\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{s,t}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}y)
.

The advantage of working with the directed polymer measure
\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{0,N}^{\beta_{N}}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}y)
in Theorem
1.7
, rather than the disordered pinning measure
{\mathcal{Z}}_{0,N}^{\beta_{N}}({\mathrm{d}}s,{\mathrm{d}}t)
in Theorem
1.1
, lies in the introduction of spatial information, resulting in a Markovian structure. Our proof of random measure convergence involves a high moment computation, achieved by coupling multiple polymer chains in the same random environment and then integrating out the environment. Since the random environment is concentrated on the spatial point 0, it is convenient to decompose the polymer chains at each renewal time that anyone of the polymers hits 0. Keeping track of the spatial positions of all polymers, unlike the renewal processes in the pinning model, gives rise to a Markov chain.

1.3.
Stochastic Volterra equation and stochastic heat equation

The stochastic Volterra equation has recently attracted vast attention from mathematical finance community. It serves as a
rough volatility model
, capturing certain key features of short time behavior for asset pricing, see Bayer et. al.
[
BFG+20
]
and the references therein. For a smooth function
f
, a parameter
H\in[0,1/2)
, a Brownian motion
W_{\cdot}
and a random initial condition
\eta
independent of
W_{\cdot}
, the Volterra equation is defined by

(1.28)

Y_{t}=\eta+\int_{0}^{t}\frac{f(Y_{s})}{(t-s)^{\frac{1}{2}-H}}{\mathrm{d}}W_{s},

which is deemed rough due to the presence of the singular kernel
(t-s)^{H-\frac{1}{2}}
. In practice, there is evidence
[
NR18
]
suggesting that the volatility aligns well with (
1.28
) for
H
close to 0.

For constant function
f(x)\equiv c
and
H>0
, (
1.28
) is the additive Volterra equation, and its solution
Y^{H}
is the Riemann-Liouville fractional Brownian motion with Hurst index
H
. Letting
H\downarrow 0
,
[
NR18
]
showed that a sequence of suitably rescaled
Y^{H}
converges to a log-correlated Gaussian measure
Y^{0}
, and thus
Y^{0}
can be viewed as the solution of additive Volterra equation with zero Hurst index.

For
f(x)=cx
, we obtain the
stochastic Volterra equation
(SVE) in a multiplicative form

(1.29)

Y_{t}=\eta+\frac{\beta}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{Y_{s}}{(t-s)^{\frac{1}{2}-H}}{\mathrm{d}}W_{s},

where we replaced the constant
c
by
\beta/\sqrt{2\pi}
for later use. For
H\in[0,\frac{1}{2})
, the SVE (
1.29
) has no classic solution, as can be seen by a calculation of regularity of the terms in the integral
[
BFG+20
]
. In particular, for
H\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
, the equation falls within the so-called
subcritical
domain, extensively explored in previous literature. In this regime, the solution has been defined in
[
BFG+20
]
using regularity structures. Parallelly, the subcritical SVE has also been studied in
[
Com20
]
via rough paths for
H\in(\frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{2})
, and in
[
PT21
]
based on paracontrolled distributions for
H\in(\frac{1}{3},\frac{1}{2})
. However, for the
critical
SVE where
H=0
, a rigorous interpretation of the equation (
1.29
) remains open. Below, we will focus on the critical SVE and attempt to address this open question.

For
H=0
, the SVE is closely related to the
stochastic heat equation
(SHE), which we now illustrate. Consider the following (formal) SHE with multiplicative noise that is white in time and delta in space,

(1.30)

\partial_{t}u(x,t)=\frac{1}{2}\Delta u(x,t)+\beta u(x,t)\cdot\delta_{0}(x)\dot{W}_{t},\quad u(x,0)=\eta(x),\quad(x,t)\in{\mathbbm{R}}\times{\mathbbm{R}}^{+},

where
\delta_{0}
is the delta function in the space coordinate,
\dot{W}_{t}
is the one-dimensional white noise indexed by time (i.e.,
W_{t}
is a one-dimensional standard Brownian motion), and the initial condition
\eta(x)
is a random function independent of
\dot{W}_{t}
. Utilizing the heat kernel

(1.31)

g_{t}(x):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi t}}e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2t}}\quad\text{with}\quad g_{0}(0):=1,

we can rewrite (
1.30
) in the following integral form

(1.32)

u(t,x)=\int_{\mathbbm{R}}g_{t}(x-y)\eta(x){\mathrm{d}}y+\beta\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbbm{R}}g_{t-s}(x-y)u(s,y)\delta_{0}(y)\dot{W}_{s}{\mathrm{d}}y{\mathrm{d}}s.

We can then obtain (
1.29
) by the following. Setting
\eta(x)\equiv\eta
, the first integral simplifies to
\eta
. Integrating over
y
in the second integral, we see that
Y_{t}=u(t,0)
solves the SVE (
1.29
) with
H=0
.

Nevertheless, with the random noise term in SHE, the solution
u
should be a distribution, and thus the equations (
1.30
) and (
1.32
) are ill-defined due to the product of two distributions
u(t,x)
and
\delta_{0}(x)\dot{W}_{t}
. To make sense of such critical SHE, we introduce the following mollifying procedure. Let

(1.33)

\rho^{\delta}(x):=\frac{1}{\delta}\rho\Big(\frac{x}{\delta}\Big),\quad x\in{\mathbbm{R}},

with
\rho(\cdot)
a smooth even probability density on
{\mathbbm{R}}
with compact support. The mollified equation

(1.34)

\begin{split}\partial_{t}u^{\delta}(t,x)=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{xx}u^{\delta}(t,x)+\beta u^{\delta}(t,x)\rho^{\delta}(x)\dot{W}_{t},\quad u^{\delta}(0,x)=\eta(x)\end{split}

is well-defined. Using the time-reversed noise
\dot{\widetilde{W}}_{t}:=\dot{W}_{1-t}
, the solution
(u^{\delta}(t,x))_{t\in[0,1],x\in{\mathbbm{R}}}
admits a generalised Feynman-Kac representation (see
[
BC95
,
CSZ17
]
) with

(1.35)

u^{\delta}(t,x)={\mathbf{E}}^{1-t,x}\Big[\eta(B_{1})\exp\Big(\beta\int_{1-t}^{1}\rho^{\delta}(B_{s}){\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{W}_{s}-\frac{1}{2}\beta^{2}\int_{1-t}^{1}(\rho^{\delta}(B_{s}))^{2}{\mathrm{d}}s\Big)\Big],

where
{\mathbf{E}}^{1-t,x}
denotes the expectation for a Brownian motion
B_{\cdot}
independent of
\widetilde{W}_{\cdot}
, starting at
x
at time
1-t
. The right-hand side of (
1.35
) is a natural continuous
point-to-plane
analogue of the directed polymer (
1.25
). Thereby as
\delta\downarrow 0
, a result similar to Theorem
1.7
is expected to hold.

The precise result is stated as follows. We only treat the marginal distribution at
t=1
(and the case for general
t
follows from Remark
1.8
). With a slight abuse of notation, denote
{\mathbf{E}}^{0,x}
and the associated probability
{\mathbf{P}}^{0,x}
by
{\mathbf{E}}^{x}
and
{\mathbf{P}}^{x}
, respectively. By a change of variables
(s,y)=(\delta^{2}\widetilde{s},\delta\widetilde{y})
and by the invariance of diffusive scaling for Brownian motion,
u^{\delta}(1,x)
is equal in distribution to the following integral,

(1.36)

\begin{split}&{\mathbf{E}}^{\frac{x}{\delta}}\Big[\eta(B_{1})\exp\Big(\beta\int_{0}^{\delta^{-2}}\rho(B_{\widetilde{s}}){\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{W}_{\widetilde{s}}-\frac{1}{2}\beta^{2}\int_{0}^{\delta^{-2}}(\rho(B_{\widetilde{s}}))^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{s}\Big)\Big]\\
=&\int_{y\in{\mathbbm{R}}}\eta(y){\mathbf{E}}^{\frac{x}{\delta}}\Big[\exp\Big(\beta\int_{0}^{\delta^{-2}}\!\!\!\rho(B_{\widetilde{s}}){\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{W}_{\widetilde{s}}-\frac{1}{2}\beta^{2}\int_{0}^{\delta^{-2}}\!\!\!(\rho(B_{\widetilde{s}}))^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{s}\Big)\Big|B_{\delta^{-2}}=\frac{y}{\delta}\Big]{\mathbf{P}}^{\frac{x}{\delta}}\Big(B_{\delta^{-2}}\in\frac{{\mathrm{d}}y}{\delta}\Big).\end{split}

In the following, we write
s=\widetilde{s}
and
W=\widetilde{W}
to lighten the notations. If we pin the Brownian motion at
y/\delta
at the terminal time
\delta^{-2}
, then we can define a rescaled continuous analogue of
\widetilde{{\mathcal{Z}}}_{0,N}^{\beta_{N}}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}y)
in (
1.26
) by

(1.37)

\widetilde{u}_{0,1}^{\delta}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}y):={\mathbf{E}}^{\frac{x}{\delta}}\Big[\exp\Big(\beta_{\delta}\int_{0}^{\delta^{-2}}\hskip-8.0pt\rho(B_{s}){\mathrm{d}}W_{s}-\frac{1}{2}\beta_{\delta}^{2}\int_{0}^{\delta^{-2}}\hskip-8.0pt(\rho(B_{s}))^{2}{\mathrm{d}}s\Big)\Big|B_{\delta^{-2}}=\frac{y}{\delta}\Big]\hskip 1.0pt{\mathbf{P}}^{x}(B_{1}\in{\mathrm{d}}y){\mathrm{d}}x,

where we also set
\beta_{\delta}\downarrow 0
as
\delta\to 0
such that

(1.38)

\beta_{\delta}^{2}:=\frac{2\pi}{\log\delta^{-2}}+\frac{\theta+o(1)}{(\log\delta^{-2})^{2}},\quad\text{for some fixed}~\theta\in{\mathbbm{R}}.

We have the following result.

Theorem 1.10
.

The random measure
\widetilde{u}_{0,1}^{\delta}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}y)
, defined as in (
1.37
), converges in distribution to the unique limit
\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{0,1}^{\vartheta}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}y)
defined in Theorem
1.6
, where with the mollifier
\rho(\cdot)
given in (
1.33
),

(1.39)

\vartheta=\log 2-\gamma+\idotsint_{{\mathbbm{R}}^{4}}\rho(x^{\prime})\rho(y^{\prime})\log\Big[\frac{1}{(x-x^{\prime})^{2}+(y-y^{\prime})^{2}}\Big]\rho(x)\rho(y){\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}y^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}y+\frac{\theta}{2\pi}\hskip 1.0pt.

Remark 1.11
.

In the proof of Theorem
1.10
, we will actually show that for
\varphi\in C_{c}({\mathbbm{R}})
and
\psi\in C_{b}({\mathbbm{R}})
,
\iint_{{\mathbbm{R}}^{2}}\varphi(x)u_{0,1}^{\delta}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}y)\psi(y)
converges weakly to
\iint_{{\mathbbm{R}}^{2}}\varphi(x)\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{0,1}^{\vartheta}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}y)\psi(y)
as
\delta\to 0
.

As in (
1.37
), for
0\leq s<t\leq 1
, we can also define

(1.40)

\widetilde{u}_{s,t}^{\delta}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}y):={\mathbf{E}}^{\frac{s}{\delta^{2}},\frac{x}{\delta}}\Big[\exp\Big(\beta_{\delta}\int_{s\delta^{-2}}^{t\delta^{-2}}\hskip-8.0pt\rho(B_{s}){\mathrm{d}}W_{s}-\frac{1}{2}\beta_{\delta}^{2}\int_{s\delta^{-2}}^{t\delta^{-2}}\hskip-8.0pt(\rho(B_{s}))^{2}{\mathrm{d}}s\Big)\Big|B_{t\delta^{-2}}=\frac{y}{\delta}\Big]\hskip 1.0pt{\mathbf{P}}^{s,x}(B_{t}\in{\mathrm{d}}y){\mathrm{d}}x.

By Remark
1.8
, we have that
(\widetilde{u}^{\delta}_{s,t})_{0\leq s<t\leq 1}
converges in finite dimensional distributions to
(\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{s,t}^{\vartheta})_{0\leq s<t\leq 1}
. Note that by (
1.35
)-(
1.36
), the solution
u^{\delta}(t,x)
of the mollified SHE (
1.34
) satisfies the relation

u^{\delta}(t,x){\mathrm{d}}x=\widetilde{u}^{\delta}_{1-t,1}({\mathrm{d}}x,\eta):=\int_{y\in{\mathbbm{R}}}\widetilde{u}^{\delta}_{1-t,1}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}y)\eta(y)
. Therefore, in view of Theorem
1.10
and Remark
1.11
, if the initial random function
\eta(x)
is bounded and continuous, then the critical singular SHE (
1.30
) has a natural candidate solution
{\mathcal{U}}(t,{\mathrm{d}}x)
given by

(1.41)

{\mathcal{U}}(t,{\mathrm{d}}x)=\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{1-t,1}^{\vartheta}({\mathrm{d}}x,\eta):=\int_{y\in{\mathbbm{R}}}\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{1-t,1}^{\vartheta}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}y)\eta(y).

We then deal with the critical singular SVE (
1.29
) with the help of (
1.35
)-(
1.37
). Since
\eta
is independent of
B
and
W
, we assume without loss of generality that
\eta\equiv 1
. Decomposing
\widetilde{u}_{0,1}^{\delta}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}y)
according to the first time in
(0,1)
that the Brownian motion
B
visits
0
, we have that

(1.42)

\widetilde{u}_{0,1}^{\delta}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}y)=\overline{Q}(x,y){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}y+\int_{0<t<1}\Big(Q(x,t)\widetilde{u}_{t,1}^{\delta}(0,{\mathrm{d}}y){\mathrm{d}}x\Big){\mathrm{d}}t,

where the probability densities
\overline{Q}
and
Q
are defined in (
1.27
) and note that the measure
\widetilde{u}_{t,1}^{\delta}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}y)
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure, and hence
\widetilde{u}_{t,1}^{\delta}(x,{\mathrm{d}}y):=\widetilde{u}_{t,1}^{\delta}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}y)/{\mathrm{d}}x
is a well-defined function of
x
.

Integrating
y
over
{\mathbbm{R}}
in (
1.42
), we obtain

(1.43)

\int_{y\in{\mathbbm{R}}}\widetilde{u}_{0,1}^{\delta}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}y)=\int_{y\in{\mathbbm{R}}}\overline{Q}(x,y){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}y+\int_{0<t<1}\Big[Q(x,t)\Big(\int_{y\in{\mathbbm{R}}}\widetilde{u}_{t,1}^{\delta}(0,{\mathrm{d}}y)\Big){\mathrm{d}}x\Big]{\mathrm{d}}t.

Recall (
1.32
) and note that
Y^{\delta}_{t}:=u^{\delta}(t,0)=\int_{y\in{\mathbbm{R}}}\widetilde{u}_{t,1}^{\delta}(0,{\mathrm{d}}y)
can be viewed as the solution of mollified SVE. Therefore, by Theorem
1.10
, as
\delta\to 0
, (
1.43
) converges in distribution to

(1.44)

\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{0,1}^{\vartheta}({\mathrm{d}}x,\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbbm{R}})=\int_{y\in{\mathbbm{R}}}\overline{Q}(x,y){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}y+\int_{0<t<1}Q(x,t){\mathcal{Y}}({\mathrm{d}}t){\mathrm{d}}x,

where the random measure
{\mathcal{Y}}({\mathrm{d}}t)
is the weak limit of
{\mathcal{Y}}^{\delta}({\mathrm{d}}t):=Y_{t}^{\delta}{\mathrm{d}}t=u^{\delta}(t,0){\mathrm{d}}t
. We can thus conclude that a natural candidate solution of the critical singular SVE (
1.29
) is
{\mathcal{Y}}({\mathrm{d}}t)
. Moreover, by comparing (
1.44
) with (
1.27
), we have that

(1.45)

{\mathcal{Y}}({\mathrm{d}}t)=\int_{t<s<1}\int_{y\in\mathbb{R}}Q(-y,1-s){\mathrm{d}}y\mathscr{L}^{\vartheta}({\mathrm{d}}t,{\mathrm{d}}s)=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\int_{t<s<1}s^{-1/2}\mathscr{L}^{\vartheta}({\mathrm{d}}t,{\mathrm{d}}s).

We summarize the above argument as the following corollary.

Corollary 1.12
.

Let
\beta_{\delta}
be specified as in (
1.38
) and
u^{\delta}(t,x)
be the solution of mollified SHE (
1.34
). Then
u^{\delta}(t,x){\mathrm{d}}x
converges in distribution to the random measure
{\mathcal{U}}(t,{\mathrm{d}}x)
, defined in (
1.41
), which can be viewed as a solution of SHE (
1.30
). Meanwhile,
u^{\delta}(t,0){\mathrm{d}}t
converges in distribution to a random measure
{\mathcal{Y}}({\mathrm{d}}t)
, given in (
), which can be viewed as a solution of SVE (
1.29
) with critical Hurst index
H=0
.

1.4.
Contributions and discussions

An effectively studied marginally relevant disordered model at criticality is the directed polymer as presented in the seminal work
[
CSZ23
]
. It proves that for the two-dimensional directed polymer with temperature in the critical window, the family of polymer measures (similar to (
1.26
)), indexed by starting and end times, converges in finite dimensional distributions to the critical 2d stochastic heat flow. The same convergence is also believed to hold for the critical 2d SHE, the continuous analogue of the directed polymer, although
[
CSZ23
]
does not provide proof details. This outcome offers an interpretation of the solution to a critical singular SPDE for the first time. The proof therein is robust enough, enabling us to adapt it for Theorem
1.7
. Using the same framework, we provide a complete proof of Theorem
1.10
as well, which, in turn, complements the case of SHE.
Besides the technical challenges needed to adapt the mathematical framework of
[
CSZ23
]
to the current setting, the interest of our paper lies in the establish of CDPM for the disordered pinning model, and in the applications to key problems in SPDEs and mathematical finance, which we highlight as follows

•

Firstly, the limiting CDPM of the marginally relevant pinning model at the critical temperature is identified for the first time. For the pinning model (
1.2
), when the disorder is relevant, a scaling limit of the pinning partition function was obtained in
[
CSZ16
]
; when the disorder is marginally relevant and the temperature is in the so-called subcritical regime, a scaling limit of the pinning partition function was established in
[
CSZ17
]
. However, to the best of our knowledge, for marginally relevant pinning model at criticality, how to obtain a scaling limit was unknown in both physics and mathematics literature.

•

Secondly, we give, for the first time, a meaning to the solution of the multiplicative SVE (
1.29
) with critical index
H=0
, in contrast to the rough volatility modelled by additive SVE with
H=0
as studied in
[
NR18
]
. By connecting with SHE, we define a solution to such singular models of SDEs, thereby extending their scope from the subcritical phase to the critical phase.

•

Thirdly, in Theorem
1.6
we establish the equivalence of convergence between random measures on time coordinate and on spatial coordinate, which allows us to use the techniques developed in
[
CSZ23
]
to deduce Theorem
1.1
by proving Theorem
1.7
. In particular, as in (
2.9
)-(
2.10
), we show that the measures on time coordinate and on spatial coordinate can be expressed as weighted averages of each other, where the weights are probability densities for the first hitting times of a Brownian motion, which might be of independent interests. Although the equivalence seems intuitively natural, it turns out that the proof is highly non-trivial, crucially relying on the expressions of transition kernel related to the Brownian motion, see, e.g., (
3.15
).

•

Fourthly, the paper can be read as a complement to
[
CSZ23
]
. Some proofs that were sketched in
[
CSZ23
]
are now provided in full detail, such as the moment computations of the coarse-grained model in Section
7
. We also provide enough details in proving Theorem
1.10
, the case of SHE, demonstrating the robustness of techniques in
[
CSZ23
]
under continuous settings.

In the next section, we will illustrate our proof strategy for Theorem
1.6
.
For Theorems
1.7
and
1.10
, we will first review the strategies in
[
CSZ23
]
, and then discuss the adaption to our case. Along the way, we will also introduce some preliminary ingredients that will be moreover used throughout the paper. At the end of that section, we will state the organization of the rest of the paper.

2.
Proof Outlines, Notations and Tools

Note that Theorem
1.1
is a direct consequence of Theorem
1.6
and Theorem
1.7
. Theorem
1.7
and Theorem
1.10
are proved in the same framework as developed in
[
CSZ23
]
. We thereby only focus on explaining the proofs for Theorem
1.6
and Theorem
1.7
. Before illustrating the proof for Theorem
1.7
, we will first review the framework in
[
CSZ23
]
, so that it is more straightforward to explain the modifications needed when adapting. We will also introduce some necessary notations and tools, including polynomial chaos expansion, the Dickman subordinator, coarse-grained disorders and a coarse-grained model.

2.1.
Proof outline for Theorem
1.6

We need to show that the random pinning measure
{\mathcal{Z}}_{N}^{\beta_{N}}({\mathrm{d}}s,{\mathrm{d}}t)
(resp. the random polymer measure
\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{0,N}^{\beta_{N}}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}y)
) has a unique limit
\mathscr{L}^{\vartheta}({\mathrm{d}}s,{\mathrm{d}}t)
(resp.
\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}^{\vartheta}_{0,1}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}y)
). Thanks to
[
Kal17
, Corollary 4.14]
, it suffices to apply test functions
h\in C_{c}([0,1]^{2}_{\leq})
(resp.
\phi\in C_{c}({\mathbbm{R}}^{2})
) w.r.t. the random measures
{\mathcal{Z}}_{N}^{\beta_{N}}({\mathrm{d}}s,{\mathrm{d}}t)
(resp.
\widetilde{{\mathcal{Z}}}_{0,N}^{\beta_{N}}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}y)
), and show that the resulting sequence of random variables has a weak limit. In particular, to prove Theorem
1.6
, we only need to consider test functions of the form
f(s)g(t)
with
f,g\in C_{c}([0,1])
(resp.
\varphi(x)\psi(y)
with
\varphi,\psi\in C_{c}({\mathbbm{R}})
) by a density argument, and show that the following two statements imply each other:

(2.1)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Z}}_{N}^{\beta_{N}}(f,g)

\displaystyle:=\iint_{[0,1]^{2}_{\leq}}f(s){\mathcal{Z}}_{N}^{\beta_{N}}({\mathrm{d}}s,{\mathrm{d}}t)g(t)\quad\text{converges weakly for all}~f,g\in C_{c}([0,1]);

(2.2)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{0,N}^{\beta_{N}}(\varphi,\psi)

\displaystyle:=\iint_{{\mathbbm{R}}^{2}}\varphi(x)\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{0,N}^{\beta_{N}}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}y)\psi(y)\quad\text{converges weakly for all}~\varphi,\psi\in C_{c}({\mathbbm{R}}).

A key step is to compare decompositions of
{\mathcal{Z}}_{N}^{\beta_{N}}(f,g)
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{0,N}^{\beta_{N}}(\varphi,\psi)
.

We first decompose
\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{0,N}^{\beta_{N}}(\varphi,\psi)
according to the first and last hitting times to
0
for the random walk in the time interval
[1,N-1]
. Let the first and last hitting times be

(2.3)

\displaystyle\tau_{1}

\displaystyle:=\inf\{n\geq 1:S_{n}=0~\text{and}~S_{k}\neq 0~\text{for}~k=1,\cdots,n-1\},

(2.4)

\displaystyle\tau_{2}

\displaystyle:=\tau_{2}^{(N)}=\sup\{n\leq N-1:S_{n}=0~\text{and}~S_{k}\neq 0~\text{for}~k=n+1,\cdots,N-1\}.

Recall
\widetilde{Z}_{0,N}^{\omega,\beta_{N}}(x,y)
from (
1.25
), and we have that

(2.5)

\widetilde{Z}_{0,N}^{\omega,\beta_{N}}(x,y)=\widetilde{Z}_{0,N}^{\omega,\beta}(x,y)\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{1}\geq N\}}\right]+\sum\limits_{m=1}^{N-1}\sum\limits_{n=m}^{N-1}\widetilde{Z}_{0,N}^{\omega,\beta_{N}}(x,y)\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{1}=m,\tau_{2}=n\}}\right],

where

\widetilde{Z}_{0,N}^{\omega,\beta_{N}}(x,y)\left[\mathbbm{1}_{A}\right]:={\mathbf{E}}^{x}\Big[\exp\Big(\sum\limits_{n=1}^{N-1}(\beta\omega_{n}-\lambda(\beta))\mathbbm{1}_{\{S_{n}=0\}}\Big)\mathbbm{1}_{\{S_{N}=y\}}\mathbbm{1}_{A}\Big].

It is straightforward that

(2.6)

\widetilde{Z}_{0,N}^{\omega,\beta}(x,y)\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{1}\geq N\}}\right]={\mathbf{P}}^{x}(S_{N}=y,\tau_{1}\geq N),

and due to the absence of the disorders at times
0
and
N
,

(2.7)

\widetilde{Z}_{0,N}^{\omega,\beta}(x,y)\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{1}=m,\tau_{2}=n\}}\right]={\mathbf{P}}^{x}(\tau_{1}=m)\times Z^{\omega,\beta_{N}}_{m,n}\times{\mathbf{P}}^{x}(\tau_{2}=n,S_{N}=y|S_{n}=0),

where
Z^{\omega,\beta_{N}}_{m,n}
is the pinning partition function defined in (
1.14
).

We introduce

(2.8)

q_{x}(n):={\mathbf{P}}^{x}(\tau_{1}=n)

to denote the probability of the first hitting time to
0
for a random walk starting at
x
. Note that by the Markov property and a time reversal, we have that

{\mathbf{P}}^{x}(S_{N}=y,\tau_{2}=n|S_{n}=0)={\mathbf{P}}^{-y}(\tau_{1}=N-n)=q_{-y}(N-n).

Plugging (
2.6
) and (
2.7
) into (
2.5
), and recalling
\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{0,N}^{\beta_{N}}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}y)
from (
1.26
), we obtain

(2.9)

\begin{split}&\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{0,N}^{\beta_{N}}(\varphi,\psi)=\iint_{{\mathbbm{R}}\times{\mathbbm{R}}}\varphi(x)\sqrt{N}\hskip 1.0pt{\mathbf{P}}^{x_{N}}(S_{N}=y_{N},\tau_{1}\geq N)\psi(y){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}y\\
+\sum\limits_{m=1}^{N-1}\sum\limits_{n=m}^{N-1}&\Big(\int_{{\mathbbm{R}}}\varphi(x)\sqrt{N}q_{x_{N}}(m){\mathrm{d}}x\Big)\Big(\sqrt{N}Z^{\omega,\beta_{N}}_{m,n}\Big)\Big(\int_{{\mathbbm{R}}}\psi(x)\sqrt{N}q_{-y_{N}}(N-n){\mathrm{d}}y\Big),\end{split}

where
x_{N}:=\lfloor x\sqrt{N}\rfloor
and
y_{N}:=\lfloor y\sqrt{N}\rfloor
.

Similarly, we have that for integral with respect to the pinning measure,

(2.10)

{\mathcal{Z}}_{N}^{\beta_{N}}(f,g)=\sum\limits_{m=0}^{N-1}\sum\limits_{n=m}^{N-1}\Big(\int_{\frac{m}{N}}^{\frac{m+1}{N}}f(s){\mathrm{d}}s\Big)\Big(\sqrt{N}Z_{m,n}^{\omega,\beta_{N}}\Big)\Big(\int_{\frac{n}{N}}^{\frac{n+1}{N}}g(t){\mathrm{d}}t\Big).

Note that the first term at the right-hand side of (
2.9
) is deterministic. To show (i)
\Longleftrightarrow
(ii) in Theorem
1.7
, it suffices to show that (a) the deterministic term converges; (b) the term
m=0
in (
2.10
) is negligible; (c) the two double-sums with
1\leq m\leq n\leq N-1
in (
2.10
) and (
2.9
) can be made arbitrarily close by choosing suitable
f,g,\varphi,\psi
. While (a) and (b) are somewhat direct to check, (c) is verified through a crucial observation (
3.15
) regarding the heat kernel, that allows approximations between test functions
f,g
and
\varphi,\psi
.

2.2.
Proof strategy in
[
CSZ23
]

In
[
CSZ23
]
, the authors treated the classic directed polymer model, whose point-to-point partition function is defined by

\overline{Z}_{M,K}^{\omega,\beta_{N}}(x,y):={\mathbf{E}}\Big[\exp\Big(\sum\limits_{n=M+1}^{K-1}(\beta_{N}\omega_{n,S_{n}}-\lambda(\beta_{N}))\Big)\mathbbm{1}_{\{S_{K}=y\}}\Big|S_{M}=x\Big],

where the disorders
\omega_{n,x}
are located on the whole time-space lattice
{\mathbbm{N}}\times{\mathbbm{Z}}^{2}
. The related random measure is defined similarly to (
1.26
), by

\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}}_{\lfloor sN\rfloor,\lfloor tN\rfloor}^{\beta_{N}}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}y):=\frac{N}{4}\overline{Z}_{\lfloor sN\rfloor,\lfloor tN\rfloor}^{\omega,\beta_{N}}\Big(\lfloor x\sqrt{N}\rfloor,\lfloor y\sqrt{N}\rfloor\Big){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}y.

Given test functions
\varphi,\psi\in C_{c}({\mathbbm{R}}^{2})
, their integral w.r.t.
\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}}_{0,N}^{\beta_{N}}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}y)
is denoted by
\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}}_{N}=\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}}_{N}^{\beta_{N}}(\varphi,\psi):=\iint_{{\mathbbm{R}}^{4}}\varphi(x)\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}}_{0,N}^{\beta_{N}}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}y)\psi(y)
. To show the weak convergence of
\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}}_{N}
, it suffices to show that for any
f\in C_{b}({\mathbbm{R}})
,
{\mathbbm{E}}[f(\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}}_{N})]
is a Cauchy sequence. The difficulty here is that, for different
N,M
,
\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}}_{N}
and
\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}}_{M}
have different
\beta_{N}
and
\beta_{M}
and different polymer lengths
N
and
M
, which makes the comparison between
{\mathbbm{E}}[f(\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}}_{N})]
and
{\mathbbm{E}}[f(\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}}_{M})]
very intractable.

To proceed, the first step is to perform a
polynomial chaos expansion
(see Subsection
2.4
) on
\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}}_{N}
, so that it can be expressed by a multilinear function of rescaled and centered disorders. Each term in the multilinear function is a product of some disorders and the random walk transition kernels connecting the time-space indices of the disorders. If we partition the time-space by boxes and observe the multilinear function in “box-level”, that is, we only concern the boxes instead of the concrete time-space coordinates visited by the random walk, then
{\mathcal{Z}}_{N}
exhibits some self-similarity.

Inspired by this, for fixed
\varepsilon>0
, we can introduce
mesoscopic boxes
by

(2.11)

{\mathcal{B}}_{N,\varepsilon}(\mathrm{i},\mathrm{a}):=\big((\mathrm{i}-1)\varepsilon N,\mathrm{i}\varepsilon N\big]\times\big((\mathrm{a}-(1,1))\sqrt{\varepsilon N},\mathrm{a}\sqrt{\varepsilon N}\big]:={\mathcal{T}}_{N,\varepsilon}(\mathrm{i})\times{\mathcal{S}}_{N,\varepsilon}(\mathrm{a}),\quad\mathrm{i}\in{\mathbbm{N}},\mathrm{a}\in{\mathbbm{Z}}^{2},

where
{\mathcal{T}}_{N,\varepsilon}(\mathrm{i})
is a mesoscopic time interval and
{\mathcal{S}}_{N,\varepsilon}(\mathrm{a})
is a mesoscopic spatial box. The advantage of introducing a mesoscopic scale is that, the mesoscopic length of the polymer is now fixed by
\frac{1}{\varepsilon}
for all
N
and many estimates only depend on
\varepsilon
uniformly in
N
large enough.

Furthermore, it can be shown that the paths with consecutive short visits in time or large deviations in space have negligible contributions to
\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}}_{N}
. This significantly reduces the number of mesoscopic boxes that we need to analyze, which brings much convenience. We can then (1) group disorders on mesoscopic boxes together to have
coarse-grained disorders
(similar to (
2.40
) and (
2.43
) below), whose structures are similar to that of
\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}}_{N}
; (2) replace the random walk transition kernels connecting coarse-grained disorders by heat kernels labeled by mesoscopic indices, where the costs can be controlled.

After all the treatments above, we end up with a
coarse-grained model
(similar to (
2.46
) below), which can be made arbitrarily close to
\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}}_{N}
for large enough
N
and small enough
\varepsilon
. The convenience of working on the coarse-grained model is that, when taking
N\to\infty
, we only need to analyze the coarse-grained disorders, without changing the structure of the model, unlike
\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}}_{N}
.

Finally, to show that
{\mathbbm{E}}[f(\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}}_{N})]
is a Cauchy sequence, we can first replace
\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}}_{N}
there by the coarse-grained model to obtain a new expectation sequence, and then show that the new expectation sequence is a Cauchy sequence as
\varepsilon\to 0
, thanks to an enhanced Lindeberg principle (see Appendix
A
). It is worth mentioning that in this last step, we need to estimate the higher moments of the coarse-grained disorders. This is done by estimating the higher moments of
\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}}_{N}
, which is a byproduct of
[
CSZ23
]
.

2.3.
Proof outlines for Theorem
1.7
and Theorem
1.10

The strategy for proving Theorem
1.7
and Theorem
1.10
is an adaption of Subsection
2.2
. Hence, we only highlight some differences below.

We mainly focus on Theorem
1.7
.

•

In our variant of directed polymer model (
1.25
), the disorders are only located on the time-space line
{\mathbbm{Z}}\times\{0\}
. Hence, except when visiting the origin, the random walk distribution is not tilted by the polymer measure. Therefore, we only need to introduce the mesoscopic time interval
{\mathcal{T}}_{N,\varepsilon}(\mathrm{i})
in (
2.11
), instead of the mesoscopic time-space boxes
{\mathcal{B}}_{N,\varepsilon}(\mathrm{i},\mathrm{a})
, since we only concern the return times to
0
.

•

As a consequence, we have much less estimates on random walk transition probability than those in
[
CSZ23
]
. To be specific, unlike in
[
CSZ23
]
, large deviation estimates are not necessary in our case. We only require a local limit theorem, and in particular, a sharp estimate for the probability of the random walk returning to 0. This allows us to relax the constraint on the random walk to the assumption (
1.10
) (e.g. see (
4.27
)-(
4.28
)).

To prove Theorem
1.10
, we adapt the techniques for continuous setting in
[
CSZ19b
]
and show that
\iint_{{\mathbbm{R}}^{2}}\varphi(x)u_{0,1}^{\delta}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}y)\psi(y)
(see Remark
1.11
) can also be approximated by the coarse-grained model (see
2.46
) introduced when proving Theorem
1.7
.

2.4.
Polynomial chaos expansion

The polynomial chaos expansion is a celebrated tool in treating random polymer models when the random environment is independent in time, see, e.g.,
[
CSZ16
,
CSZ17
,
CSZ23
]
. We illustrate it by taking
Z_{N,\beta_{N}}^{\omega}
defined in (
1.13
) as an example.

Recall
\zeta_{n}
from (
1.18
) and (
1.19
). Note that

e^{(\beta_{N}\omega_{n}-\lambda(\beta_{N}))\mathbbm{1}_{\{S_{n}=0\}}}=1+\Big(e^{\beta_{N}\omega_{n}-\lambda(\beta_{N})}-1\Big)\mathbbm{1}_{\{S_{n}=0\}}=1+\zeta_{n}\mathbbm{1}_{\{S_{n}=0\}}.

Then with the convention
n_{0}:=0
,

(2.12)

\begin{split}Z_{N,\beta_{N}}^{\omega}=&{\mathbf{E}}\Big[\prod\limits_{n=1}^{N}\Big(1+\zeta_{n}\mathbbm{1}_{\{S_{n}=0\}}\Big)\Big]=1+\sum\limits_{k=1}^{N}\beta_{N}^{k}\sum\limits_{1\leq n_{1}<\cdots<n_{k}\leq N}\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}p_{n_{i-1},n_{i}}(0)\zeta_{n_{i}},\end{split}

where

(2.13)

p_{m,n}(x,y):={\mathbf{P}}(S_{n}=y|S_{m}=x),\quad\text{for}~m<n,

is the notation for the random walk transition kernel with the conventions
p_{m,n}(y)=p_{m,n}(0,y)
,
p_{n}(y)=p_{0,n}(y)
, and
p_{0}(0)=1
.

Consequently, the integral of test functions
\varphi,\psi
with respect to the measure
\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{0,N}^{\beta_{N}}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}y)
defined in (
1.26
) can be expressed by the polynomial chaos expansion

(2.14)

\begin{split}&\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{0,N}^{\beta_{N}}(\varphi,\psi):=\iint_{{\mathbbm{R}}^{2}}\varphi(x)\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{0,N}^{\beta_{N}}(x,y)\psi(y)\\
=&\sqrt{N}\sum\limits_{u,v\in\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}{\mathbbm{Z}}}\int_{u}^{u+\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}}\varphi(x){\mathrm{d}}x\Big(\widetilde{Z}_{0,N}^{\omega,\beta_{N}}\big(u\sqrt{N},v\sqrt{N}\big)\Big)\int_{v}^{v+\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}}\psi(y){\mathrm{d}}y\\
=&q_{0,N}^{N}(\varphi,\psi)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum\limits_{r=1}^{\infty}\sum_{0<n_{1}<\cdots<n_{r}<N}q_{0,n_{1}}^{N}(\varphi,0)\zeta_{n_{1}}\Big(\prod\limits_{j=2}^{r}p_{n_{j-1},n_{j}}(0)\zeta_{n_{j}}\Big)q_{n_{r},N}^{N}(0,\psi),\end{split}

where for
x,y\in{\mathbbm{Z}}
,

(2.15)

\displaystyle q_{0,n}^{N}(\varphi,x)

\displaystyle:=\sum\limits_{u\in{\mathbbm{Z}}}\varphi_{N}(u)p_{n}(x-u),

(2.16)

\displaystyle q_{m,N}^{N}(y,\psi)

\displaystyle:=\sum\limits_{v\in{\mathbbm{Z}}}p_{m,N}(v-y)\psi_{N}(v),

(2.17)

\displaystyle q_{0,N}^{N}(\varphi,\psi)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum\limits_{u,v\in{\mathbbm{Z}}}\varphi_{N}(u)p_{N}(v-u)\psi_{N}(v),

with

(2.18)

\varphi_{N}(x):=\int_{x}^{x+1}\varphi\Big(\frac{t}{\sqrt{N}}\Big){\mathrm{d}}t,\quad\psi_{N}(y):=\int_{y}^{y+1}\psi\Big(\frac{s}{\sqrt{N}}\Big){\mathrm{d}}s.

As
(\zeta_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
are i.i.d. with mean 0, we have

(2.19)

\begin{split}{\mathbbm{E}}\left[(\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{0,N}^{\beta_{N}}(\varphi,\psi))^{2}\right]&=\left(q_{0,N}^{N}(\varphi,\psi)\right)^{2}+\\
\frac{1}{N}&\sum\limits_{r=1}^{\infty}(\sigma_{N}^{2})^{r}\sum\limits_{0<n_{1}<\cdots<n_{r}<N}\left(q_{0,n_{1}}^{N}(\varphi,0)\right)^{2}\Big(\prod\limits_{j=2}^{r}u(n_{j}-n_{j-1})\Big)\left(q_{n_{r},N}^{N}(0,\psi)\right)^{2},\end{split}

where
\sigma_{N}^{2}={\rm Var}(\zeta_{n})
, and for
n\in{\mathbbm{Z}}
,

(2.20)

u(n):=p_{n}^{2}(0)\sim\frac{1}{2\pi n}.

A powerful tool for computing the second moment (
2.19
) is the so-called Dickman subordinator, which we now introduce in the following subsection.

2.5.
Dickman subordinator and second moment computations

We first introduce a renewal process
\iota^{(N)}=\{\iota^{(N)}_{1},\iota^{(N)}_{2},\cdots\}
on
{\mathbbm{N}}\cup\{0\}
with one-step distribution

(2.21)

{\mathbf{P}}\big(\iota^{(N)}_{1}=n\big)=\frac{u(n)}{R_{N}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{1,\cdots,N\}}(n),

where
R_{N}
is the expected overlap (see (
1.11
)).

In view of (
2.19
) above, if the first and last return times to
0
for the random walk are denoted by
m
and
n
respectively with
m<n
, then we have that by the renewal structures of the random walk and (
2.21
),

(2.22)

\begin{split}&(\sigma_{N}^{2})^{2}u(n-m)+\sum\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}(\sigma_{N}^{2})^{k+2}\sum\limits_{n_{0}:=m<n_{1}<\cdots<n_{k}<n=:n_{k+1}}\prod\limits_{j=1}^{k+1}u(n_{j}-u_{j-1})\\
=&\sigma_{N}^{2}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}(\lambda_{N})^{k}{\mathbf{P}}(\iota^{(N)}_{k}=n-m),\quad\text{where}~\lambda_{N}:=\sigma_{N}^{2}R_{N}=1+\frac{\vartheta+o(1)}{\log N}.\end{split}

Hence, to analyze (
2.19
), in particular, (
2.22
), it turns out that we can investigate the renewal process
\iota^{(N)}
, which has been intensively studied in
[
CSZ19a
]
. We cite the following result.

Proposition 2.1

(
[
CSZ23
]
, Lemma 3.3)
.

Let
\iota^{(N)}
be defined as above, then as
N\to\infty

\bigg(\frac{\iota_{\lfloor s\log N\rfloor}^{(N)}}{N}\bigg)_{s\geq 0}\overset{({\rm d})}{\longrightarrow}(Y_{s})_{s\geq 0},

where
Y_{s}
is a pure jump Lévy process with Lévy measure
\frac{1}{t}\mathbbm{1}_{(0,1)}(t){\mathrm{d}}t
called the
Dickman subordinator
, and the convergence in distribution holds on the space of càdlàg paths equipped with the Skorohod topology. Moreover, the density of
Y_{s}
is known (
cf.

[
CSZ19a
, Theorem 1.1]
), which is

(2.23)

f_{s}(t)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{st^{s-1}e^{-\gamma s}}{\Gamma(s+1)},\quad&\text{for}~t\in(0,1],\\
\displaystyle\frac{st^{s-1}e^{-\gamma s}}{\Gamma(s+1)}-st^{s-1}\int_{0}^{t-1}\frac{f_{s}(a)}{(1+a)^{s}}{\mathrm{d}}s,\quad&\text{for}~t\in(0,+\infty),\end{cases}

for all
s\in(0,+\infty)
, where
\Gamma(\cdot)
is the Gamma function and
\gamma=-\int_{0}^{\infty}\log ue^{-u}{\mathrm{d}}u
is the Euler-Mascheroni constant.

To include the case that there is only one return to
0
(i.e.,
m=n
in (
2.22
)), we introduce the following quantity, which plays a central role throughout the paper. Let

(2.24)

\overline{U}_{N}(n):=\begin{cases}\sigma_{N}^{2},&\quad\text{if}~n=0,\\
\displaystyle(\sigma_{N}^{2})^{2}u(n)+\sum\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}(\sigma_{N}^{2})^{k+2}\sum\limits_{n_{0}:=0<n_{1}<\cdots<n_{k}<n=:n_{k+1}}\prod\limits_{j=1}^{k+1}u(n_{j}-n_{j-1}),&\quad\text{if}~n\geq 1.\end{cases}

We then collect some useful properties of
\overline{U}_{N}(n)
from
[
CSZ19a
, Section 1-3]
. Given
\vartheta
in (
1.20
), let

(2.25)

G_{\vartheta}(t):=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{\vartheta s}\alpha_{s}(t){\mathrm{d}}s,

where
\alpha_{s}(\cdot)
is the density of the Dickman subordinator
Y_{s}
(see Proposition
2.1
).

•

By
[
CSZ19a
, Theorem 1.4]
, given
T>0
, there exists some constant
C>0
, such that

(2.26)

\overline{U}(n)\leq\frac{C}{N}G_{\vartheta}\left(\frac{n}{N}\right),\quad\text{uniformly for all}~1\leq n\leq TN.

Furthermore, if
\delta\in(0,1)
is also given, then

(2.27)

\overline{U}_{N}(n)=\frac{2\pi}{N}\left(G_{\vartheta}\left(\frac{n}{N}\right)+o_{N}(1)\right),\quad\text{uniformly for all}~\delta N\leq n\leq TN.

•

By
[
CSZ19a
, Proposition 1.6]
, the asymptotics of
G_{\vartheta}(t)
is known, that is,

(2.28)

\displaystyle G_{\vartheta}(t)\sim\frac{1}{t}\Big(\log\frac{1}{t}\Big)^{-2}\quad\text{and}\quad\int_{0}^{t}G_{\vartheta}(s){\mathrm{d}}s\sim\Big(\log\frac{1}{t}\Big)^{-1},\quad\text{as}\quad t\to 0.

Remark 2.2
.

It is not surprising that the Dickman subordinator and the function
\overline{U}(\cdot)
appear both in
[
CSZ23
]
and our paper. If we perform a polynomial chaos expansion to
\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}}_{N}
in Subsection
2.2
and compute its second moment, then we have

\begin{split}{\mathbbm{E}}\left[(\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}}_{N})^{2}\right]&=\left(q_{0,N}^{N}(\varphi,\psi)\right)^{2}+\\
\frac{1}{N}&\sum\limits_{r=1}^{\infty}(\sigma_{N}^{2})^{r}\sum\limits_{0<n_{1}<\cdots<n_{r}<N\atop x_{1},\cdots,x_{r}\in{\mathbbm{Z}}^{2}}\left(q_{0,n_{1}}^{N}(\varphi,x_{1})\right)^{2}\Big(\prod\limits_{j=2}^{r}p_{n_{j-1},n_{j}}(x_{j-1},x_{j})^{2}\Big)\left(q_{n_{r},N}^{N}(x_{r},\psi)\right)^{2},\end{split}

where we have slightly abused the notation
p_{m,n}(x,y)
for simple random walk on
{\mathbbm{Z}}^{2}
.

Next, we introduce a pair of a renewal process and a random walk
(\overline{\iota}^{(N)}_{k},\overline{S}^{(N)}_{k})_{k\geq 0}
on
({\mathbbm{N}}\cup\{0\})\times{\mathbbm{Z}}^{2}
starting at
(0,0)
with one-step probability

{\mathbf{P}}\Big(\overline{\iota}^{(N)}_{1}=n,\overline{S}^{(N)}_{1}=x\Big):=\frac{p_{n}(x)^{2}}{\overline{R}_{N}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{1,\cdots,N\}}(n)\quad\text{where}~\overline{R}_{N}:=\sum\limits_{n=1}^{N}{\mathbf{P}}(S_{n}=S^{\prime}_{n}),

and
S_{n}
and
S^{\prime}_{n}
are two independent simple random walks on
{\mathbbm{Z}}^{2}
. Note that

\sum\limits_{x\in{\mathbbm{Z}}^{2}}{\mathbf{P}}\Big(\overline{\iota}^{(N)}_{1}=n,\overline{S}^{(N)}_{1}=x\Big)={\mathbf{P}}\Big(\overline{\iota}^{(N)}_{1}=n\Big)=\frac{{\mathbf{P}}(S_{n}=S^{\prime}_{n})}{\overline{R}_{N}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{1,\cdots,N\}}(n)

and
{\mathbf{P}}(S_{n}=S^{\prime}_{n})={\mathbf{P}}(S_{2n}=0)\sim\frac{1}{\pi n}
, and consequently
\overline{R}_{N}\sim\log N/\pi
. Hence, the distributions of
\overline{\iota}^{(N)}_{1}
and
\iota^{(N)}_{1}
in (
2.21
) have the same asymptotics.

Finally, similar to (
2.22
), a straightforward computations yields that

\begin{split}&\sum\limits_{x\in{\mathbbm{Z}}}\bigg((\sigma_{N}^{2})^{2}p_{n}(x)+\sum\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}(\sigma_{N}^{2})^{k+2}\sum\limits_{n_{0}:=0<n_{1}<\cdots<n_{k}<n=:n_{k+1}\atop x_{0}:=0,x_{1},\cdots,x_{k},x_{k+1}:=x}\prod\limits_{j=1}^{k+1}p_{n_{j-1},n_{j}}(x_{j-1},x_{j})^{2}\bigg)\\
=&\sigma_{N}^{2}\sum\limits_{x\in{\mathbbm{Z}}^{2}}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}(\lambda_{N})^{k}{\mathbf{P}}\Big(\overline{\iota}^{(N)}_{k}=n,\overline{S}^{(N)}_{k}=x\Big)=\sigma_{N}^{2}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}(\lambda_{N})^{k}{\mathbf{P}}\Big(\overline{\iota}^{(N)}_{k}=n\Big),\end{split}

which has the same asymptotics of
\overline{U}_{N}(n)
. Here, the key observation is that both
{\mathbf{P}}(S_{2n}=0)
for simple random walk on
{\mathbbm{Z}}^{2}
and
u(n)
in (
2.20
) are asymptotically
cn^{-1}
.

As an easy application, we can immediately compute the limits of the first two moments of
\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{0,N}^{\beta_{N}}(\varphi,\psi)
. Recalling the heat kernel
g_{t}(x)
from (
1.31
), define

(2.29)

\displaystyle g_{t}(\varphi,a)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathbbm{R}}\varphi(x)g_{t}(a-x){\mathrm{d}}x,

(2.30)

\displaystyle g_{t}(b,\psi)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathbbm{R}}g_{t}(y-b)\psi(y){\mathrm{d}}y,

(2.31)

\displaystyle g_{t}(\varphi,\psi)

\displaystyle:=\int_{{\mathbbm{R}}\times{\mathbbm{R}}}\varphi(x)g_{t}(y-x)\varphi(y){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}y.

We omit the subscript
t
when
t=1
.

Proposition 2.3
.

For any
\varphi\in C_{c}({\mathbbm{R}})
and
\psi\in C_{b}({\mathbbm{R}})
, we have that

(2.32)

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{0,N}^{\beta_{N}}(\varphi,\psi)\Big]

\displaystyle=g(\varphi,\psi),

(2.33)

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\Big(\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{0,N}^{\beta_{N}}(\varphi,\psi)\Big)^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle=g(\varphi,\psi)^{2}+{\mathcal{V}}_{1}^{\vartheta}(\varphi,\psi)

where

(2.34)

{\mathcal{V}}_{1}^{\vartheta}(\varphi,\psi)=\iiiint_{{\mathbbm{R}}^{\otimes 4}}\varphi(x)\varphi(x^{\prime})K_{1}^{\vartheta}(x,x^{\prime};y,y^{\prime})\psi(y)\psi(y^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}y{\mathrm{d}}y^{\prime}\leq 2\pi\|\varphi\|_{\infty}^{2}\|\psi\|^{2}_{\infty}\int_{0}^{1}G_{\vartheta}(u){\mathrm{d}}u

with

(2.35)

\displaystyle K_{t}^{\vartheta}(x,x^{\prime};y,y^{\prime})

\displaystyle:=2\pi\iint_{0<r<s<t}g_{r}(x)g_{r}(x^{\prime})G_{\vartheta}(s-r)g_{t-s}(y)g_{t-s}(y^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}r{\mathrm{d}}s.

Proof.

The limit (
2.32
) directly follows by the local limit theorem and a Riemann sum approximation.

For (
2.33
), we only need to treat the second line of (
2.19
). We have that

(2.36)

\begin{split}q_{0,n}^{N}(\varphi,0)&=\sum\limits_{u\in{\mathbbm{Z}}}\sqrt{N}\int_{\frac{u}{\sqrt{N}}}^{\frac{u+1}{\sqrt{N}}}\varphi(x){\mathbf{P}}(S_{n}=u){\mathrm{d}}x=\int_{\mathbbm{R}}\varphi(x)g_{\frac{n}{N}}(x){\mathrm{d}}x+o_{N}(1)\\
q_{n,N}^{N}(0,\psi)&=\sum\limits_{v\in{\mathbbm{Z}}}\sqrt{N}\int_{\frac{v}{\sqrt{N}}}^{\frac{v+1}{\sqrt{N}}}\psi(y){\mathbf{P}}(S_{N-n}=v){\mathrm{d}}y=\int_{\mathbbm{R}}\varphi(y)g_{1-\frac{n}{N}}(y){\mathrm{d}}y+o_{N}(1).\end{split}

Then recall the definition and properties of
\overline{U}(n)
from (
2.24
)-(
2.27
). By a Riemann sum approximation, we have that for any
\varepsilon>0
and
\varepsilon N\leq n\leq(1-\varepsilon)N
,

\begin{split}\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}&\sum\limits_{s,t\in\frac{1}{N}{\mathbbm{N}}\cap[\varepsilon,1-\varepsilon]}\frac{1}{N^{2}}g_{s}(x)g_{s}(x^{\prime})\overline{U}_{N}(t-s)g_{1-t}(y)g_{1-t}(y^{\prime})\\
=&2\pi\int_{\varepsilon<s<t<1-\varepsilon}g_{s}(x)g_{s}(x^{\prime})G_{\vartheta}(t-s)g_{1-t}(y)g_{1-t}(y^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}s{\mathrm{d}}t.\end{split}

By (
2.26
), the contribution from
n\leq\varepsilon N
and
n\geq(1-\varepsilon)N
is bounded by
C_{\varphi,\psi}\int_{[0,\varepsilon]\cup[1-\varepsilon,1]}G_{\vartheta}(t){\mathrm{d}}t
, which converges to
0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. The proof is completed.
∎

Remark 2.4
.

It can be seen from above that if we introduce an external field
h_{N}
in (
1.25
), then the proper choice should be
h_{N}=\widehat{h}/\sqrt{N}
with
\widehat{h}\in{\mathbbm{R}}
. In this case,
\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbbm{E}}\big[\widetilde{{\mathcal{Z}}}_{0,N}^{\beta_{N}}(\varphi,\psi)\big]
is shifted by

\sum\limits_{r=1}^{\infty}(\widehat{h})^{r}\idotsint\limits_{0<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{r}<1}g_{t_{1}}(\varphi,0)\Big(\prod\limits_{i=2}^{r}\frac{1}{\sqrt{t_{i}-t_{i-1}}}\Big)g_{1-t_{r}}(\psi,0){\mathrm{d}}t_{1},\dots{\mathrm{d}}t_{r},

which is convergent by
[
CSZ16
, Lemma B.2]
. Note that as long as
h_{N}=o((\log N)^{-1})
, the asymptotics of
\sigma_{N}^{2}
in (
1.20
) remains unchanged. Since
h_{N}
only affects the mean, we can set
h_{N}\equiv 0
without loss of generality.

2.6.
Coarse graining procedure

We now introduce the definitions and notations related to the
mesoscopic time intervals
, the
coarse-grained disorders
and the
coarse-grained model
introduced in Subsection
2.2
-
2.3
.

Given
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
N\in{\mathbbm{N}}
, we partition the time period
[1,N]
into mesoscopic intervals

(2.37)

{\mathcal{T}}_{N,\varepsilon}(\mathrm{i}):=((\mathrm{i}-1)\varepsilon N,\mathrm{i}\varepsilon N],\quad\mathrm{i}=1,\cdots,\left\lfloor\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right\rfloor.

Fix a threshold

(2.38)

K_{\varepsilon}:=\Big(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\Big)^{6}

and we introduce a collection of indices for the mesoscopic intervals, which is

(2.39)

\begin{split}{\mathcal{A}}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(no triple)}}:=\bigcup\limits_{k\in{\mathbbm{N}}}\Big\{&(\mathrm{i}_{1},\cdots,\mathrm{i}_{k})\in{\mathbbm{N}}^{k}:K_{\varepsilon}\leq\mathrm{i}_{1}<\cdots<\mathrm{i}_{k}\leq\left\lfloor\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right\rfloor-K_{\varepsilon},\\
&\text{such that if}~\mathrm{i}_{j+1}-\mathrm{i}_{j}<K_{\varepsilon},\text{then}~\mathrm{i}_{j+2}-\mathrm{i}_{j+1}\geq K_{\varepsilon}\Big\}.\end{split}

This set is labeled by “no triple” since there is no consecutive three indices
\mathrm{i}_{j},\mathrm{i}_{j+1},\mathrm{i}_{j+2}
such that
\mathrm{i}_{j+1}-\mathrm{i}_{j}<K_{\varepsilon}
and
\mathrm{i}_{j+2}-\mathrm{i}_{j+1}<K_{\varepsilon}
.

To proceed, we introduce

(2.40)

X_{d,f}:=\begin{cases}\zeta_{d},&\quad\text{if}~d=f,\\
\zeta_{d}\widetilde{Z}_{d,f}^{\omega,\beta_{N}}(0,0)\zeta_{f},&\quad\text{if}~f\geq d+1,\end{cases}

where
\zeta_{n}
is defined in (
1.18
) and
\widetilde{Z}_{d,f}^{\omega,\beta_{N}}(0,0)
is defined in (
1.25
).

Now we can define a modified version of
\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{0,N}^{\beta_{N}}(\varphi,\psi)
, which is

(2.41)

\begin{split}{\mathcal{Z}}_{N,\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(no triple)}}(\varphi,\psi):=q_{0,N}^{N}(\varphi,\psi)+&\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{\left(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2}}\sum\limits_{(\mathrm{i}_{1},\cdots,\mathrm{i}_{k})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(no triple)}}}\sum\limits_{d_{1}\leq f_{1}\in{\mathcal{T}}_{N,\varepsilon}(\mathrm{i}_{1}),\cdots,d_{k}\leq f_{k}\in{\mathcal{T}}_{N,\varepsilon}(\mathrm{i}_{k})}\\
&q_{0,d_{1}}^{N}(\varphi,0)X_{d_{1},f_{1}}\bigg\{\prod\limits_{j=2}^{k}p_{d_{j}-f_{j-1}}(0)X_{d_{j},f_{j}}\bigg\}q_{f_{k},N}^{N}(0,\psi),\end{split}

where we rule out the consecutive visits to mesoscopic intervals
{\mathcal{T}}_{N,\varepsilon}(\mathrm{i}_{j}),{\mathcal{T}}_{N,\varepsilon}(\mathrm{i}_{j+1})
and
{\mathcal{T}}_{N,\varepsilon}(\mathrm{i}_{j+2})
with
\mathrm{i}_{j+1}-\mathrm{i}_{j}<K_{\varepsilon}
and
\mathrm{i}_{j+2}-\mathrm{i}_{j+1}<K_{\varepsilon}
.

We will approximate
\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{0,N}^{\beta_{N}}(\varphi,\psi)
by
{\mathcal{Z}}_{N,\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(no triple)}}(\varphi,\psi)
in
L_{2}
.

Then we introduce the width and the “distance” for mesoscopic time intervals.

Definition 2.5
.

We call any pair
\vec{\mathrm{i}}=(\mathrm{i},\mathrm{i}^{\prime})\in{\mathbbm{N}}\times{\mathbbm{N}}
with
\mathrm{i}\leq\mathrm{i}^{\prime}
a
time block
. Its width is defined by

|\vec{\mathrm{i}}|:=\mathrm{i}^{\prime}-\mathrm{i}+1.

The (non-symmetric) “distance” between two time blocks
\vec{\mathrm{i}}=(\mathrm{i},\mathrm{i}^{\prime})
and
\vec{\mathrm{j}}=(\mathrm{j},\mathrm{j}^{\prime})
is defined by

{\rm dist}(\vec{\mathrm{i}},\vec{\mathrm{j}})=\mathrm{j}-\mathrm{i}^{\prime}.

We write
\vec{\mathrm{i}}<\vec{\mathrm{j}}
if and only if
\mathrm{i}^{\prime}<\mathrm{j}
. For some constant
K
, we write
K\leq\vec{\mathrm{i}}
if and only if
K\leq\mathrm{i}
and
\vec{\mathrm{i}}\leq K
if and only if
\mathrm{i}^{\prime}\leq K
.

With the definitions above, we can pair
\mathrm{i}_{j}
and
\mathrm{i}_{j+1}
for
\mathrm{i}_{j+1}-\mathrm{i}_{j}<K_{\varepsilon}
as
\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{j}
and by noting the “no triple” condition, we can rewrite
{\mathcal{A}}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{(no triple)}}
by

(2.42)

\begin{split}\vec{\mathbf{{\mathcal{A}}}}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(no triple)}}:=\bigcup\limits_{r\in{\mathbbm{N}}}\Big\{&(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1},\cdots,\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{r})\in{\mathbbm{N}}^{r}:K_{\varepsilon}\leq\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1}<\cdots<\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{k}\leq\Big\lfloor\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\Big\rfloor-K_{\varepsilon},~\text{such that}\\
&|\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{j}|<K_{\varepsilon},\forall~j=1,\cdots,r,~{\rm dist}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{j-1},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{j})\geq K_{\varepsilon},\forall~j=2,\cdots,r\Big\}.\end{split}

We then bind
X_{d,f}
and
X_{d^{\prime},f^{\prime}}
together if their mesoscopic distance is smaller than
K_{\varepsilon}
, which gives the definition of the coarse-grained variable. To be precise, let

(2.43)

\Theta(\vec{\mathrm{i}})=\Theta_{N,\varepsilon}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}):=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\varepsilon N}}\sum\limits_{d\leq f\in{\mathcal{T}}_{N,\varepsilon}(\mathrm{i})}X_{d,f},&\text{if}~|\vec{\mathrm{i}}|=1,\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{\varepsilon N}}\sum\limits_{d\leq f\in{\mathcal{T}}_{N,\varepsilon}(\mathrm{i})}\sum\limits_{d^{\prime}\leq f^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{T}}_{N,\varepsilon}(\mathrm{i}^{\prime})}\big(X_{d,f}\times p_{d^{\prime}-f}(0)\times X_{d^{\prime},f^{\prime}}\big),\quad&\text{if}~|\vec{\mathrm{i}}|>1.\end{cases}

Now we introduce a second modified version of
\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{0,N}^{\beta_{N}}(\varphi,\psi)
, which is

(2.44)

\begin{split}{\mathcal{Z}}_{N,\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(cg)}}(\varphi,\psi):=g_{1}(\varphi,\psi)+&\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sum\limits_{r=1}^{(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon})^{2}}\sum\limits_{(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1},\cdots,\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{r})\in\vec{\mathbf{{\mathcal{A}}}}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(no triple)}}}\sum\limits_{\mathrm{a},\mathrm{b}\in{\mathbbm{Z}}}\\
&\varphi_{N,\varepsilon}(\mathrm{a})g_{\mathrm{i}_{1}}(\mathrm{a})\Theta(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1})\times\bigg\{\prod_{j=2}^{r}g_{(\mathrm{i}_{j}-\mathrm{i}^{\prime}_{j-1})}(0)\Theta(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{j})\bigg\}g_{(\lfloor\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\rfloor-\mathrm{i}^{\prime}_{r})}(\mathrm{b})\psi_{N,\varepsilon}(\mathrm{b}),\end{split}

where
g_{t}(x)
is the heat kernel defined in (
1.31
), and

(2.45)

\varphi_{N,\varepsilon}(\mathrm{a}):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\varepsilon N}}\sum\limits_{u\in(\mathrm{a}\sqrt{\varepsilon N},(\mathrm{a}+1)\sqrt{\varepsilon N}]\cap{\mathbbm{Z}}}\varphi_{N}(u),\quad\psi_{N,\varepsilon}(\mathrm{b}):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\varepsilon N}}\sum\limits_{v\in(\mathrm{b}\sqrt{\varepsilon N},(\mathrm{b}+1)\sqrt{\varepsilon N}]\cap{\mathbbm{Z}}}\varphi_{N}(v).

We will approximate
{\mathcal{Z}}_{N,\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(no triple)}}(\varphi,\psi)
by
{\mathcal{Z}}_{N,\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(cg)}}(\varphi,\psi)
in
L_{2}
.

Finally, we introduce the coarse-grained model
\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(cg)}}(\varphi,\psi|\Theta)
. We define

(2.46)

\begin{split}\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(cg)}}(\varphi,\psi|\Theta):=g_{1}(\varphi,\psi)+&\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sum\limits_{r=1}^{(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon})^{2}}\sum\limits_{(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1},\cdots,\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{r})\in\vec{\mathbf{{\mathcal{A}}}}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(no triple)}}}\sum\limits_{\mathrm{a},\mathrm{b}\in{\mathbbm{Z}}}\\
&\varphi_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{a})g_{\mathrm{i}_{1}}(\mathrm{a})\Theta(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1})\times\bigg\{\prod_{j=2}^{r}g_{(\mathrm{i}_{j}-\mathrm{i}^{\prime}_{j-1})}(0)\Theta_{N,\varepsilon}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{j})\bigg\}g_{(\lfloor\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\rfloor-\mathrm{i}^{\prime}_{r})}(\mathrm{b})\psi_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{b}),\end{split}

where

(2.47)

\varphi_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{a}):=\int_{\mathrm{a}}^{\mathrm{a}+1}\varphi(\sqrt{\varepsilon}x){\mathrm{d}}x,\quad\psi_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{b}):=\int_{\mathrm{b}}^{\mathrm{b}+1}\psi(\sqrt{\varepsilon}y){\mathrm{d}}y,\quad\text{for}~\mathrm{a},\mathrm{b}\in{\mathbbm{Z}}.

We will approximate
{\mathcal{Z}}_{N,\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(cg)}}(\varphi,\psi)
by
\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(cg)}}(\varphi,\psi|\Theta)
in
L_{2}
. For
u_{0,1}^{\delta}(\varphi,\psi)
, we will also approximate it by

\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(cg)}}(\varphi,\psi|\Theta_{\delta,\varepsilon})
, where
\Theta_{\delta,\varepsilon}
is a continuum version of coarse-grained variables (see (
8.23
)).

2.7.
Organizations of the paper

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. In Section
3
, we complete the proof of Theorem
1.6
. In Section
4
, we estimate the moments of
\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{0,N}^{\beta_{N}}(\varphi,\psi)
. In Section
5
, we approximate
\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{0,N}^{\beta_{N}}(\varphi,\psi)
by the coarse-grained model. In Section
6
, we bound the fourth moment of the coarse-grained model, where a first step is to bound the moments of the coarse-grained disorders. In Section
7
, we prove Theorem
1.7
by showing the convergence of the coarse-grained model, where an enhanced Lindeberg principle is applied. In Section
8
, we prove Theorem
1.10
.

Since Theorem
1.6
proved in Section
3
is novel, and the continuous setting treated in Section
8
was not addressed in
[
CSZ23
]
, we provide enough details for both sections. In contrast, the proofs in Sections
4
-
7
are adapted from
[
CSZ23
]
. Section
7
is written with full details as the corresponding part in
[
CSZ23
]
is only outlined. For the remaining three sections, we explain the strategies in detail but only sketch some computations, so that the paper is convincing while maintaining in a reasonable length. We also point out the major differences and modifications between
[
CSZ23
]
and our paper. To make the paper self-contained, we include the enhanced Lindeberg principle in Appendix
A
.

2.8.
Notations

Due to various notations in our paper, we collect some important ones here.

\bullet

\zeta_{n}
: The disorder in polynomial chaos expansion, see (
1.18
).

\bullet

\sigma_{N}^{2}
: The variance of
\zeta_{n}
, see (
1.19
).

\bullet

{\mathcal{Z}}_{N}^{\beta_{N}}({\mathrm{d}}s,{\mathrm{d}}t)
