: The pinning measure, see (
1.15
).

\bullet

\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{0,N}^{\beta_{N}}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}y)
: The directed polymer measure, see (
1.26
).

\bullet

\overline{U}_{N}(n)
: A renewal structure related to the Dickman subordinator, see (
2.24
).

\bullet

G_{\vartheta}(t)
: The asymptotics of
\overline{U}_{N}(n)
, see (
2.25
).

\bullet

X_{d,f}
: The directed polymer partition function
\widetilde{Z}_{d,f}^{\omega,\beta_{N}}(0,0)
equipped with
\zeta_{d}
and
\zeta_{f}
, see (
2.40
).

\bullet

\Theta_{N,\varepsilon}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})
: The coarse-grained variable, see (
2.43
).

\bullet

\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(cg)}}(\varphi,\psi|\Theta)
: The coarse-grained model, see (
2.46
).

3.
Proof of Theorem
1.6

We are going to show the equivalence between (i) and (ii) in Theorem
1.6
. From Subsection
2.1
, it suffices to work on
\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{0,N}^{\beta_{N}}(\varphi,\psi)
and
{\mathcal{Z}}_{N}^{\beta_{N}}(f,h)
(see (
2.9
) and (
2.10
)), which can be expressed by

(3.1)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{0,N}^{\beta_{N}}(\varphi,\psi)=

\displaystyle\iint_{{\mathbbm{R}}\times{\mathbbm{R}}}\varphi(x)\sqrt{N}\hskip 1.0pt{\mathbf{P}}^{x_{N}}(S_{N}=y_{N},\tau_{1}\geq N)\psi(y){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}y

\displaystyle+\sum\limits_{m=1}^{N-1}\sum\limits_{n=m}^{N-1}

\displaystyle\Big(\int_{{\mathbbm{R}}}\varphi(x)\sqrt{N}q_{x_{N}}(m){\mathrm{d}}x\Big)\Big(\sqrt{N}Z^{\omega,\beta_{N}}_{m,n}\Big)\Big(\int_{{\mathbbm{R}}}\psi(x)\sqrt{N}q_{-y_{N}}(N-n){\mathrm{d}}y\Big),

(3.2)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Z}}_{N}^{\beta_{N}}(f,h)=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{m=0}^{N-1}\sum\limits_{n=m}^{N-1}\Big(\int_{\frac{m}{N}}^{\frac{m+1}{N}}f(s){\mathrm{d}}s\Big)\Big(\sqrt{N}Z_{m,n}^{\omega,\beta_{N}}\Big)\Big(\int_{\frac{n}{N}}^{\frac{n+1}{N}}h(t){\mathrm{d}}t\Big).

In particular, it suffices to show that

(a) the first term in (
3.1
) converges;

(b) the term refers to
m=0
, that is

(3.3)

\mathscr{R}_{N}:=\mathscr{R}_{N}(f,h)=\sqrt{N}\int_{0}^{\frac{1}{N}}f(s){\mathrm{d}}s\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}Z_{0,n}^{\omega,\beta_{N}}\int_{\frac{n}{N}}^{\frac{n+1}{N}}h(t){\mathrm{d}}t,

converges to
0
.

(c) two pairs of integrals,

(3.4)

\int_{{\mathbbm{R}}}\varphi(x)\sqrt{N}q_{x_{N}}(m){\mathrm{d}}x~~\text{with}~~\int_{\frac{m}{N}}^{\frac{m+1}{N}}f(s){\mathrm{d}}s\quad\text{and}\quad\int_{{\mathbbm{R}}}\psi(x)\sqrt{N}q_{-y_{N}}(N-n){\mathrm{d}}y~~\text{with}~~\int_{\frac{n}{N}}^{\frac{n+1}{N}}h(t){\mathrm{d}}t

can be made arbitrarily close and uniformly in
N
.

We start by treating
Part (a)
and
Part (b)
, which are common parts in both (ii)
\Longrightarrow
(i) and (i)
\Longrightarrow
(ii). A key ingredient in the proofs is the local limit theorem
[
Uch11
, Corollary 1.1]
for first hitting times. We have that as
N\to\infty
, uniformly in
x\in\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}{\mathbbm{Z}}
and
s\in\frac{1}{N}{\mathbbm{N}}
,

(3.5)

q(x\sqrt{N},sN)=\frac{|x|}{\sqrt{2\pi}s^{3/2}N}e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2s}}+o\Big(\frac{|x|\sqrt{N}\vee 1}{(sN)^{3/2}}\wedge\frac{1}{x^{2}N\vee 1}\Big)=:\frac{1}{N}Q(x,s)+\frac{1}{N}R_{N}(x,s).

Proof for Part (a).
We have already shown in Proposition
2.3
that

\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}{\mathbbm{E}}\big[\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{N}^{\beta_{N}}(\varphi,\psi)\big]=g(\varphi,\psi).

Hence, to prove Part (a), it suffices to prove that the expectation of the second line of (
3.1
), that is,

(3.6)

\sum\limits_{m=1}^{N-1}\sum\limits_{n=m}^{N-1}\Big(\int_{{\mathbbm{R}}}\varphi(x)\sqrt{N}q_{x_{N}}(m){\mathrm{d}}x\Big)\Big(\sqrt{N}p_{m,n}(0)\Big)\Big(\int_{{\mathbbm{R}}}\psi(x)\sqrt{N}q_{-y_{N}}(N-n){\mathrm{d}}y\Big),

also converges as
N\to\infty
.

By writing
m=sN
and
n=tN
and by
[
Uch11
, Corollary 1.1]
, the above quantity equals to

(3.7)

\begin{split}\sum\limits_{s<t\in\{\frac{1}{N},\cdots,\frac{N-1}{N}\}}\sum\limits_{u,v\in\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}{\mathbbm{Z}}}&\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\varphi(u)\frac{1}{N}\big(Q(|u|,s)+R_{N}(|u|,s)\big)\\
&\times\frac{1+o(1)}{\sqrt{2\pi(t-s)}}\frac{1}{N}\Big(Q(|v|,1-t)+R_{N}(|v|,1-t)\Big)\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\psi(v).\end{split}

The main term above, by a Riemann sum approximation, converges to

(3.8)

\mathscr{E}(\varphi,\psi)\!:=\!\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{\frac{3}{2}}}\!\iint_{0<s<t<1}\iint_{{\mathbbm{R}}\times{\mathbbm{R}}}\!\varphi(u)\frac{|uv|}{s\sqrt{s(t-s)(1-t)}(1-t)}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{u^{2}}{s}+\frac{v^{2}}{1-t}\right)}\psi(v){\mathrm{d}}u{\mathrm{d}}v{\mathrm{d}}t{\mathrm{d}}s.

Note that by a change of variable
x=u/\sqrt{s}
and
y=v/\sqrt{1-t}
, we have that

|\mathscr{E}(\varphi,\psi)|\leq\frac{\|\varphi\|_{\infty}\|\psi\|_{\infty}}{(2\pi)^{\frac{3}{2}}}\iint_{0<s<t<1}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}s{\mathrm{d}}t}{\sqrt{s(t-s)(1-t)}}\iint_{{\mathbbm{R}}^{2}}|xy|e^{-\frac{x^{2}+y^{2}}{2}}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}y<+\infty,

where the finiteness can be found in
[
CSZ16
, Lemma B.2]
. Hence,
\mathscr{E}(\varphi,\psi)
is well defined.

For the error term, we only treat the term with
u,s
. The term with
v,t
is exactly the same. Note that if
|u|\leq\sqrt{s}
, then
(sN)^{\frac{3}{2}}\geq((u^{2}N)^{\frac{3}{2}}\vee 1)
since
s\geq\frac{1}{N}
. The error term is then bounded by

(3.9)

\sum\limits_{u\in\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}{\mathbbm{Z}}\atop|u|\leq\sqrt{s}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\varphi(u)|u|\frac{o(1)}{s^{\frac{3}{2}}N}+\sum\limits_{u\in\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}{\mathbbm{Z}}\atop|u|>\sqrt{s}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\frac{\varphi(u)}{|u|^{2}}\frac{o(1)}{N}\leq\frac{o(1)}{s^{\frac{3}{2}}N}\int_{|u|\leq\sqrt{s}}|u|{\mathrm{d}}u+\frac{o(1)}{N}\int_{|u|\geq\sqrt{s}}\frac{1}{|u|^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}u=\frac{o(1)}{\sqrt{s}N},

which is integrable with respect to
s
at
0
.

In summary, the error term is negligible, and it follows that

\begin{split}\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}\iint_{{\mathbbm{R}}\times{\mathbbm{R}}}\varphi(x)\sqrt{N}\hskip 1.0pt{\mathbf{P}}^{x_{N}}(S_{N}=y_{N},\tau_{1}\geq N)\psi(y){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}y=g(\varphi,\psi)-\mathscr{E}(\varphi,\psi),\end{split}

which concludes Part (a).

Proof for Part (b).
For
\mathscr{R}_{N}
in (
3.3
), we show that
\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbbm{E}}[\mathscr{R}_{N}]=0
and
\lim_{N\to\infty}{\rm Var}(\mathscr{R}_{N})=0
.

Since
{\mathbbm{E}}[\zeta_{n}]=0
, the local limit theorem and a Riemann sum approximation yields that

(3.10)

\begin{split}{\mathbbm{E}}[\mathscr{R}_{N}]&=\sqrt{N}\int_{0}^{\frac{1}{N}}f(s){\mathrm{d}}s\sum\limits_{n=0}^{N-1}p_{n}(0)\int_{\frac{n}{N}}^{\frac{n+1}{N}}h(t){\mathrm{d}}t\sim\frac{1}{N}f(0)\int_{0}^{1}\frac{h(t)}{\sqrt{t}}{\mathrm{d}}t=\frac{O(1)}{N}.\end{split}

For the variance, perform a polynomial chaos expansion to
Z_{0,n}^{\omega,\beta_{N}}
(see Subsection
2.4
) , and we note that
{\mathbbm{E}}[\mathscr{R}_{N}]
is actually the deterministic term in
\mathscr{R}_{N}
. We have that

\mathscr{R}_{N}-{\mathbbm{E}}[\mathscr{R}_{N}]=\sqrt{N}\int_{0}^{\frac{1}{N}}f(s){\mathrm{d}}s\sum\limits_{n=0}^{N-1}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{n_{0}:=0\leq n_{1}<\cdots<n_{k}=n}\zeta_{0}^{\mathbbm{1}_{\{n\neq 0\}}}\Big(\prod\limits_{j=1}^{k}p_{n_{j-1},n_{j}}(0)\zeta_{n_{j}}\Big)\int_{\frac{n}{N}}^{\frac{n+1}{N}}h(t){\mathrm{d}}t.

Taking square and expectation, and by the independence of
(\zeta_{n})_{n\in{\mathbbm{Z}}}
, we have that

(3.11)

\begin{split}{\rm Var}(\mathscr{R}_{N})=N\Big(\int_{0}^{\frac{1}{N}}f(s){\mathrm{d}}s\Big)^{2}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{N-1}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}\big(\sigma_{N}^{2}\big)^{k+1}\sum\limits_{n_{0}:=0\leq n_{1}<\cdots<n_{k}=n}\Big(\prod_{j=1}^{k}u(n_{j}-n_{j-1})\Big)\Big(\int_{\frac{n}{N}}^{\frac{n+1}{N}}h(t){\mathrm{d}}t\Big)^{2},\end{split}

where
\sigma_{N}^{2}={\rm Var}(\zeta_{n})
(see (
1.19
)) and
u(n)
is defined in (
2.20
).

Recall the definition and properties of
\overline{U}_{N}(n)
from (
2.24
)-(
2.28
). Then (
3.11
) is bounded above by

(3.12)

\begin{split}\frac{1}{N^{3}}\|f\|_{\infty}^{2}\|h\|_{\infty}^{2}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{N-1}\overline{U}(n)\leq\frac{C_{f,h}}{N^{3}}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{N-1}\frac{1}{N}G_{\vartheta}\Big(\frac{n}{N}\Big)\leq\frac{C}{N^{3}}\int_{0}^{1}G_{\vartheta}(t){\mathrm{d}}t=\frac{O(1)}{N^{3}}.\end{split}

By (
3.10
) and (
3.12
), we have shown that
\mathscr{R}_{N}
converges to
0
in
L_{2}
.

Proof for Part (c).
We start by (i)
\Longrightarrow
(ii), which is more straightforward. Let

\displaystyle Q_{N}(\varphi,s)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathbbm{R}}\varphi(x)Q_{N}(x,s){\mathrm{d}}x,

\displaystyle\widetilde{Q}_{N}(\psi,s)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathbbm{R}}\psi(-y)Q_{N}(-y,s){\mathrm{d}}y,

\displaystyle R_{N}(\varphi,s)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathbbm{R}}\varphi(x)R_{N}(x,s){\mathrm{d}}x,

where
Q_{N}(x,s)
and
R_{N}(x,s)
have been introduced in (
3.5
). Then

(3.13)

\begin{split}&\sum\limits_{m=1}^{N-1}\sum\limits_{n=m}^{N-1}\Big(\int_{{\mathbbm{R}}}\varphi(x)\sqrt{N}q_{x_{N}}(m){\mathrm{d}}x\Big)\Big(\sqrt{N}Z^{\omega,\beta_{N}}_{m,n}\Big)\Big(\int_{{\mathbbm{R}}}\psi(x)\sqrt{N}q_{-y_{N}}(N-n){\mathrm{d}}y\Big)\\
=&\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{s\leq t\in\frac{1}{N}{\mathbbm{Z}}\cap(0,1)}\Big(Q_{N}+R_{N}\Big)(\varphi,s)\Big(\sqrt{N}Z^{\omega,\beta_{N}}_{sN,tN}\Big)\Big(\widetilde{Q}_{N}+R_{N}\Big)(\psi,1-t).\\
\end{split}

Assuming (i), the following weak convergence of random variables hold:

(3.14)

\begin{split}\frac{1}{N^{2}}&\sum_{s\leq t\in\frac{1}{N}{\mathbbm{Z}}\cap(0,1)}Q_{N}(\varphi,s)\Big(\sqrt{N}Z^{\omega,\beta_{N}}_{sN,tN}\Big)\widetilde{Q}(-\psi,1-t)\xlongrightarrow[N\to\infty]{({\mathrm{d}})}\iint\limits_{0<s\leq t<1}Q(\varphi,s)\mathscr{L}^{\vartheta}({\mathrm{d}}s,{\mathrm{d}}t)\widetilde{Q}(\psi,1-t),\end{split}

which is indeed (
1.27
), where

\displaystyle Q(\varphi,s):=\int_{\mathbbm{R}}\varphi(x)Q(x,s){\mathrm{d}}x\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{Q}(\psi,s):=\int_{\mathbbm{R}}\psi(-y)Q(-y,s){\mathrm{d}}y.

Similar to (
3.9
), we can show that all error terms converge to
0
. Hence, we conclude (i)
\Longrightarrow
(ii).

We then turn to (ii)
\Longrightarrow
(i). Note that by only knowing that
\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{0,N}^{\beta_{N}}(\varphi,\psi)
converges weakly, it is not straightforward to deduce (
1.27
). Hence, our strategy is to show that
Q(\varphi,s)
or
\widetilde{Q}(\psi,s)
are dense in
C([0,1])
, and then, to show
{\mathcal{Z}}_{N}^{\beta_{N}}(f,h)
can be approximated by
\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{0,N}^{\beta_{N}}(\varphi,\psi)-{\mathbbm{E}}[\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{0,N}^{\beta_{N}}(\varphi,\psi)]
.

We only treat
Q(\varphi,s)
. The case
\widetilde{Q}(\psi,s)
is exactly the same. Note that for
\varphi(x)=|x|^{2k+1}
,

(3.15)

\int_{{\mathbbm{R}}}\varphi(x)Q(x,s){\mathrm{d}}x=\int_{\mathbbm{R}}\frac{x^{2k+2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}s^{\frac{3}{2}}}e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2s}}{\mathrm{d}}x=(2k+1)!!s^{k},

which is a multiple of a power of
s
.
By Weierstrass approximation theorem, for any
f\in C([0,1])
and
\varepsilon>0
, we can
choose
\widetilde{\varphi}_{\varepsilon}(x)
of the form
\sum_{k=1}^{K_{\varepsilon}}C_{k}|x|^{2k+1}
such that

\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\Big|\int_{{\mathbbm{R}}}\widetilde{\varphi}_{\varepsilon}(x)Q(x,s){\mathrm{d}}x-f(x)\Big|<\varepsilon.

Since
Q(x,s)
has a double exponential decay in
x
, we can truncate and mollify
\widetilde{\varphi}_{\varepsilon}
to obtain some
\varphi_{\varepsilon}\in C_{c}({\mathbbm{R}})
, such that

(3.16)

\sup\limits_{t\in[0,1]}\Big|\int_{{\mathbbm{R}}}\widetilde{\varphi}_{\varepsilon}(x)Q(x,s){\mathrm{d}}x-\int_{{\mathbbm{R}}}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)Q(x,s){\mathrm{d}}x\Big|<\varepsilon,

and we have found the desired
\varphi_{\varepsilon}
.

To proceed, we write

(3.17)

\begin{split}{\mathcal{Z}}_{N}^{\beta_{N}}(f,h,\varphi,\psi):=&{\mathcal{Z}}_{N}^{\beta_{N}}(f,h)-\Big(\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{0,N}^{\beta_{N}}(\varphi,\psi)-{\mathbbm{E}}\big[\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{0,N}^{\beta_{N}}(\varphi,\psi)\big]\Big).\end{split}

We have the following estimate for (
3.17
), which concludes (ii)
\Longrightarrow
(i).

Lemma 3.1
.

Given
\varepsilon>0
, for any
f,h\in C([0,1])
, we can find
\varphi,\psi\in C_{c}({\mathbbm{R}})
, such that

\limsup\limits_{N\to\infty}{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\big({\mathcal{Z}}_{N}^{\beta_{N}}(f,h,\varphi,\psi)\big)^{2}\Big]\leq\varepsilon.

Proof.

Write

I_{m}^{(N)}(f):=I_{m}(f)=\int_{\frac{m}{N}}^{\frac{m+1}{N}}f(s){\mathrm{d}}s

and we have that

(3.18)

\begin{split}{\mathcal{Z}}_{N}^{\beta_{N}}(f,h,\varphi,\psi)=\sqrt{N}&\sum\limits_{m=1}^{N-1}\sum\limits_{n=m}^{N-1}I_{m}(f)\zeta_{m}^{\mathbbm{1}_{\{n\neq m\}}}\bigg[p_{m,n}(0)\zeta_{n}+\sum\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{n_{0}:=m\leq n_{1}<\cdots<n_{k}=n}\\
&\Big(\prod_{j=1}^{k}p_{n_{j-1},n_{j}}(0)\zeta_{n_{j}}\Big)\bigg]\bigg(I_{n}(h)-\widetilde{Q}_{N}\Big(\psi,\frac{N-n}{N}\Big)\bigg)\\
+\sqrt{N}&\sum\limits_{m=1}^{N-1}\sum\limits_{n=m}^{N-1}\bigg(I_{m}(f)-Q_{N}\Big(\varphi,\frac{m}{N}\Big)\bigg)\zeta_{m}^{\mathbbm{1}_{\{n\neq m\}}}\bigg[p_{m,n}(0)\zeta_{n}\\
&+\sum\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{n_{0}:=m\leq n_{1}<\cdots<n_{k}=n}\Big(\prod_{j=1}^{k}p_{n_{j-1},n_{j}}(0)\zeta_{n_{j}}\Big)I_{n}(h)\bigg].\end{split}

Note that for any
\varepsilon>0
, we have shown that we can find
\varphi,\psi
, such that

(3.19)

\max\bigg\{~\bigg|I_{m}(f)-Q_{N}\Big(\varphi,\frac{m}{N}\Big)\bigg|,\quad\bigg|I_{n}(h)-\widetilde{Q}_{N}\Big(\psi,\frac{N-n}{N}\Big)\bigg|~\bigg\}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{N}

uniformly in
1\leq m\leq n\leq N-1
, since
f,h
have compact supports.

Finally, by Proposition
2.3
, the second moment of
{\mathcal{Z}}_{N}^{\beta_{N}}(f,h,\varphi,\psi)
is bounded above by

C\varepsilon\big(\|f\|_{\infty}^{2}+\|h\|_{\infty}^{2}\big)\int_{0}^{1}G_{\vartheta}(t){\mathrm{d}}t,

which concludes the proof.
∎

4.
Moment estimates for
{\mathcal{Z}}_{0,N}^{\beta_{N}}(\varphi,\psi)

In this subsection, we bound the higher central moments of
\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{0,N}^{\beta_{N}}(\varphi,\psi)
. From now on, we work on
\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{0,N}^{\beta_{N}}(\varphi,\psi)
in (
2.9
). When there is no ambiguity, we omit the tilde in notations, and simply write
{\mathcal{Z}}_{0,N}^{\beta_{N}}(\varphi,\psi)
by
{\mathcal{Z}}_{N}^{\beta_{N}}(\varphi,\psi)
or
{\mathcal{Z}}_{N}
. We have the following Theorem
4.1
. A technical novelty is that, when proving Proposition
4.3
below, we relax the assumption on the random walk to the vanishing third moment and a finite fourth moment, which requires a more careful analysis to utilize the local limit theorem.

Theorem 4.1
.

For
N\leq\widetilde{N}\in{\mathbbm{N}}
, consider the averaged partition function
{\mathcal{Z}}_{N}^{\beta_{\widetilde{N}}}(\varphi,\psi)
as (
2.14
) with
\beta_{N}
replaced by
\beta_{\widetilde{N}}=\beta_{\widetilde{N}}(\vartheta)
satisfying (
1.20
). Fix
p,q\in(1+\infty)
with
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
. Then there exist constants
C_{1},C_{2}\in(0,+\infty)
, such that for
\varphi,\psi\in C_{c}({\mathbbm{R}})
, and uniformly in
N\leq\widetilde{N}
,

(4.1)

\begin{array}[]{rl}\displaystyle\Big|{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\Big({\mathcal{Z}}^{\beta_{\widetilde{N}}}_{N}(\varphi,\psi)-{\mathbbm{E}}\big[{\mathcal{Z}}^{\beta_{\widetilde{N}}}_{N}(\varphi,\psi)\big]\Big)^{h}\Big]\Big|&\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{1}}{\log(1+\frac{\widetilde{N}}{N})}\frac{1}{N^{\frac{h}{2}}}\lVert\varphi_{N}\rVert^{h}_{p}\lVert\psi_{N}\rVert^{h}_{\infty}\lVert\mathbbm{1}_{B_{N}}\rVert^{h}_{q}\\[5.0pt]
&\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{2}}{\log(1+\frac{\widetilde{N}}{N})}\lVert\varphi\rVert^{h}_{p}\lVert\psi\rVert^{h}_{\infty}\lVert\mathbbm{1}_{B}\rVert^{h}_{q},\end{array}

where
\varphi_{N},\psi_{N}:{\mathbbm{Z}}\to{\mathbbm{R}}
are defined in (
2.18
),
B
is a bounded ball that contains the support of
\psi
and
B_{N}:=B\sqrt{N}
, and
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{p}
is the
\ell_{p}
norm on
{\mathbbm{Z}}
.

Remark 4.2
.

We will apply Theorem
4.1
in Section
7
with
h=4
.

Proof.

We should keep in mind that during the following,
\zeta_{n}=\zeta^{(\widetilde{N})}_{n}
. Denote

(4.2)

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\quad M_{N,\widetilde{N},h}^{\varphi,\psi}:={\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\Big({\mathcal{Z}}_{N}^{\beta_{\widetilde{N}}}(\varphi,\psi)-{\mathbbm{E}}\big[{\mathcal{Z}}^{\beta_{\widetilde{N}}}_{N}(\varphi,\psi)\big]\Big)^{h}\Big]\\[10.0pt]
\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{\frac{h}{2}}}{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\Big(\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\sum_{0<n_{1}<\ldots<n_{r}<N}q^{N}_{0,n_{1}}(\varphi,0)\zeta_{n_{1}}\Big\{\prod_{j=2}^{r}p_{n_{j-1},n_{j}}(0,0)\zeta_{n_{j}}\Big\}q^{N}_{n_{j},N}(0,\psi)\Big)^{h}\Big].\end{array}

Note that when expanding the
h
-fold product above, the summands are transition probabilities timing a sequence of disorders at the return times to
0
for
h
random walks. By taking expectation, at each return time appearing in the summand, the disorder contributes a factor of
{\mathbbm{E}}[\zeta_{n}^{\#}]
, where
\#
is the number of random walks that return to
0
at time
n
. It is not hard to see that

(4.3)

{\mathbbm{E}}[\zeta_{n}]=0,\quad{\mathbbm{E}}[(\zeta_{n})^{2}]=\sigma_{\widetilde{N}}^{2}\sim\frac{2\pi}{\log\widetilde{N}},\quad\text{and}\quad|{\mathbbm{E}}[(\zeta_{n})^{l}]|\leq C\sigma_{\widetilde{N}}^{l}\quad\text{for}~l\geq 3.

Hence, if a summand is non-zero, then at each return time, there must be at least two random walks returning to
0
. Suppose that
l<m<n
are three consecutive return times, and one of the
h
random walks does not visit
0
at
m
. Its contribution is then only given by a transition probability
{\mathbf{P}}(S_{l}=x,S_{m}\neq 0,S_{n}=y)\leq p_{n-l}(y-x)
supposing
S_{k}=x,S_{m}=z
. That is the reason why we only consider a pinning model associated to a random walk. For a general renewal process, we lack such a simple expression for transition kernels in the renewal decomposition.

At a return time
n
, the
h
random walks can visit
m
positions, where
m=1,\cdots,h-1
.
m
cannot be
h
, since otherwise we have that
{\mathbbm{E}}[(\zeta_{n})^{1}]=0
. We then partition the indices
\{1,\cdots,h\}
into
m
sets
I:=\{I(k):k=1,\cdots,m\}
, such that
with
\cup_{k=1}^{m}I(k)=\{1,\cdots,h\}
and
I(j)\cap I(k)=\emptyset,\forall j\neq k
. For
i,j\in\{1,\cdots,h\}
, we write
i\overset{I}{\sim}j
if
i,j\in I(k)
for some
1\leq k\leq m
. In particular, we use
I(1)
to record the indices of random walks that visit
0
. Apparently, we only need to consider the case
|I(1)|\geq 2
. Hence, we have that at return time
n
,

(4.4)

{\mathbbm{E}}[\zeta^{I}]:={\mathbbm{E}}[(\zeta_{n})^{|I(1)|}].

For
\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\widetilde{x}}\in({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}
, we introduce the following
h
-fold transition kernels and the counterparts of averages (
2.15
) and (
2.16
) by:

(4.5)

Q_{t}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\widetilde{x}}):=\prod_{i=1}^{h}p_{t}(x_{i},\widetilde{x}_{i}),\quad Q_{t}^{N}(\varphi,\boldsymbol{x}):=\prod_{i=1}^{h}q_{0,t}^{N}(\varphi,x_{i}),\quad Q_{t}^{N}(\boldsymbol{x},\psi):=\prod_{i=1}^{h}q_{0,t}^{N}(x_{i},\psi).

Also, for a given partition
I
, we denote

(4.6)

\boldsymbol{x}\sim I,\quad\text{if}~x_{j}=x_{k}\Longleftrightarrow j\overset{I}{\sim}k,\quad\forall j,k=1,\cdots,h,

and for two consecutive partitions
I,J
, we denote

(4.7)

Q_{t}^{I,J}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\widetilde{x}}):=\mathbbm{1}_{\{\boldsymbol{x}\sim I,\boldsymbol{\widetilde{x}}\sim J\}}Q_{t}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\widetilde{x}}).

Finally, we can write

(4.8)

\begin{split}\quad M_{N,\widetilde{N},h}^{\varphi,\psi}=&\frac{1}{N^{\frac{h}{2}}}\sum\limits_{r=1}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{1\leq n_{1}<\cdots<n_{r}<n_{r+1}:=N\atop I_{1},\cdots,I_{r};\boldsymbol{x}_{1},\cdots,\boldsymbol{x}_{r}\in({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}}Q_{n_{1}}^{N}(\varphi,\boldsymbol{x}_{1}){\mathbbm{E}}[\zeta^{I}]\\
&\times\prod_{i=2}^{r}Q_{n_{i}-n_{i-1}}^{I_{i-1},I_{i}}(\boldsymbol{x}_{i-1},\boldsymbol{x}_{i}){\mathbbm{E}}[\zeta^{I}]\times\mathbbm{1}_{\{\boldsymbol{x}_{r}\sim I_{r}\}}Q_{n_{r+1}-n_{r}}^{N}(\boldsymbol{x}_{r},\psi).\end{split}

Note that since we are bounding
|M_{N,\widetilde{N},h}^{\varphi,\psi}|
, we will take absolute values of
\varphi,\psi
and
{\mathbbm{E}}[\zeta^{I}]
. Hence, we may assume that all these quantities are positive to lighten the notations.

Recall (
2.16
) and suppose that for
\text{supp}(\psi)\subset B
with
B_{N}=\sqrt{N}B
,

(4.9)

|q_{0,N-n_{r}}^{N}(y,\psi)|\leq\|\psi\|_{\infty}\sum\limits_{v\in{\mathbbm{Z}}}p_{N-n_{r}}(v-y)\mathbbm{1}_{B}\Big(\frac{v}{\sqrt{N}}\Big)=\|\psi\|_{\infty}{\mathbf{P}}^{y}(S_{N-n_{r}}\in\sqrt{N}B).

The probability is
O(1)
if
N-n_{r}=O(N)
. Hence, there exists a constant
C
, such that uniformly for all
y
and
1\leq n_{r}\leq N\leq n_{r+1}\leq 2N
,

\|\psi\|_{\infty}{\mathbf{P}}^{y}(S_{N-n_{r}}\in\sqrt{N}B)\leq C\|\psi\|_{\infty}{\mathbf{P}}^{y}(S_{n_{r+1}-n_{r}}\in\sqrt{N}B)=C\|\psi\|_{\infty}q_{0,n_{r+1}-n_{r}}^{N}(y,\mathbbm{1}_{B_{N}}).

By summing over
N\leq n_{r+1}\leq 2N
, we have that

(4.10)

\begin{split}\Big|M_{N,\widetilde{N},h}^{\varphi,\psi}\Big|\leq&\frac{C^{h}\|\psi\|_{\infty}^{h}}{N^{\frac{h}{2}+1}}\sum\limits_{r=1}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{1\leq n_{1}<\cdots<n_{r}\leq n_{r+1}:=2N\atop I_{1},\cdots,I_{r};\boldsymbol{x}_{1},\cdots,\boldsymbol{x}_{r}\in({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}}Q_{n_{1}}^{N}(\varphi,\boldsymbol{x}_{1}){\mathbbm{E}}[\zeta^{I_{1}}]\\
&\times\prod_{i=2}^{r}Q_{n_{i}-n_{i-1}}^{I_{i-1},I_{i}}(\boldsymbol{x}_{i-1},\boldsymbol{x}_{i}){\mathbbm{E}}[\zeta^{I_{i}}]\times\mathbbm{1}_{\{\boldsymbol{x}_{r}\sim I_{r}\}}Q_{n_{r+1}-n_{r}}^{N}(\boldsymbol{x}_{r},\mathbbm{1}_{B_{N}}).\end{split}

We first single out a special case that some consecutive
I_{i},I_{i+1},\dots,I_{j}
are the same with
|I_{i}|=\cdots=|I_{j}|=h-1
. Note that in this case there exist
1\leq m\neq l\leq h
, such that,
I_{k}(1)=\{m,l\}
for
k=i,i+1,\cdots,j
, namely, random walks with indices
m
and
l
keep returning to
0
consecutively at all return times, while other walks never return to
0
.

We can simplify the notation for this case. Denote the common partition of
I_{i},\cdots,I_{j}
by
I
. For
1\leq s\leq t\leq 2N
and
\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\widetilde{x}}\in({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}
, we introduce

(4.11)

U^{I}_{t-s,\widetilde{N}}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\widetilde{x}}):=\mathbbm{1}_{\{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\widetilde{x}}\sim I\}}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}{\mathbbm{E}}[\zeta^{2}]^{k}\sum\limits_{n_{0}:=s<n_{1}<\cdots<n_{k}:=t\atop\boldsymbol{y}_{i}\in({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h},\boldsymbol{y}_{0}:=\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y}_{k}:=\boldsymbol{\widetilde{x}}}\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}Q_{n_{i}-n_{i-1}}^{I,I}(\boldsymbol{y}_{i-1},\boldsymbol{y}_{i}).

Note that here
\zeta
depends on
\widetilde{N}
and
U_{0,\widetilde{N}}^{I}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\widetilde{x}})
is understood as
\mathbbm{1}_{\{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\widetilde{x}}\sim I\}}
. Then in (
4.10
), for any given first and last return times
s,t
, we can contract all consecutive
I_{i}=I_{i+1}=\cdots=I_{j}:=I
for any
j-i\in{\mathbbm{N}}
to a single kernel
U_{t-s}^{I}(\cdot,\cdot)
. Recall
\overline{U}_{N}(n)
from (
2.24
), and we write

(4.12)

U_{N}(n)=\overline{U}_{N}(n)/\sigma_{N}^{2}.

If
I(1)=\{m,l\}
, then we have that

(4.13)

U^{I}_{t-s,\widetilde{N}}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\widetilde{x}})\leq U_{\widetilde{N}}(t-s)\prod_{i\in\{1,\cdots,h\}\backslash\{m,l\}}p_{s,t}(x_{i},\widetilde{x}_{i}).

Before we continue, we introduce some extra notations for summing over
1\leq n_{1}<\cdots<n_{r}\leq 2N
in (
4.10
). For partitions
I,J
, let

(4.14)

\displaystyle Q_{\lambda,N}^{I,J}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\widetilde{x}})

\displaystyle:=\sum\limits_{n=1}^{2N}e^{-\lambda n}Q_{n}^{I,J}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\widetilde{x}}),\quad\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\widetilde{x}}\in({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h},

(4.15)

\displaystyle U_{\lambda,N,\widetilde{N}}^{J}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\widetilde{x}})

\displaystyle:=\sum\limits_{n=1}^{2N}e^{-\lambda n}U_{n,\widetilde{N}}^{J}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\widetilde{x}}),\quad\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\widetilde{x}}\in({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}.

Then we define the operators from
({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}\rightarrow({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}
by

(4.16)

P_{\lambda}^{I,J}=P_{\lambda,N,\widetilde{N}}^{I,J}:=\begin{cases}Q_{\lambda,N}^{I,J},\quad&\text{if}~|J|<h-1,\\
Q_{\lambda,N}^{I,J}U_{\lambda,N,\widetilde{N}}^{J},\quad&\text{if}~|J|=h-1.\end{cases}

Now in each summand on the right-hand side of (
4.10
), we insert a term
e^{2\lambda N}e^{-\lambda\sum_{i=1}^{r+1}(n_{i}-n_{i-1})}\geq 1
, where
\lambda>0
will be determined later. For the initial and terminal times, there is no constraint on the positions of the random walks, and we denote the “partition” by
*
. Finally, (
4.10
) can be further bounded above by

(4.17)

\Big|M_{N,\widetilde{N},h}^{\varphi,\psi}\Big|\leq\frac{C_{h}\|\psi\|_{\infty}^{h}e^{2\lambda N}}{N^{\frac{h}{2}+1}}\sum\limits_{r=1}^{\infty}\sum_{I_{1},\cdots,I_{r}}\big\langle\varphi_{N}^{\otimes h},P^{*,I_{1}}_{\lambda}P^{I_{1},I_{2}}_{\lambda}\cdots P_{\lambda}^{I_{r-1},I_{r}}Q_{\lambda}^{I_{r},*}\mathbbm{1}_{B_{N}}^{\otimes h}\big\rangle\prod_{i=1}^{r}{\mathbbm{E}}[\zeta^{I_{i}}].

Here the summation is over all partitions
I_{1},\cdots,I_{r}
of
\{1,\cdots,h\}
, such that
|I_{i}|\leq h-1
and no consecutive
I_{i}=I_{i+1}
with
|I_{i}|=h-1
. The operation
\big\langle\cdot,\cdot\big\rangle
is the classic notation for integral on
({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}\otimes({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}
. Given a function
f:{\mathbbm{Z}}\rightarrow{\mathbbm{R}}
, for
\boldsymbol{x}\in({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}
,
f^{\otimes h}(\boldsymbol{x}):=\prod_{i=1}^{h}f(x_{i})
.

For
p,q\in(1,+\infty)
such that
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
, by Hölder’s inequality, (
4.17
) is bounded above by

(4.18)

\begin{split}\Big|M_{N,\widetilde{N},h}^{\varphi,\psi}\Big|\leq\frac{C_{h}\|\psi\|_{\infty}^{h}\|\varphi\|_{p}^{h}e^{2\lambda N}}{N^{\frac{h}{2}+1}}\sum\limits_{r=1}^{\infty}&\sum_{I_{1},\cdots,I_{r}}\Big\|P_{\lambda}^{*,I_{1}}\Big\|_{\ell^{q}\to\ell^{q}}\Big\|P_{\lambda}^{I_{1},I_{2}}\Big\|_{\ell^{q}\to\ell^{q}}\cdots\\
&\cdots\Big\|P_{\lambda}^{I_{r-1},I_{r}}\Big\|_{\ell^{q}\to\ell^{q}}\Big\|Q_{\lambda}^{I_{r},*}\Big\|_{\ell^{q}\to\ell^{q}}\Big\|\mathbbm{1}_{B_{N}}^{\otimes h}\Big\|_{q}\prod\limits_{i=1}^{r}{\mathbbm{E}}[\zeta^{I_{i}}],\end{split}

where the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\ell^{q}\to\ell^{q}}
for the operator
A:({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}\rightarrow({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}
is defined by

\|A\|_{\ell^{q}\to\ell^{q}}:=\sup\limits_{f\not\equiv 0}\frac{\|Af\|_{q}}{\|f\|_{q}}.

By choosing
\lambda:=\widehat{\lambda}/N
with
\widehat{\lambda}
large but fixed (to be determined later), we have the following proposition to control all the norms above.

Proposition 4.3
.

Let all the conditions in Theorem
4.1
be satisfied. There exists some constant
c=c_{p,q,h,\widehat{\lambda}}\in(0,+\infty)
for
h\geq 3
, such that uniformly for partitions
I,J
for
\{1,\cdots,h\}
with
1\leq|I|,|J|\leq h-1
and
I\neq J
for
|I|=|J|=h-1
, we have that for large
N\leq\widetilde{N}
and
\lambda=\frac{\widehat{\lambda}}{{N}}
,

(4.19)

\displaystyle\Big\|Q_{\lambda,N}^{I,J}\Big\|_{\ell^{q}\to\ell^{q}}\leq c,

(4.20)

\displaystyle\Big\|Q_{\lambda,N}^{*,I}\Big\|_{\ell^{q}\to\ell^{q}}

\displaystyle\leq cN^{\frac{1}{q}},\quad\Big\|Q_{\lambda,N}^{I,*}\Big\|_{\ell^{q}\to\ell^{q}}\leq cN^{\frac{1}{p}},

(4.21)

\displaystyle\text{in particular, for}~|I|=h-1,\quad

\displaystyle\Big\|U_{\lambda,N,\widetilde{N}}^{J}\Big\|_{\ell^{q}\to\ell^{q}}\leq\frac{c}{\log\big(\widehat{\lambda}\frac{\widetilde{N}}{N}\big)\sigma_{\widetilde{N}}^{2}}.

We postpone the proof of this proposition and apply it to completing the proof of Theorem
4.1
first. For
h\geq 3
, note that
{\mathbbm{E}}[\zeta^{I}]=\sigma_{\widetilde{N}}^{2}
for
|I|=h-1
and
|{\mathbbm{E}}[\zeta^{I}]|\leq c^{\prime}\sigma_{\widetilde{N}}^{3}=O((\log\widetilde{N})^{-{3/2}})
for
|I|<h-1
. Then the above bounds for operators imply that for
2\leq i\leq r
,

(4.22)

\displaystyle{\mathbbm{E}}[\zeta^{I_{i}}]\Big\|P^{I_{i-1},I_{i}}_{\lambda}\Big\|_{\ell^{q}\to\ell^{q}}\leq\mathbbm{1}_{\{|I_{i}|=h-1\}}\frac{c^{2}}{\log\big(\widehat{\lambda}\frac{\widetilde{N}}{N}\big)}+\mathbbm{1}_{\{|I_{i}|<h-1\}}cc^{\prime}\sigma_{\widetilde{N}}^{3}\leq\frac{c^{\prime\prime}}{\log\big(\widehat{\lambda}\frac{\widetilde{N}}{N}\big)},

and similarly,

(4.23)

\displaystyle{\mathbbm{E}}[\zeta^{I_{1}}]\Big\|P^{\ast,I_{1}}_{\lambda}\Big\|_{\ell^{q}\to\ell^{q}}\leq N^{\frac{1}{q}}\Big(\mathbbm{1}_{\{|I_{1}|=h-1\}}\frac{c}{\log\big(\widehat{\lambda}\frac{\widetilde{N}}{N}\big)}+\mathbbm{1}_{\{|I_{1}|<h-1\}}c^{\prime}\sigma_{\widetilde{N}}^{3}\Big)\leq\frac{c^{\prime\prime}}{\log(\widehat{\lambda}\frac{\widetilde{N}}{N})}N^{\frac{1}{q}}.

For fixed
h
, the number of partitions
I_{i}
is bounded by a constant
c_{h}
.
Choose
\widehat{\lambda}
large such that
c_{h}\frac{c^{\prime\prime}}{\log\widehat{\lambda}}\leq\frac{1}{2}
. Plug the bounds (
4.22
) and (
4.23
) into (
4.18
), and we have that for
N
large enough,

(4.24)

\big|M_{N,\widetilde{N},h}^{\varphi,\psi}\big|\displaystyle\leq\frac{Ce^{2\widehat{\lambda}}\lVert\psi\rVert^{h}_{\infty}}{N^{\frac{h}{2}+1}}\big\|\varphi_{N}\big\|^{h}_{\ell^{p}}\|\mathbbm{1}_{B_{N}}\big\rVert^{h}_{\ell^{q}}N^{\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}}\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\Big(\frac{c_{h}c^{\prime\prime}}{\log(\widehat{\lambda}\frac{\widetilde{N}}{N})}\Big)^{r}\!\!\leq\!\!\frac{C_{1}}{N^{\frac{h}{2}}\log(1+\frac{\widetilde{N}}{N})}\big\|\psi\big\rVert^{h}_{\infty}\big\|\varphi_{N}\big\rVert^{h}_{\ell^{p}}\big\lVert\mathbbm{1}_{B_{N}}\big\rVert^{h}_{\ell^{q}}.

∎

It only remains to prove Proposition
4.3
. We start by bounding the following quantity

(4.25)

Q_{\lambda,N}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y}):=\sum_{n=1}^{2N}e^{-\lambda n}\prod_{i=1}^{h}p_{n}(y_{i}-x_{i}),

which is actually
Q_{\lambda,N}^{*,*}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})
, a partition-free version of (
4.14
). Since
Q_{N}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y}):=Q_{0,N}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})=\sup_{\lambda\geq 0}Q_{\lambda,N}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})
, we only need to bound
Q_{N}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})=\sum_{n=1}^{2N}\prod_{i=1}^{h}p_{n}(y_{i}-x_{i})
.

Note that
{\mathbf{E}}[|S_{1}|^{4}]<+\infty
and
{\mathbf{E}}[(S_{1})^{3}]=0
. By
[
LL10
, Theorem 2.3.10]
, there exists a constant
C\in(0,+\infty)
, such that uniformly in
x
and
n
,

(4.26)

p_{n}(x)\leq g_{n}(x)+\frac{C}{(1+|x|^{2})\sqrt{n}},

where
g_{t}(x)
is the heat kernel (see (
1.31
)). Then we have that

(4.27)

\begin{split}Q_{N}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})&\leq\sum\limits_{n=1}^{2N}\prod\limits_{i=1}^{h}\Big(g_{n}(y_{i}-x_{i})+\frac{C}{(1+|y_{i}-x_{i}|^{2})\sqrt{n}}\Big)\\
&=\sum\limits_{n=1}^{2N}\prod\limits_{i=1}^{h}g_{n}(y_{i}-x_{i})+\sum\limits_{n=1}^{2N}\sum\limits_{K\subsetneqq\{1,\cdots,h\}}\prod\limits_{i\in K}g_{n}(y_{i}-x_{i})\prod\limits_{j\notin K}\frac{C}{(1+|y_{j}-x_{j}|^{2})\sqrt{n}}.\end{split}

To bound the above quantity, we need
[
CSZ23
, Lemma 6.7]
, which shows that there exists some constant
C\in(0,+\infty)
, such that uniformly in
x,y,N
and
K\geq 3
,

(4.28)

\sum\limits_{i=1}^{2N}\prod\limits_{i\in K}g_{n}(y_{i}-x_{i})\leq\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{C}{(1+|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|^{2})^{\frac{|K|}{2}-1}},\quad&\text{for all}~\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y}\in({\mathbbm{Z}})^{|K|},\\[16.0pt]
\displaystyle\frac{C}{N^{\frac{|K|}{2}-1}}e^{-\frac{|\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{x}|^{2}}{CN}},\quad&\text{for}~|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|\geq\sqrt{N}.\end{cases}

Now we can first treat (
4.19
), that is,
\|Q_{\lambda,N}^{I,J}\|_{\ell^{q}\to\ell^{q}}
.

Proof of (
4.19
)
.

Note that this is equivalent to proving that there exists some constant
c\in(0,+\infty)
, such that uniformly for all
\varphi\in\ell^{p}(({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}_{I})
and
\psi\in\ell^{q}(({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}_{J})
,

(4.29)

\sum\limits_{\boldsymbol{x}\in({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}_{I},\boldsymbol{y}\in({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}_{J}}\varphi(\boldsymbol{x})Q_{\lambda}^{I,J}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})\psi(\boldsymbol{y})\leq c\|\varphi\|_{\ell^{p}}\|\psi\|_{\ell^{q}}.

Note that
n^{-1/2}=\sqrt{2\pi}g_{n}(0)
. By combining
g_{n}(y_{i}-x_{i})
for
i\in K
and
n^{-1/2}
for
j\notin K
, and by (
4.27
) and (
4.28
) together, we have that

(4.30)

\begin{split}&\sum\limits_{\boldsymbol{x}\in({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}_{I},\boldsymbol{y}\in({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}_{J}}\varphi(\boldsymbol{x})Q_{\lambda}^{I,J}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})\psi(\boldsymbol{y})\leq\sum\limits_{\boldsymbol{x}\in({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}_{I},\boldsymbol{y}\in({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}_{J}}\varphi(\boldsymbol{x})\frac{C}{(1+|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|^{2})^{\frac{h}{2}-1}}\psi(\boldsymbol{y})\\
&+\sum\limits_{K\subsetneqq\{1,\cdots,h\}}\sum\limits_{\boldsymbol{x}\in({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}_{I},\boldsymbol{y}\in({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}_{J}}\varphi(\boldsymbol{x})\bigg(\frac{C}{(1+\sum_{i\in K}|y_{i}-x_{i}|^{2})^{\frac{h}{2}-1}}\prod\limits_{j\notin K}\frac{C}{(1+|y_{j}-x_{j}|^{2})}\bigg)\psi(\boldsymbol{y}).\end{split}

Hence, we only need to show that both terms on the right-hand side above is bounded by
C\|\varphi\|_{\ell^{p}}\|\psi\|_{\ell^{q}}
.

The result then follows by the following lemma.

Lemma 4.4
.

Fix
p,q>1
with
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
and an integer
h\geq 3
.
Consider partitions
I,J
with
1\leq|I|,|J|\leq h-1
, and
I\neq J
if
|I|=|J|=h-1
.
Let
\varphi\in\ell^{p}(({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}_{I})
and
\psi\in\ell^{q}(({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}_{J})
.
Then there exists
C=C_{p,q,h}<\infty
, independent of
f
and
g
, such that

(4.31)

\sum\limits_{\boldsymbol{x}\in({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}_{I},\boldsymbol{y}\in({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}_{J}}\frac{\varphi(\boldsymbol{x})\psi(\boldsymbol{y})}{(1+|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|^{2})^{\frac{h}{2}-1}}\leq C\|\varphi\|_{\ell^{p}}\|\psi\|_{\ell^{q}},

and

(4.32)

\sum\limits_{K\subsetneqq\{1,\cdots,h\}}\sum\limits_{\boldsymbol{x}\in({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}_{I},\boldsymbol{y}\in({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}_{J}}\varphi(\boldsymbol{x})\bigg(\frac{C}{(1+\sum_{i\in K}|y_{i}-x_{i}|^{2})^{\frac{h}{2}-1}}\prod\limits_{j\notin K}\frac{C}{(1+|y_{j}-x_{j}|^{2})}\bigg)\psi(\boldsymbol{y})\leq C\|\varphi\|_{\ell^{p}}\|\psi\|_{\ell^{q}}.

Next we devote to prove Lemma
4.4
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
4.4
.

In the proof, we will repeatedly use the bound

(4.33)

\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{(s+x^{2})^{r}}\leq\frac{C}{s^{r-1/2}},\quad\text{with a uniform $C>0$ for}~s>1,r>\frac{1}{2}.

We first treat (
4.31
) with
|I|=|J|=h-1
. Assume that the index sets for renewal random walks are
I(1)=\{k,l\}
and
J(1)=\{m,n\}
, with
\{k,l\}\neq\{m,n\}
. Choose
0<a<\frac{1}{2(p\vee q)}
. By Höder’s inequality, the left-hand side of (
4.31
) is bounded from above by

(4.34)

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\left(\sum\limits_{\boldsymbol{x}\in({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}_{I},\boldsymbol{y}\in({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}_{J}}\frac{\varphi(\boldsymbol{x})^{p}}{(1+\sum_{i=1}^{h}|x_{i}-y_{i}|^{2})^{\frac{h}{2}-1}}\cdot\frac{(1+|x_{m}-x_{n}|^{2a})^{p}}{(1+|y_{k}-y_{l}|^{2a})^{p}}\right)^{1/p}\\[5.0pt]
\displaystyle\qquad\times\left(\sum\limits_{\boldsymbol{x}\in({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}_{I},\boldsymbol{y}\in({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}_{J}}\frac{\psi(\boldsymbol{y})^{q}}{(1+\sum_{i=1}^{h}|x_{i}-y_{i}|^{2})^{\frac{h}{2}-1}}\cdot\frac{(1+|y_{k}-y_{l}|^{2a})^{q}}{(1+|x_{m}-x_{n}|^{2a})^{q}}\right)^{1/q}.\end{array}

We split the cases into (i)
\{k,l\}\cap\{m,n\}=\emptyset
and (ii)
k=m,l\neq n
.

Proof for Case (i).
For the first factor above, note that
x_{k}=x_{l}=y_{m}=y_{n}=0
in
({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}_{J}
. Use (
4.33
) to sum over the remaining
h-4
variables
y_{j}
with
j\neq k,l,m,n
. We get an upper bound

(4.35)

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle C\Bigg(\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}\in({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}_{I}}f(\boldsymbol{x})^{p}(1+|x_{m}-x_{n}|^{2a})^{p}\sum_{y_{k},y_{l}\in{\mathbbm{Z}}}\frac{1}{(1+x_{m}^{2}+x_{n}^{2}+y_{k}^{2}+y_{l}^{2})^{\frac{h}{2}-1-\frac{h-4}{2}}(1+|y_{k}-y_{l}|^{2a})^{p}}\Bigg)^{1/p}.\end{array}

Let
\widetilde{y}_{1}:=y_{k}-y_{l}
and
\widetilde{y}_{2}:=y_{k}+y_{l}
. By
\widetilde{y}_{1}^{2}+\widetilde{y}_{2}^{2}=2(y_{k}^{2}+y_{l}^{2})
, the inner sum in (
4.35
) is bounded by

(4.36)

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\sum_{\widetilde{y}_{1},\widetilde{y}_{2}}\frac{C}{(1+x_{m}^{2}+x_{n}^{2}+\widetilde{y}_{1}^{2}+\widetilde{y}_{2}^{2})(1+\widetilde{y}_{1}^{2a})^{p}}\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\widetilde{y}_{1}}\frac{C}{(1+x_{m}^{2}+x_{n}^{2}+\widetilde{y}_{1}^{2})^{1/2}(1+\widetilde{y}_{1}^{2a})^{p}}\leq\frac{C}{(1+x_{m}^{2}+x_{n}^{2})^{ap}},\end{array}

where the last inequality is achieved by summing over
|\widetilde{y}_{1}|\leq\sqrt{x_{m}^{2}+x_{n}^{2}}
and
|\widetilde{y}_{1}|>\sqrt{x_{m}^{2}+x_{n}^{2}}
separately. Then
(1+|x_{m}-x_{n}|^{2a})^{p}
and (
4.36
) cancel out, and thus the first factor of (
4.34
) is upper bounded by
C\lVert\varphi\rVert_{\ell^{p}}
. The second factor of (
4.34
) can similarly bounded by
C\|\psi\|_{\ell^{q}}
.

Proof for Case (ii).
For the first factor in (
4.34
), note that
x_{m}=x_{l}=y_{m}=y_{n}=0
. Sum over remaining
h-3
variables
y_{j}
with
j\neq l,m,n
.
Similarly, we get an upper bound

(4.37)

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\quad C\Bigg(\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}\in({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}_{I}}\varphi(\boldsymbol{x})^{p}(1+|x_{n}|^{2a})^{p}\sum_{y_{l}}\frac{1}{(1+x_{n}^{2}+y_{l}^{2})^{1/2}(1+y_{l}^{2a})^{p}}\Bigg)^{1/p}\leq C\|\varphi\|_{\ell^{p}},\\[5.0pt]
\end{array}

where we sum over
|y_{l}|\leq|x_{n}|
and
|y_{l}|\geq|x_{n}|
separately. The second factor of (
4.34
) can be treated in the same way.

When
\min\{|I|,|J|\}<h-1
, the proof is easier. If
|I|,|J|<h-1
, then we have two patterns: either there are two visits to
0
and two visits to the same other position, or there are three visits to
0
. In either case, we only need to sum over
x_{i}
or
y_{j}
for at most
h-3
times
. Hence, we do not need to introduce the factor
(1+|x_{m}-x_{n}|^{2a})/(1+|y_{k}-y_{l}|^{2a})
. If
|I|<h-1
and
|J|=h-1
, then we can find
k,l
belonging to the same partition element of
I
with
k,j\notin J(1)
. Then we can replace the factor
(1+|x_{m}-x_{n}|^{2a})/(1+|y_{k}-y_{l}|^{2a})
in (
4.34
) by
1/(1+|y_{k}-y_{l}|^{2a})
(and also its reciprocal). The rest of the proof is then the same as that for
|I|=|J|=h-1
.

Then we turn to (
4.32
). The treatment is similar as above. If
|I|=|J|=h-1
, then we have
Case (1)
and
Case (2)
as above. We will insert different factors according to
k,l,m,n
and
K
:

Recall that in
Case (i)
,
I(1)=\{k,l\}
and
J(1)=\{m,n\}
with
\{k,l\}\cap\{m,n\}=\emptyset
.
We can insert a factor according to the following strategy:

•

If
k,l\in K
, then we insert
1+|y_{k}|^{2a}
and its reciprocal.

•

If
k
or
l
does not belong to
K
, then we do not need to insert anything related to
y_{j}
.

•

Similarly, if
m,l\in K
, then we insert
1+|x_{m}|^{2a}
and its reciprocal.

•

If
m
or
n
does not belong to
K
, then we do not need to insert anything related to
x_{i}
.

The reason behind this strategy is that when
j\notin K
,
(1+|y_{j}-x_{j}|^{2})^{-1}
is summable and we save the degree of power by
\frac{1}{2}
.

The
Case (2)
and the case
\min\{|I|,|J|\}<h-1
can be treated similarly and we omit the details.
∎

Then we treat (
4.19
), that is,
\|Q_{\lambda,N}^{*,I}\|_{\ell^{q}\to\ell^{q}}
and
\|Q_{\lambda,N}^{I,*}\|_{\ell^{p}\to\ell^{p}}
. The proof is similar to that of (
4.19
) and thus we only sketch it.

Proof sketch for (
4.20
)
.

We only treat
Q_{\lambda,N}^{I,*}
, since the treatment for
Q_{\lambda,N}^{*,I}
is the same. It suffices to prove that there exits come constant
c\in(0,+\infty)
, such that for
h\geq 3
and all
\varphi\in\ell^{p}(({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}_{I})
and
\psi\in\ell^{q}(({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}_{J})
,

(4.38)

\sum\limits_{\boldsymbol{x}\in({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}_{I},\boldsymbol{y}\in({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}}\varphi(\boldsymbol{x})Q^{I,*}_{\lambda,N}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})\psi(\boldsymbol{y})\leq cN^{\frac{1}{p}}\|\varphi\|_{\ell^{p}}\|\psi\big\|_{\ell^{q}}

uniformly for all
\varphi\in\ell^{p}(({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}_{I})
and
\psi\in\ell^{p}(({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}_{J})
.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
1,2\in I(1)
so that
x_{1}=x_{2}=0
. By (
4.27
), we first treat the kernel
\sum_{n=1}^{2N}\prod_{i=1}^{h}g_{n}(y_{i}-x_{i})
. We split the cases
|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|\leq\sqrt{N}
and
|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|\geq\sqrt{N}
. The latter case is simple by the exponential decay in (
4.28
). For
|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|\leq\sqrt{N}
, we insert a factor
\log(1+\frac{N}{y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2}})^{1/q}
and its reciprocal, and then apply the Hölder’s inequality as in (
4.34
). The bound then follows by noting that
\int(c+x^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}{\mathrm{d}}x=\ln(x+\sqrt{c+x^{2}})
.

Then for any give
K\subsetneqq\{1,\cdots,h\}
, we are going to bound

(4.39)

\sum\limits_{\boldsymbol{x}\in({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}_{I},\boldsymbol{y}\in({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}}f(\boldsymbol{x})\bigg(\sum\limits_{n=1}^{2N}\prod\limits_{i\in K}g_{n}(y_{i}-x_{i})\prod\limits_{j\notin K}\frac{C}{(1+|y_{j}-x_{j}|^{2})\sqrt{n}}\bigg)g(\boldsymbol{y}),

which can also be done by splitting the cases
|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|\leq\sqrt{N}
and
|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|\geq\sqrt{N}
. For
|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|\leq\sqrt{N}
, if
1,2\in K
, then we insert a factor
\log(1+\frac{N}{y_{1}^{2}})
and its reciprocal. If either
1
or
2
does not belong to
K
, then we do not need to insert any factor.

∎

Finally, we treat (
4.21
), that is,
\|U_{\lambda,N,\widetilde{N}}^{I}\|_{\ell^{q}\to\ell^{q}}
.

Proof of (
4.21
)
.

It suffices to prove that there exists some constant
c\in(0,+\infty)
, such that

(4.40)

\sum\limits_{\boldsymbol{x}\in({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}_{I},\boldsymbol{y}\in({\mathbbm{Z}})_{I}^{h}}\varphi(\boldsymbol{x})U_{\lambda,N,\widetilde{N}}^{I}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})\psi(\boldsymbol{y})\leq\frac{c}{\log\big(\widehat{\lambda}\frac{\widetilde{N}}{N}\big)\sigma_{\widetilde{N}}^{2}}\|\varphi\|_{\ell^{p}}\|\psi\big\|_{\ell^{q}}

uniformly for all
\varphi\in\ell^{p}(({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}_{I})
and
\psi\in\ell^{p}(({\mathbbm{Z}})^{h}_{J})
.

Recall (
4.15
) and (
4.13
) and we assume that
I(1)=\{1,2\}
, that is, only the random walks with indices 1 and 2 visits
0
. We have that

U_{\frac{\widehat{\lambda}}{N},N,\widetilde{N}}^{I}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})\leq\mathbbm{1}_{\{\boldsymbol{x}=\boldsymbol{y}\}}+\sum_{n=1}^{2N}e^{-\widehat{\lambda}\frac{n}{N}}U_{\widetilde{N}}(n)\prod_{i=3}^{h}p_{n}(y_{i}-x_{i})\leq 1+\sum\limits_{n=1}^{2N}e^{-\widehat{\lambda}\frac{n}{N}}U_{\widetilde{N}}(n),

where
U_{\widetilde{N}}(n,x)
is defined in (
4.12
) with
\sigma_{N}^{2}
replaced by
\sigma_{\widetilde{N}}^{2}\sim\sqrt{2\pi}(\log\widetilde{N})^{-1}
.

By (
2.26
) and setting
T:=\frac{\widetilde{N}}{N}\geq 1
, we have that

\begin{split}\sum\limits_{\boldsymbol{y}\in({\mathbbm{Z}})_{I}^{h}}U_{\frac{\widehat{\lambda}}{N},N,\widetilde{N}}^{I}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})\leq 1+C\log\widetilde{N}\sum\limits_{n=1}^{2\widetilde{N}/T}e^{-\widehat{\lambda}\frac{nT}{\widetilde{N}}}G_{\vartheta}\Big(\frac{n}{\widetilde{N}}\Big)\frac{1}{\widetilde{N}}\leq 1+C\log\widetilde{N}\int_{0}^{\frac{2}{T}}e^{-\widehat{\lambda}Tt}G_{\vartheta}(t){\mathrm{d}}t.\end{split}

By (
2.28
),
G_{\vartheta}(t)
is uniformly bounded on
[\frac{1}{2},2]
and
G_{\vartheta}(t)\sim[t(\log\frac{1}{t})^{2}]^{-1}
as
t\to 0
. We obtain that

\begin{split}\int_{0}^{\frac{2}{T}}e^{-\widehat{\lambda}Tt}G_{\vartheta}(t){\mathrm{d}}t&\leq C\int_{\frac{1}{2}}^{2}e^{-\widehat{\lambda}Tt}{\mathrm{d}}t+\int_{0}^{(\widehat{\lambda}T)^{-\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}t}{t(\log\frac{1}{t})^{2}}+e^{-\sqrt{\widehat{\lambda}T}}\int_{(\widehat{\lambda}T)^{-\frac{1}{2}}}^{\frac{1}{2}\wedge\frac{2}{T}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}t}{t(\log\frac{1}{t})^{2}}\leq\frac{C}{\log\widehat{\lambda}T},\end{split}

which yields (
4.40
) by Hölder’s inequality and
\sigma_{\widetilde{N}}^{2}\leq C(\log\widetilde{N})^{-1}
.
∎

5.
Approximating
{\mathcal{Z}}_{N}(\varphi,\psi)
by the coarse-grained model
\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(cg)}}(\varphi,\psi)

In this section, we approximate
{\mathcal{Z}}_{N}
by
{\mathcal{Z}}_{N,\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(no triple)}}(\varphi,\psi)
, and then approximate
{\mathcal{Z}}_{N,\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(no triple)}}(\varphi,\psi)
by
{\mathcal{Z}}_{N,\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(cg)}}(\varphi,\psi)
, and finally approximate
{\mathcal{Z}}_{N,\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(cg)}}(\varphi,\psi)
by
\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(cg)}}(\varphi,\psi|\Theta)
.

5.1.
Approximating
{\mathcal{Z}}_{N}
by
{\mathcal{Z}}_{N,\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(no triple)}}

Recall
{\mathcal{Z}}_{N,\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(no triple)}}
from (
2.41
). We control the
L_{2}
distance between
{\mathcal{Z}}_{N}
and
{\mathcal{Z}}_{N,\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(no triple)}}
, which is

(5.1)

\begin{split}&\Big\|\Big({\mathcal{Z}}_{N,\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(no triple)}}-{\mathcal{Z}}_{N}\Big)(\varphi,\psi)\Big\|_{2}^{2}=\Big(\sum\limits_{{\mathcal{C}}_{1}}+\sum\limits_{{\mathcal{C}}_{2}}\Big)\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{d_{1}\leq f_{1}\in{\mathcal{T}}_{N,\varepsilon}(\mathrm{i}_{1}),\cdots,d_{k}\leq f_{k}\in{\mathcal{T}}_{N,\varepsilon}(\mathrm{i}_{k})}\\
&\left(q_{0,d_{1}}^{N}(\varphi,0)\right)^{2}\overline{U}_{N}(f_{1}-d_{1})\Big\{\prod\limits_{j=2}^{k}u(d_{j}-f_{j-1})\overline{U}_{N}(f_{j}-d_{j})\Big\}\left(q_{f_{k},N}^{N}(0,\psi)\right)^{2},\end{split}

where

{\mathcal{C}}_{1}:=\bigcup\limits_{k>(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon})^{2}}\Big\{0<\mathrm{i}_{1}<\cdots<\mathrm{i}_{k}<\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\Big\},\quad{\mathcal{C}}_{2}:=\bigcup\limits_{k=1}^{(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon})^{2}}\Big\{(\mathrm{i}_{1},\cdots,\mathrm{i}_{k})\in\Big({\mathcal{A}}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(no triple)}}\Big)^{c}\Big\},

and
\overline{U}_{N}(n)
and
u(n)
are defined by (
2.24
) and (
2.20
). We denote the summation on
{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
by
I_{N,\varepsilon}
and
J_{N,\varepsilon}
respectively, and we have the following upper bounds.

Lemma 5.1
.

We have that for some
C\in(0,+\infty)
, which may depend on
\vartheta
, such that

(5.2)

\displaystyle\limsup\limits_{N\to\infty}I_{N,\varepsilon}\leq\frac{C}{\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}\|\varphi\|_{2}^{2}\|\psi\|_{\infty}^{2},

(5.3)

\displaystyle\limsup\limits_{N\to\infty}J_{N,\varepsilon}\leq C\bigg(\int_{0}^{K_{\varepsilon}\varepsilon}{\mathrm{d}}s\int_{\mathbbm{R}}g_{s}^{2}(x){\mathrm{d}}x+\frac{(\log K_{\varepsilon})^{2}}{\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}\bigg)\|\varphi\|_{2}^{2}\|\psi\|_{\infty}^{2}.

Consequently,

\limsup\limits_{N\to\infty}\left\|\left({\mathcal{Z}}_{N,\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(no triple)}}-{\mathcal{Z}}_{N}\right)(\varphi,\psi)\right\|_{2}^{2}\leq C\bigg(\int_{0}^{K_{\varepsilon}\varepsilon}{\mathrm{d}}s\int_{\mathbbm{R}}g_{s}^{2}(x){\mathrm{d}}x+\frac{(\log K_{\varepsilon})^{2}}{\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}\bigg)\|\varphi\|_{2}^{2}\|\psi\|_{\infty}^{2}.

Proof sketch.

First, note that

q_{f_{k},N}^{N}(0,\psi)=\sum\limits_{v\in 2{\mathbbm{Z}}}p_{N-f_{k}}(v)\psi_{N}(v)\leq\|\psi_{N}\|_{\infty}\sum\limits_{v\in{\mathbbm{Z}}}p_{N-f_{k}}(v)\leq\|\psi\|_{\infty}.

Next, we can use the following lemma to control the inner summation in (
5.1
).

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
{\mathbf{I}}:=(\mathrm{i}_{1},\cdots,\mathrm{i}_{k})\subset\{1,\cdots,1/\varepsilon\}
with
\mathrm{i}_{1}<\cdots<\mathrm{i}_{k}
. We have that

(5.4)

\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{d_{1}\leq f_{1}\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon,N}(\mathrm{i}_{1}),\cdots,d_{k}\leq f_{k}\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon,N}(\mathrm{i}_{k})}\left(q_{0,d_{1}}^{N}(\varphi,0)\right)^{2}\overline{U}_{N}(f_{1}-d_{1})\bigg\{\prod\limits_{j=2}^{k}u(d_{j}-f_{j-1})\overline{U}_{N}(f_{j}-d_{j})\bigg\}={\mathcal{I}}_{\varepsilon}^{\varphi}({\mathbf{I}})

with

(5.5)

{\mathcal{I}}_{\varepsilon}^{\varphi}({\mathbf{I}}):=\idotsint\limits_{a_{1}\leq b_{1}\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{i}_{1}),\cdots,a_{k}\leq b_{k}\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{i}_{k})}g_{a_{1}}(\varphi,0)^{2}G_{\vartheta}(b_{1}-a_{1})\bigg\{\prod\limits_{j=2}^{k}\frac{G_{\vartheta}(b_{j}-a_{j})}{a_{j}-b_{j-1}}\bigg\}{\mathrm{d}}a_{1}{\mathrm{d}}b_{1}\cdots{\mathrm{d}}a_{k}{\mathrm{d}}b_{k},

where
{\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{i}):=(\varepsilon(\mathrm{i}-1),\varepsilon\mathrm{i}]
,
g_{t}(\varphi,0)
is defined by (
2.29
), and
G_{\vartheta}
is introduced in (
2.26
)-(
2.28
).

Proof.

Recall the local limit (
2.20
) for
u(n)
, the asymptotics (
2.27
) and the uniform bound (
2.26
) for
\overline{U}_{N}(n)
. The lemma then follows by a Riemann sum approximation, similar to the proof of Proposition
2.3
.
{\mathcal{I}}_{\varepsilon}^{\varphi}({\mathbf{I}})
is well-defined by the renewal property (6.14) in
[
CSZ19a
]
:

(5.6)

G_{\vartheta}(t)=\iint_{0<u<\bar{t}\leq v<t}G_{\vartheta}(u)\frac{1}{v-u}G_{\vartheta}(t-v){\mathrm{d}}u{\mathrm{d}}v,\quad\forall t\in(\bar{t},+\infty).

∎

Note that by the renewal property (
5.6
), if for
{\mathbf{I}}:=(\mathrm{i}_{1},\cdots,\mathrm{i}_{k})\subset\{1,\cdots,1/\varepsilon\}
, we introduce

(5.7)

{\mathcal{I}}_{\varepsilon}^{s,t}({\mathbf{I}}):=\idotsint\limits_{s\leq b_{1}\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{i}_{1}),\cdots,a_{k}\leq t\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{i}_{k})}G_{\vartheta}(b_{1}-s)\bigg\{\prod\limits_{j=2}^{k-1}\frac{G_{\vartheta}(b_{j}-a_{j})}{a_{j}-b_{j-1}}\bigg\}\frac{G_{\vartheta}(t-a_{k})}{a_{k}-b_{k-1}}{\mathrm{d}}b_{1}{\mathrm{d}}a_{2}\cdots{\mathrm{d}}b_{k-1}{\mathrm{d}}a_{k},

then
\forall~\mathrm{j}\leq\mathrm{j}^{\prime},\forall~s\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{j})
, and
\forall~t\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{j}^{\prime})
,

(5.8)

\sum\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{\mathrm{j}=:\mathrm{i}_{1}<\mathrm{i}_{2}<\cdots<\mathrm{i}_{k-1}<\mathrm{i}_{k}:=\mathrm{j}^{\prime}}{\mathcal{I}}_{\varepsilon}^{s,t}(\mathrm{i}_{1},\cdots,\mathrm{i}_{k})=G_{\vartheta}(t-s)

By the above lemma, we have that

(5.9)

\displaystyle\limsup\limits_{N\to\infty}I_{N,\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\leq\|\psi\|_{\infty}\sum\limits_{k\geq(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon})^{2}}\sum\limits_{0<\mathrm{i}_{1}<\cdots<\mathrm{i}_{k}\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}{\mathcal{I}}_{\varepsilon}^{\varphi}(\mathrm{i}_{1},\cdots,\mathrm{i}_{k})=:I_{\varepsilon},

(5.10)

\displaystyle\limsup\limits_{N\to\infty}J_{N,\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\leq\|\psi\|_{\infty}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon})^{2}}\sum\limits_{(\mathrm{i}_{1},\cdots,\mathrm{i}_{k})\in({\mathcal{A}}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(no triple)}})^{c}}{\mathcal{I}}_{\varepsilon}^{\varphi}(\mathrm{i}_{1},\cdots,\mathrm{i}_{k})=:J_{\varepsilon}.

To bound (
5.9
), note that

I_{\varepsilon}\leq\frac{1}{(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon})^{2}}\sum\limits_{{\mathbf{I}}\subset\{1,\cdots,\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\},|{\mathbf{I}}|\geq(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon})^{2}}|{\mathbf{I}}|{\mathcal{I}}_{\varepsilon}^{\varphi}({\mathbf{I}})\leq\frac{1}{(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon})^{2}}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}\sum\limits_{j\in{\mathbf{I}}\subset\{1,\cdots,\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\}}{\mathbf{I}}_{\varepsilon}^{\varphi}({\mathbf{I}}).

The result then follows by the renewal property (
5.8
) and direct computations.

To bound (
5.10
), a fundamental tool is still the renewal property (
5.8
). For the cases
\mathrm{i}_{1}<K_{\varepsilon}
and
\mathrm{i}_{k}>\frac{1}{\varepsilon}-K_{\varepsilon}
, since each
{\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{i})
is of length
\varepsilon
, we have an integral on
[0,K_{\varepsilon}\varepsilon]
, whose length tends to
0
by the choice of
K_{\varepsilon}
in (
2.38
). For the case that there exists consecutive
\mathrm{i}-\mathrm{i}^{\prime}<K_{\varepsilon}
and
\mathrm{i}^{\prime\prime}-\mathrm{i}^{\prime}<K_{\varepsilon}
, we need to consider separately the cases (1)
\mathrm{i}^{\prime}-\mathrm{i}\geq 2
and
\mathrm{i}^{\prime\prime}-\mathrm{i}^{\prime}\geq 2
; (2)
\mathrm{i}^{\prime}-\mathrm{i}=1
or
\mathrm{i}^{\prime\prime}-\mathrm{i}^{\prime}=1
. The case (1) is relatively simple, since we can bound the transition kernels between mesoscopic intervals from below uniformly. The case (2) needs a delicate computation, the readers are recommended to refer to
[
CSZ23
, Section 5.1]
for details.
∎

5.2.
Approximating
{\mathcal{Z}}_{N,\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(no triple)}}
by
{\mathcal{Z}}_{N,\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(cg)}}
and
\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(cg)}}(\varphi,\psi)

Recall
{\mathcal{Z}}_{N,\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(no triple)}}
,
{\mathcal{Z}}_{N,\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(cg)}}
and
\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(cg)}}
from (
2.41
), (
2.44
) and (
2.46
). We have the following approximations.

Lemma 5.3
.

We have that for some
C\in(0,+\infty)
, which may depend on
\vartheta
, such that

(5.11)

\displaystyle\limsup\limits_{N\to\infty}

\displaystyle\left\|\left({\mathcal{Z}}_{N,\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(no triple)}}-{\mathcal{Z}}_{N,\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(cg)}}\right)(\varphi,\psi)\right\|_{2}^{2}\leq C\Big(\frac{1}{\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}+\frac{(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon})^{2}}{K_{\varepsilon}^{2}}\Big)\|\varphi\|_{2}^{2}\|\psi\|_{\infty}^{2},

(5.12)

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}

\displaystyle\left\|\left({\mathcal{Z}}_{N,\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(cg)}}-\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(cg)}}(\varphi,\psi|\Theta)\right)(\varphi,\psi)\right\|_{2}^{2}=0.

Proof.

We first prove (
5.11
). The approximation to
g_{1}(\varphi,\psi)
by
q_{0,N}^{N}(\varphi,\psi)
has been proved in (
2.32
). There are two steps remained: (1) expanding
{\mathcal{A}}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(no triple)}}
to
\vec{\mathbf{{\mathcal{A}}}}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(no triple)}}
; (2) replacing
X_{d,f}
by
\Theta_{N,\varepsilon}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})
and replacing the random walk transition kernels between
\Theta_{N,\varepsilon}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})
by heat kernels.

Step 1.
To expand
{\mathcal{A}}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(no triple)}}
to
\vec{\mathbf{{\mathcal{A}}}}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(no triple)}}
, we bind
X_{d,f}
and
X_{d^{\prime},f^{\prime}}
together via a transition probability
p_{d^{\prime}-f}(0)
, where
d,f\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon,N}(\mathrm{i})
and
d^{\prime},f^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon,N}(\mathrm{i}^{\prime})
with
\mathrm{i}^{\prime}-\mathrm{i}<K_{\varepsilon}
. Since each
\vec{\mathrm{i}}=(\mathrm{i},\mathrm{i}^{\prime})
can at most refer to 2 mesoscopic intervals. By expanding
{\mathcal{A}}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(no triple)}}
to
\vec{\mathbf{{\mathcal{A}}}}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(no triple)}}
, the terms refer to indices
{\mathbf{I}}=(\mathrm{i}_{1},\cdots,\mathrm{i}_{k})
with
(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon})^{2}\leq k\leq 2(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon})^{2}
will be added, and the error has been controlled by (
5.3
) and (
5.10
).

Step 2.
Then we replace the transition kernels by the heat kernels. Note that each time we bind two
X_{d_{j},f_{j}}
and
X_{d^{\prime}_{j},f^{\prime}_{j}}
together to get a
\Theta_{N,\varepsilon}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{j})
, we have an extra
\sqrt{\varepsilon N}
. For the kernel connecting
\Theta_{N,\varepsilon}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{j-1})
and
\Theta_{N,\varepsilon}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{j})
, we have an approximation
\sqrt{\varepsilon N}p_{d_{j}-f^{\prime}_{j-1}}(0)\approx g_{(\mathrm{i}_{j}-\mathrm{i}^{\prime}_{j-1})}(0)
. We need to show that such kernel replacement is controllable. By
[
LL10
, Theorem 2.3.5]
, since the random walk has finite third moment, it follows that for fixed
\varepsilon>0
and
N
large enough,

\Big|p_{d_{j}-f^{\prime}_{j-1}}(0)-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\varepsilon N}}g_{(\mathrm{i}_{j}-\mathrm{i}^{\prime}_{j-1})}(0)\Big|\leq\frac{C}{\varepsilon N(\mathrm{i}_{j}-\mathrm{i}^{\prime}_{j-1})}+\frac{C}{\sqrt{\varepsilon N}(\mathrm{i}_{j}-\mathrm{i}^{\prime}_{j-1})^{3/2}}\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{\varepsilon N}(\mathrm{i}_{j}-\mathrm{i}^{\prime}_{j-1})^{3/2}}.

For the summand in (
2.44
) and (
2.41
), each time we perform the kernel replacement, we gain an
L^{2}
error as follows (without loss of generality, we take
d_{2},f_{1}
as an example):

\begin{split}\bigg\|&\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum\limits_{d_{1}\leq f_{1}\in{\mathcal{T}}_{N,\varepsilon,}(\mathrm{i}_{1}),\cdots,d_{k}\leq f_{k}\in{\mathcal{T}}_{N,\varepsilon}(\mathrm{i}_{k})}q_{0,d_{1}}^{N}(\varphi,0)X_{d_{1},f_{1}}\Big(p_{d_{2}-f_{1}}(0)-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\varepsilon N}}g_{(\mathrm{i}_{2}-\mathrm{i}_{1})}(0)\Big)X_{d_{2},f_{2}}\\
&\times\bigg\{\prod\limits_{j=3}^{k}p_{d_{j}-f_{j-1}}(0)X_{d_{j},f_{j}}\bigg\}q_{f_{k},N}^{N}(0,\psi)\bigg\|_{2}^{2}\\
\leq&\frac{C\|\psi\|_{\infty}^{2}}{N}\sum\limits_{d_{1}\leq f_{1}\in{\mathcal{T}}_{N,\varepsilon}(\mathrm{i}_{1}),\cdots,d_{k}\leq f_{k}\in{\mathcal{T}}_{N,\varepsilon}(\mathrm{i}_{k})}\big(q_{0,d_{1}}^{N}(\varphi,0)\big)^{2}\overline{U}_{N}(f_{1}-d_{1})\frac{1}{\varepsilon N(\mathrm{i}_{2}-\mathrm{i}_{1})^{3}}\overline{U}_{N}(f_{2}-d_{2})\\
&\times\bigg\{\prod\limits_{j=3}^{k}u(d_{j}-f_{j-1})\overline{U}_{N}(f_{j}-d_{j})\bigg\}.\end{split}

Note that
1/(\varepsilon N(\mathrm{i}_{2}-\mathrm{i}_{1}))\leq C/(d_{2}-f_{1})
and
\mathrm{i}_{2}-\mathrm{i}_{1}\geq K_{\varepsilon}
by the “no triple” condition in
\vec{\mathbf{{\mathcal{A}}}}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(no triple)}}
. By Lemma
5.2
, taking
\limsup_{N\to\infty}
, the above term is bounded by
C\|\psi\|_{\infty}^{2}(K_{\varepsilon})^{-2}{\mathcal{I}}_{\varepsilon}^{\varphi}(\mathrm{i}_{1},\cdots,\mathrm{i}_{k})
. Since there are at most
2(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon})^{2}
kernel replacements, by triangle inequality, the total
L^{2}
error for kernel replacements is bounded by
C\|\psi\|_{\infty}^{2}(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon})^{2}(K_{\varepsilon})^{-2}{\mathcal{I}}_{\varepsilon}^{\varphi}(\mathrm{i}_{1},\cdots,\mathrm{i}_{k})
. Finally, by summing over all
k
and
\mathrm{i}_{1},\cdots,\mathrm{i}_{k}
, and by the orthogonality of different
\mathrm{i}_{1},\cdots,\mathrm{i}_{k}
, the upper bound for kernel replacement is

\begin{split}&\limsup\limits_{N\to\infty}C\|\psi\|_{\infty}^{2}\big(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\big)^{2}(K_{\varepsilon})^{-2}{\rm Var}({\mathcal{Z}}_{N}(\varphi,\psi))\\
\leq&\frac{C\big(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\big)^{2}\|\psi\|_{\infty}^{2}}{K_{\varepsilon}^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}G_{\vartheta}(t){\mathrm{d}}t\int_{0}^{1}g_{s}(\varphi,0)^{2}{\mathrm{d}}s\leq\frac{C_{\vartheta}\big(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\big)^{2}}{K_{\varepsilon}^{2}}\|\psi\|_{\infty}^{2}\|\varphi\|_{2}^{2}.\end{split}

Combine
step 1
and
step 2
and we conclude (
5.11
).

Finally we prove (
5.12
), the approximation to
{\mathcal{Z}}_{N,\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(cg)}}
by
\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(cg)}}(\varphi,\psi)
. This is just a Riemann sum approximation from
\sum_{\mathrm{a}\sqrt{\varepsilon N}<u\leq(\mathrm{a}+1)\sqrt{\varepsilon N},u\in{\mathbbm{Z}}}\varphi_{N}(u)
and
\sum_{\mathrm{b}\sqrt{\varepsilon N}<v\leq(\mathrm{b}+1)\sqrt{\varepsilon N},v\in{\mathbbm{Z}}}\psi_{N}(v)
to
\varphi_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{a})
and
\psi_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{b})
respectively.
∎

6.
Moment estimates for
\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(cg)}}(\varphi,\psi)

In this section, we first bound the second and fourth moments of the coarse-grained disorder
\Theta_{N,\varepsilon}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})
(see (
2.43
)), and then we will use these bounds to bound moments of coarse-grained model
\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(cg)}}(\varphi,\psi|\Theta)
.

6.1.
Moment bounds for the coarse-grained variable
\Theta_{N,\varepsilon}

For the second moment of
\Theta_{N,\varepsilon}
, we have the following bound.

Lemma 6.1
.

For any
\vec{\mathrm{i}}
, we have that
{\mathbbm{E}}[\Theta_{N,\varepsilon}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})]=0
and the second moment of
\Theta_{N,\varepsilon}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})
converges, that is, there exists some
\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})\geq 0
, such that

(6.1)

\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}):=\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\big(\Theta_{N,\varepsilon}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})\big)^{2}\Big].

Furthermore, there exists some constant
C>0
, independent of
\vec{\mathrm{i}}
and
\varepsilon
, such that

(6.2)

\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})\leq\frac{C}{\big(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\big)^{1+\mathbbm{1}_{\{|\vec{\mathrm{i}}|\geq 2\}}}|\vec{\mathrm{i}}|}.

Proof sketch.

The computation is similar to those for Proposition
2.3
and Lemma
5.2
. When
|\vec{\mathrm{i}}|\leq 2
,
\Theta_{N,\varepsilon}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})
is an average of
X_{d,f}
(see (
2.40
)),which has the same structure as
{\mathcal{Z}}_{N}(\varphi,\psi)
with
\psi(y)=\mathbbm{1}_{0}(y)
. When
|\vec{\mathrm{i}}|\geq 3
, we take advantage of the fact that
p_{d^{\prime}-f}(0)
in (
2.43
) is uniformly bounded below by
C(|\vec{\mathrm{i}}|\varepsilon N)^{-1/2}
.
∎

For the fourth moment of
\Theta_{N,\varepsilon}
, we have the following bound.

Lemma 6.2
.

For any
\vec{\mathrm{i}}
and sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a uniform constant
C>0
, such that

\limsup\limits_{N\to\infty}{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\big(\Theta_{N,\varepsilon}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})\big)^{4}\Big]\leq\frac{C}{\big(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\big)^{1+\mathbbm{1}_{|\vec{\mathrm{i}}|\geq 3}}|\vec{\mathrm{i}}|^{2}}.

Proof sketch.

The strategy is the same as in Lemma
6.1
and the difference is that we apply Theorem
4.1
. We split the case
|\vec{\mathrm{i}}|\leq 2
and
|\vec{\mathrm{i}}|\geq 3
. Note that in the latter case, we can decouple the fourth moments of
X_{d,f}
and
X_{d^{\prime},f^{\prime}}
in (
2.43
) by the independence.∎

6.2.
Fourth moment bounds for
\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(cg)}}(\varphi,\psi)

Recall from (
2.46
) that

\begin{split}\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(cg)}}(\varphi,\psi|\Theta):=g_{1}(\varphi,\psi)+&\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sum\limits_{r=1}^{(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon})^{2}}\sum\limits_{(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1},\cdots,\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{r})\in\vec{\mathbf{{\mathcal{A}}}}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(no triple)}}}\sum\limits_{\mathrm{a},\mathrm{b}\in{\mathbbm{Z}}}\\
&\varphi_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{a})g_{\mathrm{i}_{1}}(\mathrm{a})\Theta(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1})\times\bigg\{\prod_{j=2}^{r}g_{(\mathrm{i}_{j}-\mathrm{i}^{\prime}_{j-1})}(0)\Theta(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{j})\bigg\}g_{(\lfloor\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\rfloor-\mathrm{i}^{\prime}_{r})}(\mathrm{b})\psi_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{b}).\end{split}

We have the following fourth moment bound for
\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(cg)}}(\varphi,\psi)
, which is an analogue to Theorem
4.1
.

Theorem 6.3
.

Recall
\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(cg)}}(\varphi,\psi)
from (
2.46
). Suppose that
\psi
is supported on
B
(could be
{\mathbbm{R}}
) with
\|\psi\|_{\infty}<+\infty
. Then for any
p,q\in(1,+\infty)
with
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
and
w:{\mathbbm{R}}\to(0,+\infty)
such that
\log w
is Lipschitz continuous, there exists a constant
C\in(0,+\infty)
, such that uniformly for any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,

\limsup\limits_{N\to\infty}{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\big(\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(cg)}}(\varphi,\psi)-{\mathbbm{E}}\big[\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(cg)}}(\varphi,\psi)\big]\big)^{4}\Big]\leq C\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{p}}\bigg\|\frac{\varphi_{\varepsilon}}{w_{\varepsilon}}\bigg\|^{4}_{\ell^{p}}\|\psi\|_{\infty}^{4}\|w\mathbbm{1}_{B}\|^{4}_{\ell^{q}},

where
\varphi_{\varepsilon}
and
w_{\varepsilon}
are defined by (
2.47
).

Remark 6.4
.

We introduce
w
since in Section
7
we will consider
\psi
with unbounded support.

Proof.

We have that

(6.3)

\begin{split}{\mathcal{M}}_{N,\varepsilon}^{\varphi,\psi}:&={\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\big(\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(cg)}}(\varphi,\psi)-{\mathbbm{E}}\big[\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(cg)}}(\varphi,\psi)\big]\big)^{4}\Big]=\varepsilon^{2}{\mathbbm{E}}\bigg[\bigg(\sum\limits_{r=1}^{(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon})^{2}}\sum\limits_{(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1},\cdots,\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{r})\in\vec{\mathbf{{\mathcal{A}}}}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(no triple)}}}\sum\limits_{\mathrm{a},\mathrm{b}\in{\mathbbm{Z}}}\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\varphi_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{a})g_{\mathrm{i}_{1}}(\mathrm{a})\Theta_{N,\varepsilon}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1})\times\Big\{\prod_{j=2}^{r}g_{(\mathrm{i}_{j}-\mathrm{i}^{\prime}_{j-1})}(0)\Theta_{N,\varepsilon}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{j})\Big\}g_{(\lfloor\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\rfloor-\mathrm{i}^{\prime}_{r})}(\mathrm{b})\psi_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{b})\bigg)^{4}\bigg].\end{split}

Similar to the proof of Theorem
4.1
(see (
4.9
)-(
4.10
)), we can find some some constant
C\in(0,\infty)
, such that

(6.4)

\begin{split}{\mathcal{M}}_{N,\varepsilon}^{\varphi,\psi}\leq C\|\psi\|_{\infty}^{4}\varepsilon^{3}&\sum\limits_{\mathbf{n}=1}^{2/\varepsilon}{\mathbbm{E}}\bigg[\bigg(\sum\limits_{r=1}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1},\cdots,\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{r})\in\vec{\mathbf{{\mathcal{A}}}}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(no triple)}}}\sum\limits_{\mathrm{a},\mathrm{b}\in{\mathbbm{Z}}\atop|\mathrm{a}|\leq M_{\varepsilon}\sqrt{\mathrm{i}_{1}},|\mathrm{b}|\leq M_{\varepsilon}\sqrt{\lfloor\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\rfloor-\mathrm{i}_{r}^{\prime}}}\\
&\varphi_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{a})g_{\mathrm{i}_{1}}(\mathrm{a})\Theta_{N,\varepsilon}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1})\times\Big\{\prod_{j=2}^{r}g_{(\mathrm{i}_{j}-\mathrm{i}^{\prime}_{j-1})}(0)\Theta_{N,\varepsilon}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{j})\Big\}g_{(\mathbf{n}-\mathrm{i}^{\prime}_{r})}(\mathrm{b})\mathbbm{1}_{B_{\varepsilon}}(\mathrm{b})\bigg)^{4}\bigg].\end{split}

Here
2/\varepsilon
plays the role of
2N
in (
4.10
).

Summation rearrangement

Next, we rearrange the summation in (
6.4
). If we view
\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{j}
as a renewal time, then after expanding the fourth moment, we have 4 sequences of renewal times. Furthermore, if
|\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{j}|\geq 2
, then
\mathrm{i}_{j},\mathrm{i}^{\prime}_{j}
are recorded as 2 distinct renewal times. We denote each sequence of renewal times by
\{\mathrm{i}_{i}^{j}\}_{i=1}^{r_{j}}
for
j=1,2,3,4
, and call them
mesoscopic renewal sequences
.

We then perform the following rearrangement for
\{\mathrm{i}_{i}^{j}\}_{i=1}^{r_{j}}
,
j=1,2,3,4
:

(1) Sum over the set
\bigcup_{j=1}^{4}\{\mathrm{i}_{i}^{j}\}:=\{n_{1},\cdots,n_{r}\}
.

(2) For each
n_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq r
, sum over the indices set
J_{n_{i}}\subset\{1,2,3,4\}
. Here
J_{n_{i}}
records the indices of the 4 renewal sequences which have a renewal time
n_{i}
.

Recall that for
\vec{\mathrm{i}}\neq\vec{\mathrm{j}}
,
\Theta_{N,\varepsilon}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})
and
\Theta_{N,\varepsilon}(\vec{\mathrm{j}})
are uncorrelated. Since they also have zero means, a summand in (1)-(2) above is nonzero if and only if for any
n_{i}
, we must have
|J_{n_{i}}|\geq 2
.

We also have the following constraints on
(n_{1},\cdots,n_{r})
and
J_{1},\cdots,J_{r}
by
(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1},\cdots,\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{r})\in\vec{\mathbf{{\mathcal{A}}}}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(no triple)}}
:

(a)
K_{\varepsilon}\leq n_{1}<n_{2}<\cdots<n_{r}\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}-K_{\varepsilon}
with
K_{\varepsilon}=(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon})^{6}
(recall (
2.38
)).

(b)
(n_{1},\cdots,n_{r})
can be partitioned into consecutive blocks
{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\cdots,{\mathcal{D}}_{m}
, where each
{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
consists of consecutive integers and
\max{\mathcal{D}}_{i}\leq\min{\mathcal{D}}_{i+1}-2
.
We must have
|{\mathcal{D}}_{i}|\leq 4
for all
i=1,\cdots,m
. The reason is that
|J_{n_{i}}|\geq 2
for all
i=1,\cdots,r
, and by the definition of
\vec{\mathbf{{\mathcal{A}}}}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(no triple)}}
, there are at most two renewal times within a time period
K_{\varepsilon}
. Hence,
|{\mathcal{D}}_{i}|\leq 4
can be obtained by simple counting.

Starting from the constraint (b) above, we can further group the blocks
{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\cdots,{\mathcal{D}}_{m}
. Suppose that for consecutive
{\mathcal{D}}_{i_{1}},{\mathcal{D}}_{i_{1}+1},\cdots,{\mathcal{D}}_{i_{2}}
(
i_{1}
may equal to
i_{2}
, that is, we have a single
D_{i_{1}}
), and for any
n_{j}\in\bigcup_{i=i_{1}}^{i_{2}}{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
, we always have that
J_{n_{j}}=\{k,l\}
with
k\neq l
. Then we only record
s:=\min{\mathcal{D}}_{i_{1}}
and
t:=\max{\mathcal{D}}_{i_{2}}
and identify the integer sequence
\bigcup_{i=i_{1}}^{i_{2}}{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
by
[s,t]
(similar to the construction of (
4.11
)). For other (single)
{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
, we also denote
s_{i}=\min{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
and
t_{i}=\max{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
. Then we can further group
{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\cdots,{\mathcal{D}}_{m}
according to intervals
{\mathcal{I}}_{i}=[s_{i},t_{i}]\cap{\mathbbm{N}}
,
1\leq i\leq k
. We can now rearrange the summing strategy (1) and (2) as follows:

(1’) Sum over integers
K_{\varepsilon}<s_{1}\leq t_{1}<s_{2}\leq t_{2}<\cdots<s_{k}\leq t_{k}<\mathbf{n}\leq\frac{2}{\varepsilon}
with
s_{i+1}-t_{i}\geq 2
for
i=2,\cdots,k-1
. Recall that
\mathbf{n}
is introduced in (
6.4
).

(2’) Suppose that
{\mathcal{I}}_{i}=[s_{i},t_{i}]
represents some blocks
\bigcup_{i=i_{1}}^{i_{2}}{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
with
\min{\mathcal{D}}_{i_{1}}=s_{i}
and
\max{\mathcal{D}}_{i_{2}}=t_{i}
. Recall the indices set
J_{n_{i}}
introduced in (2) above. We write
{\mathcal{J}}_{i}=\bigcup_{n_{i}}J_{n_{i}}
, where
n_{i}
runs over all renewal times in
\bigcup_{i=i_{1}}^{i_{2}}{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
, that is,
{\mathcal{J}}_{i}
records all indices of the mesoscopic renewal processes that renew at least once at some time
n_{i}\in\bigcup_{i=i_{1}}^{i_{2}}{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
. If
|{\mathcal{J}}_{i}|=2
, then we call the related
{\mathcal{I}}_{i}
a
group of type U
, since it is a counterpart of (
4.11
). If
|{\mathcal{J}}_{i}|\geq 3
, then we call the related
{\mathcal{I}}_{i}
a
a group of type V
and it is a counterpart of
\zeta_{n}
in the expansion of
{\mathcal{Z}}_{N}^{\beta_{N}}(\varphi,\psi)
. Note that as we discussed above, two consecutive U blocks must have different indices set
{\mathcal{J}}_{i}
and
{\mathcal{J}}_{i+1}
, while a V block must have length
|{\mathcal{I}}_{i}|\leq 4
. Finally, for each
{\mathcal{I}}_{i}=[s_{i},t_{i}]
, we sum over the indices set
{\mathcal{J}}_{i}
.

Adjustments on coarse-grained disorders and heat kernels

To treat the summations (1’) and (2’), we use the framework in the proof of Theorem
4.1
. We start with some surgery on
\Theta_{N,\varepsilon}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})
, the heat kernel, and the random walk transition probability.

(A) we unbind
X_{d,f}
and
X_{d^{\prime},f^{\prime}}
in the definition of
\Theta_{N,\varepsilon}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})
(see (
2.43
)), when
|\vec{\mathrm{i}}|>1
and
\mathrm{i},\mathrm{i}^{\prime}
belong to two different groups
{\mathcal{I}}_{i},{\mathcal{I}}_{i^{\prime}}
, so that we have two coarse-grained disorder
\Theta_{N,\varepsilon}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1})
and
\Theta_{N,\varepsilon}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2})
with
\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1}=(\mathrm{i},\mathrm{i})
and
\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}=(\mathrm{i}^{\prime},\mathrm{i}^{\prime})
.

(B) Decompose the heat kernel by Chapman-Kolmogorov equation, so that we can sum over all 4 mesoscopic renewal processes at any time
n
, since some of them renew but some others do not.

For step (A), note that since
{\mathcal{I}}_{i}
and
{\mathcal{I}}_{i^{\prime}}
are different groups, we have that
s_{i^{\prime}}-t_{i}\geq 2
. Hence,
d^{\prime}-f\geq(s_{i^{\prime}}-t_{i}-1)\varepsilon N\geq\varepsilon N
. Moreover, If
{\mathcal{I}}_{i}
is a U group, then
\mathrm{i}=t_{i}
. Otherwise, if
\mathrm{i}<t_{i}
, then
\mathrm{i}<\mathrm{i}^{\prime}\leq t_{i}
and thus
\mathrm{i}^{\prime}
cannot belong to another group
{\mathcal{I}}_{i^{\prime}}
. If
{\mathcal{I}}_{i}
is a V group, then
\mathrm{i}\geq s_{i}\geq t_{i}-3
, since the length of a V group can at most be
4
. Similarly, if
{\mathcal{I}}_{i^{\prime}}
is a U group, then
\mathrm{i}^{\prime}=s_{i^{\prime}}
and if
{\mathcal{I}}_{i^{\prime}}
is a V group, then
\mathrm{i}^{\prime}\leq t_{i^{\prime}}\leq s_{i^{\prime}}+3
. In all cases, we have that
d^{\prime}-f\leq(t_{i^{\prime}}+1-s_{i})\leq(s_{i^{\prime}}-t_{i}+7)\varepsilon N
. By local limit theorem, we have that uniformly in all
f\in{\mathcal{T}}_{N,\varepsilon}(\mathrm{i})
and
d^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{T}}_{N,\varepsilon}(\mathrm{i}^{\prime})
, there exists some
c\in(0,+\infty)
, such that

p_{f,d^{\prime}}(0)\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{2\pi(d^{\prime}-f)}}\leq\frac{C^{\prime}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon N}}g_{(s_{i^{\prime}}-t_{i})}(0).

Note that we are considering the moment bounds and we will bound every moment of
\Theta_{N,\varepsilon}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})
by its absolute value. Hence, we can upper bound any moment of
\Theta_{N,\varepsilon}((\mathrm{i},\mathrm{i}^{\prime}))
above by the related moment of
C^{\prime}\Theta_{N,\varepsilon}((\mathrm{i},\mathrm{i}))g_{(s_{i^{\prime}}-t_{i})}(0)\Theta_{N,\varepsilon}((\mathrm{i}^{\prime},\mathrm{i}^{\prime}))
. We will see that the constant
C^{\prime}
is not important.

For step (B), note that after the modifications in step (A), any two different groups
{\mathcal{I}}_{i}
and
{\mathcal{I}}_{j}
that are visited consecutively by one mesoscopic renewal process are always connected by a heat kernel. If the heat kernel comes from a modification of
p_{f,d^{\prime}}(0)
, then it is now
g_{s_{j}-t_{i}}(0)
. If the heat kernel is an original one, for example
g_{\mathrm{j}-\mathrm{i}}(0)
for some
\mathrm{i}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{i}
and
\mathrm{j}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{j}
, then we must have
\mathrm{j}-\mathrm{i}>K_{\varepsilon}
. Similarly to the discussion in step (A),
t_{i}-3\leq\mathrm{i}\leq t_{i}
and
s_{j}\leq\mathrm{j}\leq s_{j}+3
. Hence, this heat kernel can also be replaced by
Cg_{s_{j}-t_{i}}(0)
. Next, we can squeeze the length of each V group to
1
. By squeezing V groups, for two consecutive renewal times
\mathrm{i}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{i}
and
\mathrm{j}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{j}
, the new distance
u
between the two renewal times
\mathrm{i}
and
\mathrm{j}
satisfies
(s_{j}-t_{i}+7)\geq u\geq\frac{1}{4}(\mathrm{j}-\mathrm{i})\geq\frac{1}{4}(s_{j}-t_{i})
, since each V group has length at most
4
. From now on, we can assume that for any V group
{\mathcal{I}}_{i}
, its length is one, that is
s_{i}=t_{i}
, and
g_{u}(0)\leq 2g_{(s_{j}-t_{i})}(0)
. To this end, all heat kernels are
Cg_{(s_{j}-t_{i})}(0)
.

Now, by Chapman-Kolmogorov equation, for any
t:=u_{0}<u_{1}<\cdots<u_{k}=:s
, we have that

\begin{split}g_{s-t}(0)&=\idotsint\limits_{x_{1},\cdots,x_{k-1}\in{\mathbbm{R}}}g_{u_{1}-u_{0}}(x_{1})\prod\limits_{i=2}^{k-1}g_{(u_{i}-u_{i-1})}(x_{i}-x_{i-1})g_{(u_{k}-u_{k-1})}(x_{k-1}){\mathrm{d}}x_{1}\cdots{\mathrm{d}}x_{k-1}\\
&\leq C^{k}\sum\limits_{z_{1},\cdots,z_{z-1}\in{\mathbbm{Z}}}g_{u_{1}-u_{0}}(z_{1})\prod\limits_{i=2}^{k-1}g_{(u_{i}-u_{i-1})}(z_{i}-z_{i-1})g_{(u_{k}-u_{k-1})}(z_{k-1}),\end{split}

for some
C
uniformly. By this decomposition, we introduce a “virtual spatial position”, so that when a mesoscopic renewal process does not renew, we can allocate a “position” to it. Then, we can treat the summation similar to the proof of Theorem
4.1
.

Moments estimates

We now consider the expectation of a U group
{\mathcal{I}}=[s,t]
. Without loss of generality, we assume that the related indices set for mesoscopic renewal processes is
{\mathcal{J}}=\{1,2\}
. Taking expectation in (
6.4
), each U group leads to a following quantity

(6.5)

\overline{U}_{N,\varepsilon}^{(\rm cg)}(t-s):=\sum\limits_{(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1},\cdots,\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{r})\in\vec{\mathbf{{\mathcal{A}}}}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(no triple)}}\atop\mathrm{i}_{1}=s,\mathrm{i}^{\prime}_{r}=t}{\mathbbm{E}}\big[(\Theta_{N,\varepsilon}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1}))^{2}\big]\prod\limits_{j=2}^{r}g^{2}_{(\mathrm{i}_{j}-\mathrm{i}^{\prime}_{j-1})}(0){\mathbbm{E}}\big[(\Theta_{N,\varepsilon}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{j}))^{2}\big].

For a fixed
\varepsilon>0
, there are only finite many terms in the above summation. Hence, by Lemma
6.1
, the following limit exists

(6.6)

\overline{U}_{\infty,\varepsilon}^{\rm(cg)}(t-s):=\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}\overline{U}_{N,\varepsilon}^{(\rm cg)}(t-s).

We then consider the expectation of a V group
{\mathcal{I}}=[s,t]
with
t-s\leq 3
. Let
{\mathcal{J}}\subset\{1,2,3,4\}
be its related indices set for mesoscopic renewal processes. Note that we must have
|{\mathcal{J}}|\geq 3
to have a nonzero mean (
|{\mathcal{J}}|=2
refers to a U group). For each
j\in{\mathcal{J}}
, the
j
-th mesoscopic renewal process can renew in
{\mathcal{I}}
for at most two times. Hence, the relevant coarse-grained disorder can always be written by
\Theta_{N,\varepsilon}((\mathrm{i}_{j},\mathrm{i}^{\prime}_{j}))
, where
\mathrm{i}_{j}
is the entering time and
\mathrm{i}^{\prime}_{j}
is the exiting time (it is possible that
\mathrm{i}_{j}=\mathrm{i}^{\prime}_{j}
). Then, taking expectation in (
6.4
), each
V
group leads to a following quantity

(6.7)

V_{N,\varepsilon}^{\rm(cg)}({\mathcal{J}}):={\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\prod_{j\in{\mathcal{J}}}\Theta_{N,\varepsilon}((\mathrm{i}_{j},\mathrm{i}^{\prime}_{j}))\Big].

By Hólder’s inequality and Lemma
6.2
, we have that

(6.8)

\limsup_{N\to\infty}V_{N,\varepsilon}^{\rm(cg)}({\mathcal{J}})\leq\limsup_{N\to\infty}\prod\limits_{j\in{\mathcal{J}}}{\mathbbm{E}}\big[\Theta_{N,\varepsilon}((\mathrm{i}_{j},\mathrm{i}^{\prime}_{j}))^{4}\big]^{\frac{1}{4}}:=V_{\infty,\varepsilon}^{\rm(cg)}({\mathcal{J}})\leq\frac{C}{(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon})^{\frac{3}{4}}}.

We now derive an upper bound for
\limsup_{N\to\infty}{\mathcal{M}}_{N,\varepsilon}^{\varphi,\psi}
similar to (
4.10
). For this purpose, we replace the indices set
{\mathcal{J}}
by partition
I
to make the notations consistent. Note that we actually have
{\mathcal{J}}=I(1)
(recall the definition of
I
from the paragraph above (
4.4
)). Recall the summing strategy (1’) and (2’) above.
\limsup_{N\to\infty}M_{N,\varepsilon}^{\varphi,\psi}
can be expressed as follows:

(1”) Suppose that there are
r
groups
{\mathcal{I}}_{1},{\mathcal{I}}_{2},\cdots,{\mathcal{I}}_{r}
. We sum over
K_{\varepsilon}<s_{1}\leq t_{1}<s_{2}\leq t_{2}<\cdots<s_{r}\leq t_{r}<s_{r+1}\leq\frac{2}{\varepsilon}
, where
{\mathcal{I}}_{i}=[s_{i},t_{i}]
and
s_{r+1}
plays the role of
\mathbf{n}
in (
6.4
).

(2”) For each
1\leq i\leq r
, we sum over
I_{i}
with
|I_{i}|\leq 3
. When
|I_{i}|=3
, we have that
{\mathcal{I}}_{i}
is a U group, while when
|I_{i}|<3
, we have that
{\mathcal{I}}_{i}
is a V group. Note that to have a nonzero expectation, we cannot have
|I_{i}|=4
for any
i
.

(3”) We sum over
r\geq 1
(note that the
r=0
refers to the mean, which has been subtracted).

We now introduce some notations similar to (
4.5
) and (
4.11
). For
\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\in({\mathbbm{Z}})^{4}
, let

(6.9)

{\mathcal{Q}}_{t}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}):=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{4}g_{t}(x^{\prime}_{i}-x_{i}),\quad{\mathcal{Q}}_{t}(\varphi,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}):=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{4}\Big(\sum\limits_{y_{i}\in{\mathbbm{Z}}}\varphi(y_{i})g_{t}(x^{\prime}_{i})\Big),\quad{\mathcal{Q}}_{t}(\boldsymbol{x},\psi):=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{4}\Big(\sum\limits_{y^{\prime}_{i}\in{\mathbbm{Z}}}g_{t}(x_{i})\psi(y^{\prime}_{i})\Big).

Recall (
6.6
), (
6.8
), and
{\mathcal{J}}=I(1)
, we define

(6.10)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{U}}^{\text{(cg)},I}_{\infty,\varepsilon}(t;\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})

\displaystyle:=\overline{U}^{\text{(cg)}}_{\infty,\varepsilon}(t)\prod\limits_{j\notin I(1)}g_{t}(x^{\prime}_{j}-x_{j}),

(6.11)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{V}}^{\text{(cg)},I}_{\infty,\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})

\displaystyle:=V^{\text{(cg)}}_{\infty,\varepsilon}(I(1))\prod\limits_{j\notin I(1)}g_{0}(x^{\prime}_{j}-x_{j}),

where for
{\mathbf{V}}^{\text{(cg)},I}_{\infty,\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})
, we actually have
\boldsymbol{x}=\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}
, since we have squeeze the length of V group to 1. To lighten the notations, we combine (
6.10
) and (
6.11
) by introducing

(6.12)

{\mathbf{W}}_{\infty,\varepsilon}^{{\rm(cg)},I}(t;\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}):=\mathbbm{1}_{\{|I|=3\}}{\mathbf{U}}^{\text{(cg)},I}_{\infty,\varepsilon}(t;\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})+\mathbbm{1}_{\{|I|<3\}}{\mathbf{V}}^{\text{(cg)},I}_{\infty,\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}).

Finally, we can write down an upper bound for
\limsup_{N\to\infty}M_{N,\varepsilon}^{\varphi,\psi}
according to summation (1”)-(3”):

(6.13)

\begin{split}&{\mathcal{M}}_{\infty,\varepsilon}^{\varphi,\psi}:=\limsup_{N\to\infty}M_{N,\varepsilon}^{\varphi,\psi}\leq c\|\psi\|_{\infty}^{4}\varepsilon^{3}\sum\limits_{r=1}^{\infty}C^{r}\sum\limits_{I_{1},\cdots,I_{r},|I_{i}|\leq 3,1\leq i\leq r\atop K_{\varepsilon}<s_{1}\leq t_{1}<\cdots<s_{r}\leq t_{r}<s_{r+1}\leq\frac{2}{\varepsilon}}\sum\limits_{\boldsymbol{x}_{1},\cdots,\boldsymbol{x}_{r}\in({\mathbbm{Z}})^{4}\atop\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}_{1},\cdots,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}_{r}\in({\mathbbm{Z}})^{4}}{\mathcal{Q}}_{s_{1}}(\varphi_{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{x}_{1})\\
\times\Big\{&\prod\limits_{i=1}^{r-1}{\mathbf{W}}_{\infty,\varepsilon}^{{\rm(cg)},I_{i}}(t_{i}-s_{i};\boldsymbol{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}_{i}){\mathcal{Q}}_{(s_{i+1}-t_{i})}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}_{i},\boldsymbol{x}_{i+1})\Big\}{\mathbf{W}}_{\infty,\varepsilon}^{{\rm(cg)},I_{r}}(t_{r}-s_{r};\boldsymbol{x}_{r},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}_{r}){\mathcal{Q}}_{(s_{r+1}-t_{r})}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}_{r},\mathbbm{1}_{B_{\varepsilon}}).\end{split}
