Here the factor
C^{r}
comes from all heat kernel adjustments. Note that those adjustments are only needed at renewal times
s_{i}
and
t_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq r
and thus we have a power
r
. Also note that if
|I_{i}|<3
, then
s_{i}=t_{i}
by our squeezing on V group.

Similar to (
4.14
)-(
4.16
), we define

(6.14)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Q}}_{\lambda,\varepsilon}^{I,J}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})

\displaystyle:=\sum\limits_{n=1}^{2/\varepsilon}e^{-\lambda n}{\mathcal{Q}}_{n}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}),

(6.15)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{U}}_{\lambda,\varepsilon}^{I}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})

\displaystyle:=\sum\limits_{n=0}^{2/\varepsilon}e^{-\lambda n}{\mathbf{U}}^{\text{(cg)},I}_{\infty,\varepsilon}(t;\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}),

(6.16)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{V}}_{\lambda,\varepsilon}^{I}(\boldsymbol{x}.\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})

\displaystyle:={\mathbf{V}}^{\text{(cg)},I}_{\infty,\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}),

(6.17)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{P}}_{\lambda,\varepsilon}^{I,J}

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}{\mathcal{Q}}_{\lambda,\varepsilon}^{I,J}{\mathbf{V}}_{\lambda,\varepsilon}^{J},\quad&\text{if}~|J|<3,\\
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\lambda,\varepsilon}^{I,J}{\mathbf{U}}_{\lambda,\varepsilon}^{J},\quad&\text{if}~|J|=3.\end{cases}

Then, we insert
w
to define a weighted version of
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\lambda,\varepsilon}^{I,J}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})
by

(6.18)

\widehat{{\mathcal{Q}}}_{\lambda,\varepsilon}^{I,J}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}):=\frac{w_{\varepsilon}^{\otimes 4}(\boldsymbol{x})}{w_{\varepsilon}^{\otimes 4}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})}{\mathcal{Q}}_{\lambda,\varepsilon}^{I,J}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}).

The weighted operators
\widehat{{\mathbf{U}}}_{\lambda,\varepsilon}^{I}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})
,
\widehat{{\mathbf{V}}}_{\lambda,\varepsilon}^{I}(\boldsymbol{x}.\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})
and
\widehat{{\mathcal{P}}}_{\lambda,\varepsilon}^{I,J}
are defined in the same way.

Introducing a factor
e^{2\lambda/\varepsilon}e^{-\lambda\sum_{i=1}^{r+1}s_{i}-t_{i-1}-\lambda\sum_{i=1}^{r}(t_{i}-s_{i})}\geq 1
with
t_{0}:=0
, (
6.13
) is then bounded by

(6.19)

\Big|{\mathcal{M}}_{\infty,\varepsilon}^{\varphi,\psi}\Big|\leq ce^{\frac{2\lambda}{\varepsilon}}\|\psi\|_{\infty}^{4}\varepsilon^{3}\sum\limits_{r=1}^{\infty}C^{r}\sum\limits_{I_{1},\cdots,I_{r}\atop|I_{i}|\leq 3,1\leq i\leq r}\Big\langle\frac{\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{\otimes 4}}{w_{\varepsilon}^{\otimes 4}},\widehat{{\mathcal{P}}}_{\lambda,\varepsilon}^{*,I_{1}}\widehat{{\mathcal{P}}}_{\lambda,\varepsilon}^{I_{1},I_{2}}\cdots\widehat{{\mathcal{P}}}_{\lambda,\varepsilon}^{I_{r-1},I_{r}}\widehat{{\mathcal{Q}}}_{\lambda,\varepsilon}^{I_{r},*}w_{\varepsilon}\mathbbm{1}_{B_{\varepsilon}}^{\otimes 4}\Big\rangle,

where “
*
” is the partition of
\{1,2,3,4\}
consisting of all singletons, and
\lambda:=\widehat{\lambda}\varepsilon
with
\widehat{\lambda}
a large but fixed constant, such that
e^{2\lambda/\varepsilon}
is bounded. We have the following proposition to control (
6.19
)

Proposition 6.5
.

There exists some constant
c>0
, such that

(6.20)

\displaystyle\Big\|\widehat{{\mathcal{Q}}}_{\lambda,\varepsilon}^{I,J}\Big\|_{\ell^{q}\to\ell^{q}}

\displaystyle\leq c,

(6.21)

\displaystyle\Big\|\widehat{{\mathcal{Q}}}_{\lambda,\varepsilon}^{*,I}\Big\|_{\ell^{q}\to\ell^{q}}\leq c\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{q}},

\displaystyle\quad\quad\Big\|\widehat{{\mathcal{Q}}}_{\lambda,\varepsilon}^{I,*}\Big\|_{\ell^{q}\to\ell^{q}}\leq c\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{p}},

(6.22)

\displaystyle\Big\|\widehat{{\mathbf{U}}}_{\widehat{\lambda}\varepsilon,\varepsilon}^{I}\Big\|_{\ell^{q}\to\ell^{q}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c}{\log\widehat{\lambda}},\quad\text{for}~|I|=3,

(6.23)

\displaystyle\Big\|\widehat{{\mathbf{V}}}_{\lambda,\varepsilon}^{I}\Big\|_{\ell^{q}\to\ell^{q}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c}{(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon})^{\frac{3}{4}}},\quad\text{for}~|I|\leq 2.

We postpone the proof of Proposition
6.5
and first complete the proof of Theorem
6.3
. By Hölder inequality, we can bound (
6.19
) by

\begin{split}\Big|{\mathcal{M}}_{\infty,\varepsilon}^{\varphi,\psi}\Big|\leq ce^{\frac{2\lambda}{\varepsilon}}\|\psi\|_{\infty}^{4}\varepsilon^{3}\sum\limits_{r=1}^{\infty}C^{r}\sum\limits_{I_{1},\cdots,I_{r}\atop|I_{i}|\leq 3,1\leq i\leq r}&\Big\|\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{\otimes 4}\Big\|_{\ell^{p}}\Big\|\widehat{{\mathcal{P}}}_{\lambda,\varepsilon}^{*,I_{1}}\Big\|_{\ell^{q}\to\ell^{q}}\Big\|\widehat{{\mathcal{P}}}_{\lambda,\varepsilon}^{I_{1},I_{2}}\Big\|_{\ell^{q}\to\ell^{q}}\\
&\cdots\Big\|\widehat{{\mathcal{P}}}_{\lambda,\varepsilon}^{I_{r-1},I_{r}}\Big\|_{\ell^{q}\to\ell^{q}}\Big\|\widehat{{\mathcal{Q}}}_{\lambda,\varepsilon}^{I_{r},*}\Big\|_{\ell^{q}\to\ell^{q}}\Big\|w_{\varepsilon}^{\otimes 4}\mathbbm{1}_{B_{\varepsilon}}^{\otimes 4}\Big\|_{\ell^{q}}.\end{split}

Recall the definition of
\widehat{{\mathcal{P}}}_{\lambda,\varepsilon}^{I,J}
from (
6.17
). Since we will let
\varepsilon\to 0
, we actually have that

\Big\|\widehat{{\mathcal{P}}}_{\lambda,\varepsilon}^{I,J}\Big\|_{\ell^{q}\to\ell^{q}}\leq c\Big(\frac{1}{\log\widehat{\lambda}}\vee\frac{1}{(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon})^{\frac{3}{4}}}\Big)=\frac{c}{\log\widehat{\lambda}}.

Hence, noting that number of partition of
\{1,2,3,4\}
is finite, we then have that

\Big|{\mathcal{M}}_{\infty,\varepsilon}^{\varphi,\psi}\Big|\leq ce^{\frac{2\lambda}{\varepsilon}}\varepsilon^{2}\|\psi\|_{\infty}^{4}\bigg\|\frac{\varphi_{\varepsilon}}{w_{\varepsilon}}\bigg\|_{\ell^{p}}^{4}\|w_{\varepsilon}\mathbbm{1}_{B_{\varepsilon}}\|_{\ell^{q}}^{4}\sum\limits_{r=1}^{\infty}\Big(\frac{C^{\prime}}{\log\widehat{\lambda}}\Big)^{r}\leq C_{\widehat{\lambda}}\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{p}}\|\psi\|_{\infty}^{4}\bigg\|\frac{\varphi_{\varepsilon}}{w_{\varepsilon}}\bigg\|_{\ell^{p}}^{4}\|w\mathbbm{1}_{B}\|^{4}_{\ell^{q}}

by choosing
\widehat{\lambda}
large enough.
∎

We now prove Proposition
6.5
, which is similar to Proposition
4.3
. Some extra work is needed to treat
w
. First note that by (
4.28
), we have that

(6.24)

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\lambda,\varepsilon}^{I,J}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})\leq\sum\limits_{n=1}^{2/\varepsilon}\prod\limits_{i=1}^{4}g_{n}(y_{i}-x_{i})\leq\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{C}{1+|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|^{2}},\quad&\text{for all}~\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y}\in({\mathbbm{Z}})^{4},\\[16.0pt]
\displaystyle C\varepsilon e^{-\frac{\varepsilon|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|^{2}}{C}},\quad&\text{for}~|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}.\end{cases}

We split the space by
A_{\varepsilon}:=\{|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|<C/\sqrt{\varepsilon}\}
and
A_{\varepsilon}^{c}
. Note that
\log w
is Lipschitz continuous. Hence,
|\log w_{\varepsilon}(x)-\log w_{\varepsilon}(y)|\leq C^{\prime}\sqrt{\varepsilon}|x-y|
, which implies that

(6.25)

\widehat{{\mathcal{Q}}}_{\lambda,\varepsilon}^{I,J}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y}):=\frac{w_{\varepsilon}^{\otimes 4}(\boldsymbol{x})}{w_{\varepsilon}^{\otimes 4}(\boldsymbol{y})}{\mathcal{Q}}_{\lambda,\varepsilon}^{I,J}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})\leq\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{Ce^{C_{0}}}{1+|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|^{2}}:=\frac{C}{1+|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|^{2}},&\quad\text{on}~A_{\varepsilon},\\[16.0pt]
\displaystyle C\varepsilon e^{-\frac{\varepsilon|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|^{2}+C^{\prime}\sqrt{\varepsilon}|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|}{C}}:=C_{1}\varepsilon e^{-C_{1}\varepsilon|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|},&\quad\text{on}~A_{\varepsilon}^{c}.\end{cases}

To prove (
6.20
), (
6.21
) and (
6.22
), we split the space by
A_{\varepsilon}
and
A_{\varepsilon}^{c}
, and the computations are exactly the same as those in the proofs for (
4.19
), (
4.20
) and (
4.21
). Therefore, we omit the details. Note that in (
4.11
),
k
transition kernels are equipped with
{\mathbbm{E}}[\zeta^{2}]^{k}
, but in (
6.5
),
r-1
heat kernels are equipped with
(\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{r}
. Hence an extra
\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}
is not needed in (
6.22
), compared to (
4.21
).

Proof of (
6.23
)
.

We need to show that there exists some
C>0
, such that uniformly for any
\varphi\in\ell^{p}(({\mathbbm{Z}})_{I}^{h})
and
\psi\in\ell^{q}(({\mathbbm{Z}})_{J}^{h})
,

(6.26)

\sum\limits_{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y}\in({\mathbbm{Z}})_{I}^{4}}\varphi(\boldsymbol{x})\widehat{{\mathbf{V}}}_{\infty,\varepsilon}^{I}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})\psi(\boldsymbol{y})\leq\frac{C}{\left(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\frac{3}{4}}}\|\varphi\|_{\ell^{p}}\|\psi\|_{\ell^{q}}.

Recall (
6.16
), (
6.11
) and (
6.8
). We know that

\widehat{{\mathbf{V}}}_{\infty,\varepsilon}^{I}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})\leq\frac{C}{\big(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\big)^{\frac{3}{4}}}\frac{w_{\varepsilon}^{\otimes 4}(\boldsymbol{x})}{w_{\varepsilon}^{\otimes 4}(\boldsymbol{y})}\prod\limits_{j\notin I(1)}\mathbbm{1}_{\{x_{j}=y_{j}\}}.

Then
\widehat{{\mathbf{V}}}_{\infty,\varepsilon}^{I}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})\neq 0
if and only if
\boldsymbol{x}=\boldsymbol{y}
. By Hölder’s inequality, (
6.26
) is bounded above by

\begin{split}\frac{C}{\left(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\frac{3}{4}}}\Big(\sum\limits_{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y}\in({\mathbbm{Z}})_{I}^{4}}|\varphi(\boldsymbol{x})|^{p}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\boldsymbol{x}=\boldsymbol{y}\}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}\Big(\sum\limits_{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y}\in({\mathbbm{Z}})_{I}^{4}}|\psi(\boldsymbol{y})|^{p}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\boldsymbol{x}=\boldsymbol{y}\}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{q}}\leq\frac{C}{\left(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\frac{3}{4}}}\|\varphi\|_{\ell^{p}}\|\psi\|_{\ell^{q}}.\end{split}

∎

7.
Proof of Theorem
1.7

We prove Theorem
1.7
by showing that for
\varphi\in C_{c}({\mathbbm{R}})
and
\psi\in C_{b}({\mathbbm{R}})
,
{\mathcal{Z}}_{N}=\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{N}^{\beta_{N}}(\varphi,\psi)
converges in distribution to a unique limit.

Proof of the convergence of
{\mathcal{Z}}_{N}
.

First note that by Proposition
2.3
,
\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbbm{E}}[({\mathcal{Z}}_{N})^{2}]
exists. Hence,
({\mathcal{Z}}_{N})_{N\geq 1}
are tight and have sequential weak limits. To show the limit is unique, We show that
({\mathcal{Z}}_{N})_{N\geq 1}
is a Cauchy sequence.

It suffices to show that for any bounded
f:{\mathbbm{R}}\to{\mathbbm{R}}
with uniformly bounded first three derivatives, the limit
\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbbm{E}}[f({\mathcal{Z}}_{N})]
exists (
cf.

[
Bil13
]
). We then show that
{\mathbbm{E}}[f({\mathcal{Z}}_{N})]
is an Cauchy sequence.

Again, by Proposition
2.3
and its proof, we can see that given any
\varepsilon>0
, we can truncate and mollify
\psi
to get
\psi_{\varepsilon}\in C_{c}({\mathbbm{R}})
, such that by dominated convergence theorem,

\limsup\limits_{N\to\infty}\big\|{\mathcal{Z}}_{N}(\varphi,\psi)-{\mathcal{Z}}_{N}(\varphi,\psi_{\varepsilon})\big\|_{2}\leq\varepsilon.

Hence, in the following, we work on
\varphi,\psi\in C_{c}({\mathbbm{R}})
.

Note that we have shown that
{\mathcal{Z}}_{N}
can be approximated by coarse-grained model
\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(cg)}}(\varphi,\psi|\Theta_{N,\varepsilon})
in
L^{2}
. The function
f
is Lipschitz continuous, and we have that

\begin{split}\big|{\mathbbm{E}}[f({\mathcal{Z}}_{N})]-{\mathbbm{E}}[f(\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(cg)}}(\varphi,\psi|\Theta_{N,\varepsilon}))]\big|\leq C\|f^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\big\|{\mathcal{Z}}_{N}-\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(cg)}}(\varphi,\psi|\Theta_{N,\varepsilon})\big\|_{L^{2}}=o_{\varepsilon}(1),\quad\text{as}~\varepsilon\to 0.\end{split}

Hence, it suffices to show that

(7.1)

\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{m,n\geq N}\big|{\mathbbm{E}}\big[f(\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(cg)}}(\varphi,\psi|\Theta_{n,\varepsilon}))\big]-{\mathbbm{E}}\big[f(\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(cg)}}(\varphi,\psi|\Theta_{m,\varepsilon}))\big]\big|=0.

To lighten the notations, we write
\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}(\Theta_{N})=\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(cg)}}(\varphi,\psi|\Theta_{N,\varepsilon})
. We will prove (
7.1
) by a generalized Lindeberg principle (
cf.

[
MOO10
,
Röl13
,
CSZ23
]
). We first verify the local dependence Assumption
A.1
for
\Theta(\vec{\mathrm{i}})
. Recall that for indices

\vec{\mathrm{i}}\in{\mathbbm{T}}_{\varepsilon}:=\{\vec{\mathrm{i}}=(\mathrm{i},\mathrm{i}^{\prime}):|\vec{\mathrm{i}}|=\mathrm{i}^{\prime}-\mathrm{i}-1\leq K_{\varepsilon}\},

its dependency neighborhood is

(7.2)

A_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}}=\{\vec{\mathrm{j}}=(\mathrm{j},\mathrm{j}^{\prime})\in{\mathbbm{T}}_{\varepsilon}:\{\mathrm{i},\mathrm{i}^{\prime}\}\cap\{\mathrm{j},\mathrm{j}^{\prime}\}\neq\emptyset\}.

It is not hard to see that
|A_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}}|\leq 4K_{\varepsilon}
. Besides, for
\vec{\mathrm{i}}\in{\mathbbm{T}}_{\varepsilon}
and
\vec{\mathrm{j}}\in A_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}}
, the dependency neighborhood for
\vec{\mathrm{i}}
and
\vec{\mathrm{j}}
is denoted by
A_{\vec{\mathrm{i}},\vec{\mathrm{j}}}=A_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}}\cup A_{\vec{\mathrm{j}}}
.

We now verify the condition (
A.6
) in order to apply Lemma
A.3
, that is,

(7.3)

\forall~\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1}\in{\mathbbm{T}}_{\varepsilon},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}\in A_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1}},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{3}\in A_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}},\quad\partial_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1}\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}}^{2}\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}(\Theta)=\partial_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1}\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{3}}^{2}\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}(\Theta)=\partial_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}}^{3}\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}(\Theta)=0,

where
\partial_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}}
is the partial derivative with respect to
\Theta(\vec{\mathrm{i}})
. Note that
\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}(\Theta)
is a polynomial of
\Theta
. We only need to verify that in each term of
\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}(\Theta)
, there is at most one
\Theta(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{k})
,
k=1,2,3
. Given
\Theta(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1})
and
\Theta(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2})
in
\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}(\Theta)
, we must have
\mathrm{i}_{2}>\mathrm{i}^{\prime}_{1}
or
\mathrm{i}_{1}>\mathrm{i}^{\prime}_{2}
. However, by the
\vec{\mathbf{{\mathcal{A}}}}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(no triple)}}
condition, we must have
\mathrm{i}_{2}-\mathrm{i}^{\prime}_{1}>K_{\varepsilon}
and
\mathrm{i}_{1}-\mathrm{i}^{\prime}_{2}>K_{\varepsilon}
. Hence,
\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}\not\in A_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1}}
, that is, no terms contain the product
\Theta(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1})\Theta(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2})
for
\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}\in A_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1}}
. By the same argument, given
\Theta(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1}),\Theta(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2})
and
\Theta(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{3})
in
\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}(\Theta)
, we must have
\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{3}\notin A_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1}}
and
\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{3}\notin A_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}}
. We have now verified (
7.3
).

By Lemma
A.1
-
A.3
, we can bound (
7.1
) by

(7.4)

\big|{\mathbbm{E}}\big[f(\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}(\Theta_{m})\big]-{\mathbbm{E}}\big[f(\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}(\Theta_{n}))\big]\big|\leq I_{1}^{(m)}+I_{1}^{(n)}+I_{2}^{(m)}+I_{2}^{(n)}+I_{3}^{(m,n)}.

Here
I_{1}^{(m)},I_{1}^{(n)},I_{2}^{(m)}
and
I_{2}^{(n)}
comes from Lemma
A.1
and
I_{3}^{(m,n)}
come from Lemma
A.2
. We have applied the lemmas to
X_{k}=\Theta_{k}
,
h(\cdot)=f(\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}(\cdot))
and
Z_{k}=\Theta^{\rm(G)}_{k}
a family of centered Gaussian random variable with the same covariance structure as
\Theta_{k}
, for
k=m,n
. We will show that

(7.5)

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup\limits_{n\to\infty}I_{1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=0,

(7.6)

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup\limits_{n\to\infty}I_{2}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=0,

(7.7)

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup\limits_{n,m\to\infty}I_{3}^{(m,n)}

\displaystyle=0.

Proof for (
7.5
).
By (
A.7
), we have that

(7.8)

\begin{split}|I_{1}^{(n)}|\leq&\frac{1}{2}\|f^{\prime\prime\prime}\|_{\infty}\sup_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1}\in{\mathbbm{T}}_{\varepsilon}}{\mathbbm{E}}\big[|\Theta_{n}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1})|^{3}\big]\sum\limits_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1}\in{\mathbbm{T}}_{\varepsilon},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}\in A_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1}},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{3}\in A_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}}}\\
&\sup_{s,t,u}{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\big|\partial_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1}}\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}(W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}})\big|^{3}\Big]^{\frac{1}{3}}\sup_{s,t,u}{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\big|\partial_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}}\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}(W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}})\big|^{3}\Big]^{\frac{1}{3}}\sup_{s,t,u}{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\big|\partial_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{3}}\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}(W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}})\big|^{3}\Big]^{\frac{1}{3}},\end{split}

where for
s,t,u\in[0,1]
,

W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}}:=su\sqrt{t}\Theta_{n}^{A_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1}}}+u\sqrt{t}\Theta_{n}^{A_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}}\backslash A_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1}}}+\sqrt{t}\Theta_{n}^{A_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}}^{c}}+\sqrt{1-t}\Theta_{n}^{\rm(G)},\quad\text{with}~\Theta_{n}^{A}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}):=\Theta_{n}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})\mathbbm{1}_{\{\vec{\mathrm{i}}\in A\}}.

First note that there are finite many terms in
\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}(\Theta_{n})
, since
\varphi,\psi\in C_{c}({\mathbbm{R}})
. Hence, the number of indices
\vec{\mathrm{i}}
is finite and we can pass the
\lim_{n\to\infty}
inside the summation. By Lemma
6.2
and the boundedness of
f^{\prime\prime\prime}
, we readily have that

(7.9)

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1}\in{\mathbbm{T}}_{\varepsilon}}\|f^{\prime\prime\prime}\|_{\infty}{\mathbbm{E}}\big[|\Theta_{n}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1})|^{3}\big]\leq C\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1}\in{\mathbbm{T}}_{\varepsilon}}{\mathbbm{E}}\big[|\Theta_{n}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1})|^{4}\big]^{\frac{3}{4}}\leq\frac{C}{(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon})^{\frac{3}{4}}}.

Then, by AM-GM inequality, the summation in (
7.8
) is bounded by

(7.10)

\sum\limits_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1}\in{\mathbbm{T}}_{\varepsilon},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}\in A_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1}},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{3}\in A_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}}}\frac{1}{3}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{3}\sup_{s,t,u}{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\Big|\partial_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{k}}\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}\Big(W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}}\Big)\Big|^{4}\Big]^{\frac{3}{4}}.

The next step is to compute the partial derivative of
\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}(W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}})
.

Note that we can write

(7.11)

W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})=\begin{cases}su\sqrt{t}\Theta_{n}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})+\sqrt{1-t}\Theta_{n}^{\rm(G)}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}),\quad&\text{for}~\vec{\mathrm{i}}\in A_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1}},\\
u\sqrt{t}\Theta_{n}(\vec{\vec{\mathrm{i}}})+\sqrt{1-t}\Theta_{n}^{\rm(G)}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}),\quad&\text{for}~\vec{\mathrm{i}}\in A_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}}\backslash A_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1}},\\
\sqrt{t}\Theta_{n}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})+\sqrt{1-t}\Theta_{n}^{\rm(G)}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}),\quad&\text{for}~\vec{\mathrm{i}}\in A_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}}^{c}.\end{cases}

Hence, by recalling the definition of
\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}
from (
2.46
), we have that

\begin{split}\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}\Big(W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{2}}\Big)=g_{1}(\varphi,\psi)+&\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sum\limits_{r=1}^{(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon})^{2}}\sum\limits_{(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1},\cdots,\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{r})\in\vec{\mathbf{{\mathcal{A}}}}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(no triple)}}}\sum\limits_{\mathrm{a},\mathrm{b}\in{\mathbbm{Z}}}\\
&\varphi_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{a})g_{\mathrm{i}_{1}}(\mathrm{a})W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{2}}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1})\times\bigg\{\prod_{j=2}^{r}g_{(\mathrm{i}_{j}-\mathrm{i}^{\prime}_{j-1})}(0)W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{i},\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{2}}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{j})\bigg\}g_{(\lfloor\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\rfloor-\mathrm{i}^{\prime}_{r})}(\mathrm{b})\psi_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{b}),\end{split}

where we use
\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{2}
to avoid the notations abuse for
\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}
in the summation. Suppose that we are differentiating
\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}
with respect to
\Theta_{n}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})
in the above expression with
\vec{\mathrm{i}}=\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{k}
for some
1\leq k\leq r
. Then we only differentiate
W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{2}}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})
and get that

(7.12)

\partial_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}}W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{2}}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})=\begin{cases}su\sqrt{t},\quad&\text{for}~\vec{\mathrm{i}}\in A_{\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{1}},\\
u\sqrt{t},\quad&\text{for}~\vec{\mathrm{i}}\in A_{\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{2}}\backslash A_{\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{1}},\\
\sqrt{t},\quad&\text{for}~\vec{\mathrm{i}}\in A_{\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{2}}^{c}.\end{cases}

Then we can see that the consecutive product stops at
g_{(\mathrm{i}_{k}-\mathrm{i}^{\prime}_{k-1})}
and restarts at
g_{(\mathrm{i}_{k+1}-\mathrm{i}^{\prime}_{k})}
. Also, the constant term
g_{1}(\varphi,\psi)
disappears. We find that

(7.13)

\partial_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}}\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}\Big(W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}}\Big)=C(\vec{\mathrm{i}})\frac{1}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\overline{\mathscr{L}}_{\varepsilon,[0,\mathrm{i}]}\Big(\varphi,\delta_{0}|W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}}\Big)\overline{\mathscr{L}}_{\varepsilon,[\mathrm{i}^{\prime},\frac{1}{\varepsilon}]}\Big(\delta_{0},\psi|W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}}\Big),

where
C(\mathrm{i})
is the constant determined by (
7.12
),
\delta_{0}
is the Dirac measure, and
\overline{\mathscr{L}}_{\varepsilon,[t_{1},t_{2}]}
is a coarse-grained model without the deterministic term and having the same structure as
\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}
with initial time
t_{1}
and terminal time
t_{2}
. Here we need to multiply an
1/\sqrt{\varepsilon}
because we decompose one coarse-grained model by two coarse-grained models, and thus an extra
\sqrt{\varepsilon}
is needed by the definition of
\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}
.

We then have

(7.14)

{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\Big|\partial_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}}\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}\Big(W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}}\Big)\Big|^{4}\Big]\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\Big|\overline{\mathscr{L}}_{\varepsilon,[0,\mathrm{i}]}\Big(\delta_{0},\varphi|W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}}\Big)\Big|^{4}\Big]{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\Big|\overline{\mathscr{L}}_{\varepsilon,[\mathrm{i}^{\prime},\frac{1}{\varepsilon}]}\Big(\delta_{0},\psi|W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}}\Big)\Big|^{4}\Big],

where we have used
C(\mathrm{i})\leq 1
by
s,u,t\in[0,1]
and the time reversal invariance for
\overline{\mathscr{L}}_{\varepsilon}
for the first expectation. Note that
\overline{\mathscr{L}}_{\varepsilon}(W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}})
has mean
0
and we have the following analogue of Theorem
6.3
for coarse-grained disorders
W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}}
.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let all settings be the same as in Theorem
6.3
and recall the definition of
W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{i}},\vec{\mathrm{j}}}
from (
7.11
). We have that, there exists some constant
C\in(0,+\infty)
, such that for
w(x)=\exp(-|x|)
,

{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\Big|\overline{\mathscr{L}}_{\varepsilon,[0,1]}\Big(\varphi,\psi|W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{i}},\vec{\mathrm{j}}}\Big)\Big|^{4}\Big]\leq C\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{p}}\bigg\|\frac{\varphi_{\varepsilon}}{w_{\varepsilon}}\bigg\|^{4}_{\ell^{p}}\|\psi\|_{\infty}^{4}\|w\mathbbm{1}_{B}\|^{4}_{\ell^{q}},

uniformly for all
\mathrm{i},\mathrm{j}\in{\mathbbm{T}}_{\varepsilon}
,
s,u,t\in[0,1]
and
n
large (note that
n
comes from
\Theta_{n}
).

The proof of Lemma
7.1
is similar to Theorem
6.3
. We only need to obtain some moment bounds on
W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{i}},\vec{\mathrm{j}}}
. We postpone the proof of the lemma later. Note that in the proof of Theorem
6.3
, we do not use the continuity of
\varphi
and
\psi
but only the boundedness of their values and supports. Hence, the above bound is also valid for
\varphi=\delta_{0}
. Also note that by assuming
{\mathbbm{E}}[\zeta_{n}^{3}]\geq 0
, the above fourth moment is increasing as the time interval getting larger. By (
7.2
), (
7.14
) and Lemma
7.1
, we have that

\begin{split}&\sum\limits_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1}\in{\mathbbm{T}}_{\varepsilon},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}\in A_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1}},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{3}\in A_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}}}\frac{1}{3}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{3}\sup_{s,t,u}{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\Big|\partial_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{k}}\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}\Big(W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}}\Big)\Big|^{4}\Big]^{\frac{3}{4}}\\
\leq&\sum\limits_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1}\in{\mathbbm{T}}_{\varepsilon},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}\in A_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1}},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{3}\in A_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}}}C\bigg\|\frac{(\delta_{0})_{\varepsilon}}{w_{\varepsilon}}\bigg\|^{6}_{\ell^{p}}\|\psi\|_{\infty}^{6}\|w\mathbbm{1}_{B}\|^{6}_{\ell^{q}}\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{p}-\frac{3}{2}}\leq C_{\psi}\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{p}-\frac{5}{2}}(K_{\varepsilon})^{2}.\end{split}

Hence, by choosing
1<p<\frac{6}{5}
, there exist some
C=C_{\psi,p}
and
\delta>0
such that
\limsup_{n\to\infty}|I_{1}|\leq C\varepsilon^{\delta}
, since
K_{\varepsilon}=(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon})^{6}
, which concludes (
7.5
).

Proof for (
7.6
).
By (
A.8
), we have that

(7.15)

\begin{split}|I_{1}^{(n)}|\leq&\frac{1}{2}\|f^{\prime\prime\prime}\|_{\infty}\sup_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1}\in{\mathbbm{T}}_{\varepsilon}}{\mathbbm{E}}\big[|\Theta_{n}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1})|^{3}\big]\sum\limits_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1}\in{\mathbbm{T}}_{\varepsilon},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}\in A_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1}},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{3}\in A_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}}}\\
&\sup_{t,u}{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\big|\partial_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1}}\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}(W_{t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}})\big|^{3}\Big]^{\frac{1}{3}}\sup_{t,u}{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\big|\partial_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}}\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}(W_{t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}})\big|^{3}\Big]^{\frac{1}{3}}\sup_{t,u}{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\big|\partial_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{3}}\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}(W_{t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}})\big|^{3}\Big]^{\frac{1}{3}},\end{split}

where for
t,u\in[0,1]
,

W_{t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}}:=u\sqrt{t}\Theta_{n}^{A_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}}}+\sqrt{t}\Theta_{n}^{A_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}}^{c}}+\sqrt{1-t}\Theta_{n}^{\rm(G)}.

The proof are exactly the same as that for (
7.5
). We can find some constant
C\in(0,+\infty)
and
\delta>0
, such that
\limsup_{n\to\infty}|I_{2}|\leq C\varepsilon^{\delta}
, which concludes the (
7.6
). Note that
W_{t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}}
and
W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}}
have the same structure. In particular, both of them can be expressed by
c_{1}\Theta_{N}+c_{2}\Theta_{N}^{\rm(G)}
with
c_{1},c_{2}\in[0,1]
(see (
7.11
)). Hence, we can have a bound similar to Lemma
7.1
, which is

{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\Big|\overline{\mathscr{L}}_{\varepsilon,[0,1]}\Big(\varphi,\psi|W_{t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{i}},\vec{\mathrm{j}}}\Big)\Big|^{4}\Big]\leq C\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{p}}\bigg\|\frac{\varphi_{\varepsilon}}{w_{\varepsilon}}\bigg\|^{4}_{\ell^{p}}\|\psi\|_{\infty}^{4}\|w\mathbbm{1}_{B}\|^{4}_{\ell^{q}},

and we omit its proof.

Proof for (
7.7
).
By (
A.9
) and note that
(\Theta_{n}({\vec{\mathrm{i}}}))_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}\in{\mathbbm{T}}_{\varepsilon}}
are uncorrelated, we have that

(7.16)

|I_{3}|\leq\frac{1}{2}\|f^{\prime\prime}\|_{\infty}\sum\limits_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}\in{\mathbbm{T}}_{\varepsilon}}\Big({\mathbbm{E}}\big[\Theta_{n}^{2}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})\big]-{\mathbbm{E}}\big[\Theta_{m}^{2}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})\big]\Big)\sup\limits_{t\in[0,1]}{\mathbbm{E}}\big[|\partial_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}}\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}(W_{t})|^{2}\big],

where for
t\in[0,1]
,
W_{t}:=\sqrt{t}\Theta_{n}^{\rm(G)}+\sqrt{1-t}\Theta_{m}^{\rm(G)}
.

First note that by Lemma
6.1
, for any fixed
\varepsilon>0
,
\lim_{m,n\to\infty}\big({\mathbbm{E}}[\Theta_{n}^{2}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})]-{\mathbbm{E}}[\Theta_{m}^{2}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})]\big)=0
. Then note that
\partial_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}}\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}(W_{t})
is a multilinear polynomial in
W_{t}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})
(see the discussion for (
7.13
)). Hence,
{\mathbbm{E}}[|\partial_{\vec{\mathrm{i}}}\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}(W_{t})|^{2}]
is a multilinear polynomial in
{\mathbbm{E}}[W_{t}^{2}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})]
, which is a linear combination of
{\mathbbm{E}}[\Theta_{n}^{2}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})]
and
{\mathbbm{E}}[\Theta_{m}^{2}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})]
(we can assume that
\Theta_{n}^{\rm(G)}
and
\Theta_{m}^{\rm(G)}
are independent). Again by Lemma
6.1
and noting that there are finite many
{\mathbbm{E}}[W_{t}^{2}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})]
for fixed
\varepsilon
, we have shown that
\lim_{n,m\to\infty}I_{3}^{(m,n)}=0
.

Combine (
7.5
)-(
7.7
) together and we have shown that
{\mathcal{Z}}_{N}
converges to a unique limit.
∎

Remark 7.2
.

We can actually extend Theorem
1.7
to a convergence in finite-dimensional distribution. For any fixed positive integer
k
, let
-\infty<s_{i}<t_{i}<+\infty
and
\varphi_{i},\psi_{i}\in C_{c}({\mathbbm{R}})
for
i=1,2,\cdots,k
, each component
{\mathcal{Z}}_{\lfloor s_{i}N\rfloor,\lfloor t_{i}N\rfloor}^{\beta_{N}}(\varphi_{i},\psi_{i})
in the random vector
({\mathcal{Z}}_{\lfloor s_{i}N\rfloor,\lfloor t_{i}N\rfloor}^{\beta_{N}}(\varphi_{i},\psi_{i}))_{i=1,\cdots,k}
can be approximated in
L_{2}
by a coarse-grained model
\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon,[s_{i},t_{i}]}(\varphi_{i},\psi_{i}|\Theta_{N,\varepsilon})
. We then have an
L_{2}
approximation in vector level for fixed
k
. For any bounded test function
f:{\mathbbm{R}}^{k}\to{\mathbbm{R}}
with bounded derivatives up to third order. The Lindeberg principle also holds for
f(\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon,[s_{1},t_{1}]}(\cdot),\cdots,\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon,[s_{k},t_{k}]}(\cdot))
(see remark
A.4
). The proof for single
{\mathcal{Z}}_{N}
can be applied here without any change.

We are left to prove Lemma
7.1
.

Proof of Lemma
7.1
.

We write

(7.17)

\begin{split}{\mathbf{M}}_{n,\varepsilon}^{\varphi,\psi}:&={\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\Big(\overline{\mathscr{L}}_{\varepsilon,[0,1]}\Big(\varphi,\psi|W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{2}}\Big)\Big)^{4}\Big]=\varepsilon^{2}{\mathbbm{E}}\bigg[\bigg(\sum\limits_{r=1}^{(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon})^{2}}\sum\limits_{(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1},\cdots,\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{r})\in\vec{\mathbf{{\mathcal{A}}}}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(no triple)}}}\sum\limits_{\mathrm{a},\mathrm{b}\in{\mathbbm{Z}}}\\
&\quad\quad\varphi_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{a})g_{\mathrm{i}_{1}}(\mathrm{a})W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{2}}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1})\times\Big\{\prod_{j=2}^{r}g_{(\mathrm{i}_{j}-\mathrm{i}^{\prime}_{j-1})}(0)W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{2}}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{j})\Big\}g_{(\lfloor\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\rfloor-\mathrm{i}^{\prime}_{r})}(\mathrm{b})\psi_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{b})\bigg)^{4}\bigg].\end{split}

Recall the definition of
W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{2}}
from (
7.11
). We can write
W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{2}}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})=c_{1}\Theta_{n}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})+c_{2}\Theta_{n}^{\rm(G)}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})
, where
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
are determined by
\vec{\mathrm{i}}=(\mathrm{i},\mathrm{i}^{\prime})
. Since
\Theta_{n}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})
and
\Theta_{n}^{\rm(G)}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})
are independent, the expectation of their products can be factorized. Similar to
\Theta_{n}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})
,
W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{2}}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})
and
W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{2}}(\vec{\mathrm{j}})
are uncorrelated for
\mathrm{i}\neq\mathrm{j}
. Also note that since the first and third moments of
\Theta_{n}^{\rm(G)}
is 0, only its second and fourth moments

(7.18)

{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\big(\Theta_{n}^{\rm(G)}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})\big)^{2}\Big]={\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\big(\Theta_{n}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})\big)^{2}\Big]\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\big(\Theta_{n}^{\rm(G)}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})\big)^{4}\Big]=3{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\big(\Theta_{n}^{\rm(G)}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})\big)^{2}\Big]^{2}=3{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\big(\Theta_{n}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})\big)^{2}\Big]^{2}

can appear in the expansion. It means that to have a nonzero moment of some
\Theta_{n}^{G}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})
,
\vec{\mathrm{i}}
must appear in the expansion for at least two times.

We can now adapt the same indices rearrangement in the proof of Theorem
6.3
. Suppose that
1\leq n\leq\frac{2}{\varepsilon}
is a renewal time. Then at least two mesoscopic renewal processes visit it, since this is necessary to have a nonzero moments of
\Theta_{n}
or
\Theta_{n}^{\rm(G)}
. We can partition all renewal times into groups
{\mathcal{I}}_{1},\cdots,{\mathcal{I}}_{k}
. We are going to decouple
W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{2}}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})
with
\mathrm{i}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{s}
and
\mathrm{i}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{t}
for
t>s
.

To lighten the notations, we denote

{\mathcal{E}}_{W}^{k}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}):={\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\Big(W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{2}}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})\Big)^{k}\Big],\quad{\mathcal{E}}_{\Theta}^{k}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}):={\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\big(\Theta_{n}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})\big)^{k}\Big],\quad{\mathcal{E}}_{G}^{k}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}):={\mathbbm{E}}\Big[(\Theta_{n}^{\rm(G)}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}))^{k}\Big].

If
\vec{\mathrm{i}}
appears for four times, then we must have

(7.19)

\begin{split}&{\mathcal{E}}_{W}^{4}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})=c_{1}^{4}{\mathcal{E}}_{\Theta}^{4}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})+6c_{1}^{2}c_{2}^{2}{\mathcal{E}}_{\Theta}^{2}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}){\mathcal{E}}_{G}^{2}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})+c_{2}^{4}{\mathcal{E}}_{G}^{4}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})\\
\leq C\big(c_{1}^{4}{\mathcal{E}}_{\Theta}^{4}((\mathrm{i},\mathrm{i}))&(g_{(\mathrm{i}^{\prime}-\mathrm{i})}(0))^{4}{\mathcal{E}}_{\Theta}^{4}((\mathrm{i}^{\prime},\mathrm{i}^{\prime}))+6c_{1}^{2}c_{2}^{2}{\mathcal{E}}_{\Theta}^{2}((\mathrm{i},\mathrm{i}))(g_{(\mathrm{i}^{\prime}-\mathrm{i})}(0))^{2}{\mathcal{E}}_{\Theta}^{2}((\mathrm{i}^{\prime},\mathrm{i}^{\prime})){\mathcal{E}}_{G}^{2}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})+c_{2}^{4}{\mathcal{E}}_{G}^{4}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})\big),\end{split}

where we have used the same argument of step (A) in “
adjustments on coarse-grained disorders and heat kernels
” in the proof of Theorem
6.3
to replace
\Theta_{n}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})
by
C\Theta_{n}((\mathrm{i},\mathrm{i}))g_{(\mathrm{i}^{\prime}-\mathrm{i})}(0)\Theta_{n}((\mathrm{i}^{\prime},\mathrm{i}^{\prime}))
.

Meanwhile, we have that

\begin{split}{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\Big(W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{2}}((\mathrm{i},\mathrm{i}))g_{(\mathrm{i}^{\prime}-\mathrm{i})}(0)W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{2}}((\mathrm{i}^{\prime},\mathrm{i}^{\prime}))\Big)^{4}\Big]={\mathcal{E}}_{W}^{4}((\mathrm{i},\mathrm{i}))(g_{(\mathrm{i}^{\prime}-\mathrm{i})}(0))^{4}{\mathcal{E}}_{W}^{4}((\mathrm{i}^{\prime},\mathrm{i}^{\prime})).\end{split}

Note that by (
7.18
),

{\mathcal{E}}_{G}^{2}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})={\mathcal{E}}_{\Theta}^{2}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})\leq C{\mathcal{E}}_{\Theta}^{2}((\mathrm{i},\mathrm{i}))(g_{(\mathrm{i}^{\prime}-\mathrm{i})}(0))^{2}{\mathcal{E}}_{\Theta}^{2}((\mathrm{i}^{\prime},\mathrm{i}^{\prime}))=C{\mathcal{E}}_{G}^{2}((\mathrm{i},\mathrm{i}))(g_{(\mathrm{i}^{\prime}-\mathrm{i})}(0))^{2}{\mathcal{E}}_{G}^{2}((\mathrm{i}^{\prime},\mathrm{i}^{\prime})),

and

{\mathcal{E}}_{G}^{4}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})=3({\mathcal{E}}_{G}^{2}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}))^{2}\leq C({\mathcal{E}}_{G}^{2}((\mathrm{i},\mathrm{i}))(g_{(\mathrm{i}^{\prime}-\mathrm{i})}(0))^{2}{\mathcal{E}}_{G}^{2}((\mathrm{i}^{\prime},\mathrm{i}^{\prime})))^{2}=C^{\prime}{\mathcal{E}}_{G}^{4}((\mathrm{i},\mathrm{i}))(g_{(\mathrm{i}^{\prime}-\mathrm{i})}(0))^{4}{\mathcal{E}}_{G}^{4}((\mathrm{i}^{\prime},\mathrm{i}^{\prime})).

Hence, by (
7.19
),

{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\Big(W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{2}}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})\Big)^{4}\Big]\leq C{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\Big(W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{2}}((\mathrm{i},\mathrm{i}))g_{(\mathrm{i}^{\prime}-\mathrm{i})}(0)W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{2}}((\mathrm{i}^{\prime},\mathrm{i}^{\prime}))\Big)^{4}\Big],

and we can replace
W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{2}}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})
by
W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{2}}((\mathrm{i},\mathrm{i}))g_{(\mathrm{i}^{\prime}-\mathrm{i})}(0)W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{2}}((\mathrm{i}^{\prime},\mathrm{i}^{\prime}))
as in the proof of Theorem
6.3
.

If
\vec{\mathrm{i}}
appears for three times, then at renewal time
\mathrm{i}
, there could be another renewal pair
\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1}
visits it, and at renewal time
\mathrm{i}^{\prime}
, there could also be another renewal pair
\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2}
visits it. Thus, we need to estimate

(7.20)

\begin{split}{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\big(\Theta_{n}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1})\big)^{k}\Big(W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{2}}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})\Big)^{3}\big(\Theta_{n}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2})\big)^{l}\Big],\quad\text{for}~k,l=0,1.\end{split}

The reason that we drop
\Theta_{n}^{\rm{G}}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{m})
for
m=1,2
is due to the fact that either they are absent or have zero means. The computation is similar to the above case and we only emphasize some key steps.

In the expansion of (
7.20
), we have the factorization

\begin{split}&{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\big(\Theta_{n}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1})\big)^{k}\big(\Theta_{n}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})\big)^{3}\big(\Theta_{n}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2})\big)^{l}\Big]\leq C{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\big(\Theta_{n}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1})\big)^{k}\big(\Theta_{n}((\mathrm{i},\mathrm{i}))\big)^{3}\Big](g_{(\mathrm{i}^{\prime}-\mathrm{i})}(0))^{3}{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\big(\Theta_{n}(\mathrm{i}^{\prime},\mathrm{i}^{\prime})\big)^{3}\big(\Theta_{n}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2})\big)^{l}\Big],\end{split}

and

\begin{split}&{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\big(\Theta_{n}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1})\big)^{k}\Theta_{n}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})\big(\Theta_{n}^{\rm(G)}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})\big)^{2}\big(\Theta_{n}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2})\big)^{l}\Big]\\
\leq&C{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\big(\Theta_{n}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1})\big)^{k}\Theta_{n}((\mathrm{i},\mathrm{i}))\Big](g_{(\mathrm{i}^{\prime}-\mathrm{i})}(0))^{3}{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\Theta_{n}(\mathrm{i}^{\prime},\mathrm{i}^{\prime})\big(\Theta_{n}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2})\big)^{l}\Big]{\mathcal{E}}_{\Theta}^{2}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})\\
\leq&C^{\prime}{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\big(\Theta_{n}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1})\big)^{k}\Theta_{n}((\mathrm{i},\mathrm{i}))\Big](g_{(\mathrm{i}^{\prime}-\mathrm{i})}(0))^{5}{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\Theta_{n}(\mathrm{i}^{\prime},\mathrm{i}^{\prime})\big(\Theta_{n}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2})\big)^{l}\Big]{\mathcal{E}}_{\Theta}^{2}((\mathrm{i},\mathrm{i})){\mathcal{E}}_{\Theta}^{2}((\mathrm{i}^{\prime},\mathrm{i}^{\prime})).\end{split}

All these terms in the expansion of (
7.20
) are contained in the expansion of

(7.21)

{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\big(\Theta_{n}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1})\big)^{k}\Big(W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{2}}((\mathrm{i},\mathrm{i}))g_{(\mathrm{i}^{\prime}-\mathrm{i})}(0)W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{2}}((\mathrm{i}^{\prime},\mathrm{i}^{\prime}))\Big)^{3}\big(\Theta_{n}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2})\big)^{l}\Big],\quad\text{for}~k,l=0,1.

If
\vec{\mathrm{i}}
appears for two times, then similarly, we only need to show that all terms in the expansion of

(7.22)

{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\big(\Theta_{n}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1})\big)^{k}\Big(W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{2}}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})\Big)^{3}\big(\Theta_{n}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2})\big)^{l}\Big]

are contained in the expansion of

(7.23)

{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\big(\Theta_{n}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{1})\big)^{k}\Big(W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{2}}((\mathrm{i},\mathrm{i}))g_{(\mathrm{i}^{\prime}-\mathrm{i})}(0)W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{2}}((\mathrm{i}^{\prime},\mathrm{i}^{\prime}))\Big)^{3}\big(\Theta_{n}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{2})\big)^{l}\Big],

for
k,l=0,1,2
. Note that we drop the moments of
\Theta_{n}^{\rm(G)}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}_{m})
for
m=1,2
by independence.

Finally, by the above replacement, the upper bound of (
7.17
) has the same structure of
\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(cg)}}(\Theta_{n})
. Proposition
6.5
also holds given that we have the same second and fourth moments bounds on
W_{s,t,u}^{\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{1},\vec{\mathrm{j}}_{2}}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})
. We are left to obtain analogues of Lemma
6.1
and Lemma
6.2
.

For
{\mathcal{E}}_{W}^{4}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})
, we have (
7.19
). Then by Jensen’s inequality,

{\mathcal{E}}_{W}^{4}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})=c_{1}^{4}{\mathcal{E}}_{\Theta}^{4}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})+6c_{1}^{2}c_{2}^{2}({\mathcal{E}}_{\Theta}^{2}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}))^{2}+3c_{2}^{4}({\mathcal{E}}_{\Theta}^{2}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}))^{2}\leq(c_{1}^{4}+6c_{1}^{2}c_{2}^{2}+3c_{2}^{4}){\mathcal{E}}_{\Theta}^{4}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}),

which provides a fourth moment bound similar to Lemma
6.2
.

For
{\mathcal{E}}_{W}^{2}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})
, we have that

{\mathcal{E}}_{W}^{2}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})=c_{1}^{2}{\mathcal{E}}_{\Theta}^{2}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})+c_{2}^{2}{\mathcal{E}}_{G}^{2}(\vec{\mathrm{i}})=(c_{1}^{2}+c_{2}^{2}){\mathcal{E}}_{\Theta}^{2}(\vec{\mathrm{i}}).

Finally, since
c_{1},c_{2}
can be
su\sqrt{t},u\sqrt{t}
or
\sqrt{t}
for
s,u,t\in[0,1]
, all constants here are bounded and the proof is completed.
∎

8.
Proof for Theorem
1.10

For simplicity, we only consider the measure
u^{\delta}({\mathrm{d}}x):=\widetilde{u}_{0,1}^{\delta}({\mathrm{d}}x,\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbbm{R}})
. we write

(8.1)

u^{\delta}[f]:=\int_{\mathbbm{R}}f(x)u^{\delta}(1,x){\mathrm{d}}x.

We show that
u^{\delta}[f]
can be approximated by a coarse-grained model
\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(cg)}}(f,\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbbm{R}})
(see (
2.46
)) that will be specified later. Then the proof is completed by Section
7
. To proceed, we will first introduce some preliminaries in the continuous setting and compute the second moment of
u^{\delta}[f]
.

8.1.
Preliminaries

By a Feymann-Kac representation and the definition of the Wick exponential, we can rewrite (
8.1
) by a Wiener chaos expansion

(8.2)

\begin{split}u^{\delta}[f]=&\int_{\mathbbm{R}}f(x){\mathrm{d}}x+\sum\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}\beta_{\delta}^{k}\idotsint\limits_{0<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{k}<\delta^{-2}\atop x_{0},\cdots,x_{k}\in{\mathbbm{R}}}f(x_{0})g_{t_{1}}\big(x_{1}-\frac{x_{0}}{\delta}\big)\rho(x_{1}){\mathrm{d}}x_{0}{\mathrm{d}}x_{1}{\mathrm{d}}W_{t_{1}}\\
&\times\prod\limits_{i=2}^{k}g_{t_{i}-t_{i-1}}(x_{i}-x_{i-1})\rho(x_{i}){\mathrm{d}}x_{i}{\mathrm{d}}W_{t_{i}},\end{split}

where
g_{t}(x)
is the heat kernel (see (
1.31
) and d
W_{t}
is a 1-dimensional white noise in time.

Since
{\mathrm{d}}W_{t}
is centered, it directly follows that
{\mathbbm{E}}\big[u^{\delta}[f]\big]=\int_{\mathbbm{R}}f(x){\mathrm{d}}x.
By a change of variable
x_{0}\to\delta x_{0}
, (
8.2
) equals to

(8.3)

\begin{split}u^{\delta}[f]=&\int_{\mathbbm{R}}f(x){\mathrm{d}}x+\delta\sum\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}\beta_{\delta}^{k}\idotsint\limits_{0<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{k}<\delta^{-2}\atop x_{0},\cdots,x_{k}\in{\mathbbm{R}}}f(\delta x_{0}){\mathrm{d}}x_{0}\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}g_{t_{i}-t_{i-1}}(x_{i}-x_{i-1})\rho(x_{i}){\mathrm{d}}x_{i}{\mathrm{d}}W_{t_{i}},\end{split}

where
t_{0}:=0
by convention. Hence,

(8.4)

\begin{split}{\mathbbm{E}}\big[(u^{\delta}[f])^{2}\big]=&\Big(\int_{\mathbbm{R}}f(x){\mathrm{d}}x\Big)^{2}+\delta^{2}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}\beta_{\delta}^{2k}\idotsint\limits_{0<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{k}<\delta^{-2}\atop x_{0},\cdot,x_{k},y_{0},\cdots,y_{k}\in{\mathbbm{R}}}f(\delta x_{0})f(\delta y_{0}){\mathrm{d}}x_{0}{\mathrm{d}}y_{0}\\
&\times\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}g_{t_{i}-t_{i-1}}(x_{i}-x_{i-1})g_{t_{i}-t_{i-1}}(y_{i}-y_{i-1})\rho(x_{i})\rho(y_{i}){\mathrm{d}}x_{i}{\mathrm{d}}y_{i}{\mathrm{d}}t_{i}.\end{split}

We only need to analyze the summation above. To have a continuous analogue of
u(\cdot)
(see (
2.20
)), by noting that
\rho(\cdot)
has a compact support, we introduce

\widetilde{r}(t):=\iint_{{\mathbbm{R}}^{2}}g_{t}(x-x^{\prime})g_{t}(y-y^{\prime})\rho(x)\rho(y){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}y\begin{cases}\leq\|\rho\|_{\infty}^{2},&\quad\forall~t>0,\\
=\frac{1}{2\pi t}+O(t^{-2}),&\quad\text{as}~t\to+\infty.\end{cases}

We first cite some known facts about
\widetilde{r}(t)
from
[
CSZ19b
, Section 8.1]
, which will be intensively used in the following computations. Let
r(t):[0,+\infty)\to(0,+\infty)
such that

(8.5)

r(t)=\frac{1}{2\pi t}(1+o(1)),\quad\text{as}~t\to+\infty.

Given
\delta>0
, let
\{{\mathcal{T}}_{i}^{\delta}\}_{i\geq 1}
be i.i.d. random variables with density

(8.6)

{\mathbf{P}}({\mathcal{T}}_{i}^{\delta}\in{\mathrm{d}}t)=\frac{r(t)}{{\mathcal{R}}_{\delta}}\mathbbm{1}_{[0,\delta^{-2}]}(t){\mathrm{d}}t,\quad\text{where}~{\mathcal{R}}_{\delta}=\int_{0}^{\delta^{-2}}r(t){\mathrm{d}}t

is the normalization constant. It is not hard to see that
{\mathcal{T}}_{i}^{\delta}
is a continuous analogue of
\iota^{(N)}_{1}
(see (
2.21
)) Note that the following properties hold for generic
r(t)
, that is, the exact value of
o(1)
and the value of
r(t)
in a neighborhood of
0
is not important:

•

There exists some
c\in(0,1)
, such that

(8.7)

{\mathbf{P}}({\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{\delta}+\cdots+{\mathcal{T}}_{\lfloor s\log\delta^{-2}\rfloor}^{\delta}\leq\delta^{-2})\leq e^{s-c\log s},\quad\forall~\delta\in(0,1),\forall~s\in[0,+\infty).

•

Given
\vartheta\in{\mathbbm{R}}
, let

\lambda_{\delta}:=1+\frac{\vartheta}{\log\delta^{-2}}(1+o(1)).

Then for any
T\in[0,1]
,

(8.8)

\lim\limits_{\delta\to 0}\frac{1}{\log\delta^{-2}}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{\delta}^{k}{\mathbf{P}}({\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{\delta}+\cdots+{\mathcal{T}}_{k}^{\delta}\leq\delta^{-2}T)=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{\vartheta u}{\mathbf{P}}(Y_{u}\leq T){\mathrm{d}}t,

where
(Y_{s})_{s\geq 0}
is the Dickman subordinator with density
\alpha_{s}(t)
(see (
2.25
)).

Note that by (
8.7
), the main contribution in (
8.8
) comes from
k\leq A(\log\delta^{-1})
for large
A
. Moreover, we can further restrict the summation to “no consecutive increments less than
(\log\delta^{-1})^{2}
, since

(8.9)

{\mathbf{P}}\Big(\bigcup\limits_{k=1}^{A\log\delta^{-1}}\!\!\!\!\big\{{\mathcal{T}}_{k}^{\delta}\leq(\log\delta^{-1})^{2},{\mathcal{T}}_{k+1}^{\delta}\leq(\log\delta^{-1})^{2}\big\}\Big)\leq A\log\delta^{-1}{\mathbf{P}}\big({\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{\delta}\leq(\log\delta^{-1})^{2}\big)^{2}\leq C_{A}\Big(\frac{\log\log\delta^{-1}}{\log\delta^{-1}}\Big)^{2}.

We now compute the limit for the second moment of
u^{\delta}[f]
as
\delta\to 0
.

8.2.
Second moment of
u^{\delta}[f]

We only need to compute the limit for the summation on the right-hand side of (
8.4
). Our approach is an adaption of
[
CSZ19b
, Section 8]
.

We can write the summation as

(8.10)

\delta^{2}\iint_{{\mathbbm{R}}^{2}}f(\delta x)f(\delta y)K^{\delta}(x,y){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}y,

with
x_{0}:=x
,
y_{0}:=y
, and

(8.11)

K^{\delta}(x,y):=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}\beta_{\delta}^{2k}\idotsint\limits_{0<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{k}<\delta^{-2}\atop x_{1},\cdots,x_{k},y_{1},\cdots,y_{k}\in{\mathbbm{R}}}\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}g_{t_{i}-t_{i-1}}(x_{i}-x_{i-1})g_{t_{i}-t_{i-1}}(y_{i}-y_{i-1})\rho(x_{i})\rho(y_{i}){\mathrm{d}}x_{i}{\mathrm{d}}y_{i}{\mathrm{d}}t_{i}.

By a change of variable
t_{1}=\delta^{-2}t
and the translation invariance of
g_{t}(x)
, we have that

(8.12)

K^{\delta}(x,y)=\int_{0}^{1}{\mathrm{d}}t\int_{{\mathbbm{R}}^{2}}g_{t}(\delta(x^{\prime}-x))g_{t}(\delta(y^{\prime}-y))\rho(x^{\prime})\rho(y^{\prime})K_{1-t}^{\delta}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}y^{\prime},

with
x_{0}:=x^{\prime}
,
y_{0}:=y^{\prime}
, and

(8.13)

K^{\delta}_{1-t}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime}):=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\beta_{\delta}^{2(k+1)}\!\!\!\!\idotsint\limits_{0<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{k}<\delta^{-2}(1-t)\atop x_{1},\cdots,x_{k},y_{1},\cdots,y_{k}\in{\mathbbm{R}}}\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}g_{t_{i}-t_{i-1}}(x_{i}-x_{i-1})g_{t_{i}-t_{i-1}}(y_{i}-y_{i-1})\rho(x_{i})\rho(y_{i}){\mathrm{d}}x_{i}{\mathrm{d}}y_{i}{\mathrm{d}}t_{i},

and the integral is set to be
0
when
k=0
. We will see that (
8.13
) plays a the role of
\overline{U}_{N}(n)
(see (
2.24
)) in the discrete setting with
N=\lfloor\delta^{-2}\rfloor
and
n=\lfloor t\rfloor
.

Now write

\bar{r}(t)=\sup_{x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\in\text{supp}(\rho)}\iint_{{\mathbbm{R}}^{2}}g_{t}(x-x^{\prime})g_{t}(y-y^{\prime})\rho(x)\rho(y){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}y=\frac{1}{2\pi t}(1+o(1)),\quad\text{and}~\bar{{\mathcal{R}}}_{\delta}=\int_{0}^{\delta^{-2}}\bar{r}(t){\mathrm{d}}t,

and note that
\beta_{\delta}^{2}\bar{{\mathcal{R}}}_{\delta}\leq 1+\frac{c}{\log\delta^{-2}}
, since
\beta_{\delta}^{2}=\frac{\pi}{\log\delta^{-1}}+\frac{\theta+o(1)}{(\log\delta^{-1})^{2}}
. Furthermore, by (
8.7
)-(
8.9
), for
t,x^{\prime},y^{\prime}
uniformly, we can restrict the summation in (
8.13
) to
k\leq A\log\delta^{-1}
for
A
sufficiently large and restrict the integral there for
t_{1},\cdots,t_{k}
to

(8.14)

\begin{split}{\mathcal{J}}^{\delta,k}_{1-t}:=\{0<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{k}<&\delta^{-2}(1-t):t_{1}>(\log\delta^{-1})^{2},\text{and}~\forall~1\leq i\leq r-1,\\
&\text{either}~t_{i}-t_{i-1}>(\log\delta^{-1})^{2}~\text{or}~t_{i+1}-t_{i}>(\log\delta^{-1})^{2}\}.\end{split}

We denote (
8.13
) by
K_{1-t,{\mathcal{J}}}^{\delta,A}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
after the summation and integral are restricted. Let

(8.15)

r(t):=\idotsint_{{\mathbbm{R}}^{4}}\rho(x^{\prime})\rho(y^{\prime})g_{t}(x-x^{\prime})g_{t}(y-y^{\prime})\rho(x)\rho(y){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}y{\mathrm{d}}y^{\prime}=\frac{1}{2\pi t}(1+o(1)).

We can replace
g_{t_{i}-t_{i-1}}(x_{i}-x_{i-1})g_{t_{i}-t_{i-1}}(y_{i}-y_{i-1})
by
r(t)
when
t_{i}-t_{i-1}>(\log\delta^{-1})^{2}
, where an extra factor
\exp(O(\log\delta^{-1})^{-2})
appears. After all these replacements, there is a factor
\exp(O(\log\delta^{-1})^{-1})
, which converges to
1
as
\delta\to 0
.

We then treat the integral in
K_{1-t,{\mathcal{J}}}^{\delta,A}
in the following manners:

•

If
t_{i}-t_{i-1}>(\log\delta^{-1})^{2}
, then we replace
g_{t_{i}-t_{i-1}}(x_{i}-x_{i-1})g_{t_{i}-t_{i-1}}(y_{i}-y_{i-1})
by
r(t_{i}-t_{i-1})
and integrate
x_{i}
and
y_{i}
, which simply gives
\iint_{{\mathbbm{R}}^{2}}\rho(x_{i})\rho(y_{i}){\mathrm{d}}x_{i}{\mathrm{d}}y_{i}=1
.

•

If
t_{i}-t_{i-1}\leq(\log\delta^{-1})^{2}
, then we integrate
x_{i},x_{i-1},y_{i},y_{i-1}
as (
8.15
), which gives a
r(t_{i}-t_{i-1})
.

Note that since there are no consecutive
t_{i}-t_{i-1}
and
t_{i+1}-t_{i}
smaller than
(\log\delta^{-1})^{2}
and we have forced
t_{1}>(\log\delta^{-1})^{2}
, there is no
x_{i}
or
y_{i}
being integrated twice.

Finally, we have that
K_{1-t}^{\delta,A}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})=(1+o(1))\widehat{K}_{1-t}^{\delta}+o(1)
as
\delta\to 0
and
A\to\infty
, where

(8.16)

\begin{split}\widehat{K}_{1-t}^{\delta}&=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\beta_{\delta}^{2(k+1)}\iint\limits_{0<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{k}<\delta^{-2}(1-t)}\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}r(t_{i}-t_{i-1}){\mathrm{d}}t_{i}\\
&=\frac{2\pi+o(1)}{\log\delta^{-2}}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}(\beta_{\delta}^{2}\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{\delta})^{k}{\mathbf{P}}({\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{\delta}+\cdots+{\mathcal{T}}_{k}^{\delta}\leq\delta^{-2}(1-t)),\end{split}

with
T_{i}^{\delta}
and
{\mathcal{R}}_{\delta}
defined by (
8.6
).

Suppose that
\beta_{\delta}^{2}{\mathcal{R}}_{\delta}\sim 1+\frac{\vartheta}{\log\delta^{-2}}
. Then by (
8.8
) and (
2.25
), we have that

(8.17)

\lim\limits_{\delta\to 0}\widehat{K}_{1-t}^{\delta}=2\pi\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{\vartheta u}{\mathbf{P}}(Y_{u}\leq 1-t){\mathrm{d}}u=2\pi\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{\vartheta u}{\mathrm{d}}u\int_{0}^{1-t}\alpha_{u}(r){\mathrm{d}}r=2\pi\int_{0}^{1-t}G_{\vartheta}(r){\mathrm{d}}r.

Hence, by a change of variables
\delta x=x_{0}
,
\delta y=y_{0}
and
r=s-t
, the second moment of
u^{\delta}[f]
equals to
(\int_{\mathbbm{R}}f(x){\mathrm{d}}x)^{2}
plus
(1+o(1))2\pi
multiplying

(8.18)

\begin{split}&\idotsint_{{\mathbbm{R}}^{4}}f(x_{0})f(y_{0})g_{t}(\delta x^{\prime}-x_{0})g_{t}(\delta y^{\prime}-y_{0})\rho(x^{\prime})\rho(y^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}x_{0}{\mathrm{d}}y_{0}{\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}y^{\prime}\iint\limits_{0<t<s<1}G_{\vartheta}(s-t){\mathrm{d}}s{\mathrm{d}}t\\
\longrightarrow&\idotsint\limits_{0<t<s<1\atop x,y\in{\mathbbm{R}}}f(x)f(y)g_{t}(x)g_{t}(y)G_{\vartheta}(s-t){\mathrm{d}}s{\mathrm{d}}t{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}y,\quad\text{as}~\delta\to 0.\end{split}

We now determine
\vartheta
and then the second moment computations will be completed. Note that

(8.19)

{\mathcal{R}}_{\delta}=\idotsint_{{\mathbbm{R}}^{4}}\rho(x^{\prime})\rho(y^{\prime})\rho(x)\rho(y){\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}y^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}y\int_{0}^{\delta^{-2}}g_{t}(x-x^{\prime})g_{t}(y-y^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}t.

We have that by the end of
[
CSZ19b
, Section 8.2]
,

\int_{0}^{\delta^{-2}}g_{t}(x-x^{\prime})g_{t}(y-y^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}t=\frac{1}{2\pi}\Big[\log\Big(1+\frac{2}{\delta^{2}[(x-x^{\prime})^{2}+(y-y^{\prime})^{2}]}\Big)-\gamma+o(1)\Big],

and thus by
\log(1+x)\sim\log x
as
x\to\infty
,

R_{\delta}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\Big(\log\delta^{-2}+\log 2-\gamma+\idotsint_{{\mathbbm{R}}^{4}}\!\rho(x^{\prime})\rho(y^{\prime})\log\!\Big[\frac{1}{(x-x^{\prime})^{2}+(y-y^{\prime})^{2}}\Big]\rho(x)\rho(y){\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}y^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}y+o(1)\Big).

Hence, by
\beta_{\delta}^{2}=\frac{2\pi}{\log\delta^{-2}}+\frac{\theta+o(1)}{(\log\delta^{-2})^{2}}
and
\beta_{\delta}^{2}{\mathcal{R}}_{\delta}=1+\frac{\vartheta+o(1)}{\log\delta^{-2}}
, we have that

(8.20)

\vartheta=\log 2-\gamma+\idotsint_{{\mathbbm{R}}^{4}}\rho(x^{\prime})\rho(y^{\prime})\log\Big[\frac{1}{(x-x^{\prime})^{2}+(y-y^{\prime})^{2}}\Big]\rho(x)\rho(y){\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}y^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}y+\frac{\theta}{2\pi}.

8.3.
Approximation by coarse-grained model

We will approximate
u^{\delta}[f]
by a coarse-grained model
\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(cg)}}(f,\mathbbm{1}_{{\mathbbm{R}}}|\Theta)
(see (
2.46
)) with
\Theta
being determined later in (
8.23
).

We partition the time interval
[0,\delta^{-2}]
into mesoscopic intervals

(8.21)

{\mathcal{I}}_{\delta,\varepsilon}(\mathrm{i}):=[(\mathrm{i}-1)\varepsilon\delta^{-2},\varepsilon\delta^{-2}],\quad\mathrm{i}=1,\cdots,\frac{1}{\varepsilon}.

Then rearrange (
8.3
) according to the transitions in
{\mathcal{I}}_{\delta,\varepsilon}(\mathrm{i})
and we have that

(8.22)

\begin{split}u^{\delta}[f]=&\int_{\mathbbm{R}}f(x){\mathrm{d}}x+\delta\sum\limits_{k=1}^{1/\varepsilon}\sum\limits_{1\leq\mathrm{i}_{1}<\cdots<\mathrm{i}_{k}<\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}\sum\limits_{j_{0}:=0<j_{1}<\cdots<j_{k}}\int_{{\mathbbm{R}}}f(\delta x_{0}){\mathrm{d}}x_{0}\times\\
&\bigg(\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{k}\beta_{\delta}^{j_{\ell}-j_{\ell-1}}\idotsint\limits_{\hskip 8.19447ptt_{j_{\ell-1}+1}<\cdots<t_{j_{\ell}}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{\delta,\varepsilon}(\mathrm{i}_{2})\atop x_{j_{\ell-1}+1},\cdots,x_{j_{\ell}}\in{\mathbbm{R}}}\prod\limits_{i=j_{\ell-1}+1}^{j_{\ell}}g_{t_{i}-t_{i-1}}(x_{i}-x_{i-1})\rho(x_{i}){\mathrm{d}}x_{i}{\mathrm{d}}W_{t_{i}}\bigg).\end{split}

By the independent increments of
W_{t}
, when computing the second moment of (
8.22
), the cross terms related to different
\mathrm{i}_{1}<\cdots<\mathrm{i}_{k}
and
\mathrm{i}^{\prime}_{1}<\cdots<\mathrm{i}^{\prime}_{l}
have no contributions.By the second moment computations in Subsection
8.2
, we have an continuous analogue of Lemma
5.2
, that is, as
\delta\to 0
, for fixed
{\mathbf{I}}=(\mathrm{i}_{1},\cdots,\mathrm{i}_{k})
, the related second moment converges to
{\mathcal{I}}_{\varepsilon}^{f}({\mathbf{I}})
defined in (
5.5
). Thus, we can restrict the summation for
\mathrm{i}_{1},\cdots,\mathrm{i}_{k}
to
\vec{\mathbf{{\mathcal{A}}}}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(no triple)}}
defined in (
2.42
) as we have done in Section
5
.

Next, we approximate
u^{\delta}[f]
by the coarse-grained model
\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(cg)}}(f,\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbbm{R}}|\Theta)
defined by (
2.46
) with

(8.23)

\Theta(\vec{\mathrm{i}}):=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{\delta}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\sum\limits_{k=2}^{\infty}\beta_{\delta}^{k}\int_{{\mathbbm{R}}}\rho(x_{1}){\mathrm{d}}x_{1}\idotsint\limits_{t_{1}<\cdots<t_{k}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{\delta,\varepsilon}(\mathrm{i})\atop x_{2},\cdots,x_{k}\in{\mathbbm{R}}}\prod\limits_{i=2}^{k}g_{t_{i}-t_{i-1}}(x_{i}-x_{i-1})\rho(x_{i}){\mathrm{d}}x_{i}{\mathrm{d}}W_{t_{i}},&~~\text{if}~|\vec{\mathrm{i}}|=1,\\
\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{\delta}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}\beta_{\delta}^{k}&\int_{{\mathbbm{R}}}\rho(x_{1}){\mathrm{d}}x_{1}\idotsint\limits_{t_{1}<\cdots<t_{k}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{\delta,\varepsilon}(\mathrm{i})\atop x_{2},\cdots,x_{k}\in{\mathbbm{R}}}\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}g_{t_{i}-t_{i-1}}(x_{i}-x_{i-1})\rho(x_{i}){\mathrm{d}}x_{i}{\mathrm{d}}W_{t_{i}}\times\\
&\sum\limits_{j=1}^{\infty}\beta_{\delta}^{j}\int_{{\mathcal{I}}_{\delta,\varepsilon}(\mathrm{i}^{\prime})}\int_{\mathbbm{R}}g_{s_{1}-t_{k}}(y_{1}-x_{k})\rho(y_{1}){\mathrm{d}}y_{1}{\mathrm{d}}W_{s_{1}}\times\\
&\idotsint\limits_{s_{2}<\cdots<s_{j}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{\delta,\varepsilon}(\mathrm{i}^{\prime})\atop y_{2},\cdots,y_{j}\in{\mathbbm{R}}}\prod\limits_{i=2}^{j}g_{s_{i}-s_{i-1}}(y_{i}-y_{i-1})\rho(y_{i}){\mathrm{d}}y_{i}{\mathrm{d}}W_{s_{i}}\end{split},&\text{if}~|\vec{\mathrm{i}}|>1,\end{cases}

where for
k=1
, the integral is interpreted by
1
.

Suppose that
\Theta(\vec{\mathrm{i}})
and
\Theta(\vec{\mathrm{j}})
are two consecutive coarse-grained disorders. We only need to show that the replacement of
g_{t-s}(y-x)
by
\frac{\delta}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}g_{\mathrm{j}-\mathrm{i}^{\prime}}(0)
has sufficiently small costs uniformly for all
t\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\delta,\varepsilon}(\mathrm{j})
and
s\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\delta,\varepsilon}(\mathrm{i}^{\prime})
with
\mathrm{j}-\mathrm{i}^{\prime}>K_{\varepsilon}
, and for all
x,y\in\text{supp}(\rho)
. The proof is the same as the
Step 2
in proving Lemma
5.3
, and we omit the details. Hence,
u^{\delta}[f]
can be made arbitrarily close to
\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{\rm(cg)}}(f,\mathbbm{1}_{{\mathbbm{R}}})
.

Finally, we estimate the second and fourth moments of
\Theta(\vec{\mathrm{i}})
. Once we establish the analogues of Lemma
6.1
and Lemma
6.2
, then we have done the proof of Theorem
1.10
, since the rest arguments are exactly the same as those in the discrete setting (see Section
7
).

Indeed, the second moment can be computed as in Subsection
8.2
. For the fourth moment, similar to Lemma
6.2
, if we already compute the fourth moment of
\Theta(\vec{\mathrm{i}})
for
|\vec{\mathrm{i}}|\leq 2
, then for
|\vec{\mathrm{i}}|\geq 3
, we can decouple
\Theta(\mathrm{i},\mathrm{i})
and
\Theta(\mathrm{i}^{\prime},\mathrm{i}^{\prime})
and the result follows immediately. Hence, it only remains to compute the case
|\vec{\mathrm{i}}|\leq 2
. Without loss of generality, we only need to treat the case
|\vec{\mathrm{i}}|=1
.

We have that by Gaussian property and Wick formula,
{\mathbbm{E}}[(\Theta(i,i))^{4}]
is bounded above by

(8.24)

\begin{split}\frac{3\delta^{4}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\sum\limits_{k=2}^{\infty}\beta_{\delta}^{4k}&\Bigg(\iint_{{\mathbbm{R}}^{2}}\rho(x_{1})\rho(y_{1}){\mathrm{d}}x_{1}{\mathrm{d}}y_{1}\idotsint\limits_{t_{1}<\cdots<t_{k}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{\delta,\varepsilon}(\mathrm{i})\atop x_{2},y_{2},\cdots,x_{k},y_{k}\in{\mathbbm{R}}}\prod\limits_{i=2}^{k}g_{t_{i}-t_{i-1}}(x_{i}-x_{i-1})g_{t_{i}-t_{i-1}}(y_{i}-y_{i-1})\times\\
&\rho(x_{i})\rho(y_{i}){\mathrm{d}}x_{i}{\mathrm{d}}y_{i}\Bigg)^{2}+\frac{\delta^{4}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\Bigg(\sum\limits_{m=2}^{\infty}\beta_{\delta}^{2m}\iint_{{\mathbbm{R}}^{2}}\rho(x_{1})\rho(y_{1}){\mathrm{d}}x_{1}{\mathrm{d}}y_{1}\idotsint\limits_{t_{1}<\cdots<t_{m}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{\delta,\varepsilon}(\mathrm{i})\atop x_{2},y_{2},\cdots,x_{m},y_{m}\in{\mathbbm{R}}}\\
&\prod\limits_{i=2}^{m}g_{t_{i}-t_{i-1}}(x_{i}-x_{i-1})g_{t_{i}-t_{i-1}}(y_{i}-y_{i-1})\rho(x_{i})\rho(y_{i}){\mathrm{d}}x_{i}{\mathrm{d}}y_{i}\Bigg)^{2}.\end{split}

As
\delta\to 0
, the second term above converges to

(8.25)

\begin{split}\frac{4\pi}{\varepsilon^{2}}\Big(\iint_{(\mathrm{i}-1)\varepsilon<s<t<\mathrm{i}\varepsilon}G_{\vartheta}(t-s){\mathrm{d}}s{\mathrm{d}}t\Big)^{2}\leq\frac{C}{(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon})^{2}}.\end{split}

For the first term, by (
8.6
)-(
8.9
), as
\delta\to 0
, it equals to

(8.26)

\begin{split}\lim\limits_{\delta\to 0}&\frac{3}{\varepsilon^{2}}\sum\limits_{k=2}^{\infty}\lambda_{\delta}^{2k}\bigg(\int_{(\mathrm{i}-1)\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{i}\varepsilon}{\mathbf{P}}\Big({\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{\delta}+\cdots+{\mathcal{T}}_{k}^{\delta}\leq\delta^{-2}(1-t)\Big){\mathrm{d}}t\bigg)^{2}\\
&=\frac{3}{\varepsilon^{2}}\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{2\vartheta u}\bigg(\int_{(\mathrm{i}-1)\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{i}\varepsilon}{\mathrm{d}}t\int_{0}^{1-t}\alpha_{u}(s){\mathrm{d}}s\bigg)^{2}{\mathrm{d}}u.\end{split}

Note that by (
2.25
), uniformly for
u\geq 0
,

e^{\vartheta u}\int_{(\mathrm{i}-1)\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{i}\varepsilon}{\mathrm{d}}t\int_{0}^{1-t}\alpha_{u}(s){\mathrm{d}}s\leq\varepsilon e^{\vartheta u}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{e^{-\gamma u}us^{u-1}}{\Gamma(u+1)}{\mathrm{d}}s=\varepsilon\frac{e^{(\vartheta-\gamma)u}}{\Gamma(u+1)}\leq C\varepsilon.

Hence, (
8.26
) is bounded above by

\frac{C}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{\vartheta u}{\mathrm{d}}u\int_{(\mathrm{i}-1)\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{i}\varepsilon}{\mathrm{d}}t\int_{0}^{1-t}\alpha_{u}(s){\mathrm{d}}s=\frac{C}{\varepsilon}\iint_{(\mathrm{i}-1)\varepsilon<s<t<\mathrm{i}\varepsilon}G_{\vartheta}(t-s){\mathrm{d}}s{\mathrm{d}}t\leq\frac{C}{\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}},

which completes the proof.

Appendix A
Enhanced Lindeberg Principle

To make this paper more self-contained, we cite
[
CSZ23
, Appendix A]
here.

Assumption A.1

(Local dependence)
.

Let
X=(X_{i})_{i\in{\mathbbm{T}}}
be a family of random variables, such that

(1)
{\mathbbm{E}}[X_{i}]=0
and
{\mathbbm{E}}[X_{i}X_{j}]=\sigma_{ij}
.

(2) For any
k\in{\mathbbm{T}}
, there is an
A_{k}\subset{\mathbbm{T}}
, such that
X_{k}
is independent of
(X_{i})_{i\in A_{k}^{c}}
.

(3) For any
k\in{\mathbbm{T}}
and any
l\in A_{k}
, there is an
A_{kl}\subset{\mathbbm{T}}
such that
(X_{k},X_{l})
is independent of
(X_{i})_{i\in A_{kl}^{c}}
.

The set
(A_{k})_{k\in{\mathbbm{T}}}
and
(A_{kl})_{k\in{\mathbbm{T}},l\in A_{k}}
will be called dependency neighborhoods of
X=(X_{i})_{i\in{\mathbbm{T}}}
.

Let
Z=(Z_{i})_{i\in{\mathbbm{T}}}\sim({\mathcal{N}}(0,\sigma_{ii}))_{i\in{\mathbbm{T}}}
be a family of Gaussian random variables, which is independent of
X
but has the same covariance matrix as
X
. For
u,t\in[0,1]
,
k\in{\mathbbm{T}}
and
l\in A_{k}
, we define

(A.1)

W_{t,u}^{k,l}:=u\sqrt{t}X^{A_{kl}}+\sqrt{t}A^{A_{kl}^{c}}+\sqrt{1-t}Z,

where
X^{B}
denotes
X^{B}_{i}=X_{i}\mathbbm{1}_{\{i\in B\}}
for
B\subset{\mathbbm{T}}
. For
s,u,t\in[0,1]
,
k\in{\mathbbm{T}}
and
l\in A_{k}
, we further define

(A.2)

W_{s,t,u}^{k,l}:=su\sqrt{t}X^{A_{k}}+u\sqrt{t}X^{A_{kl}\backslash A_{k}}+\sqrt{t}X^{A_{kl}^{c}}+\sqrt{1-t}Z.

We will apply following lemmas in proving our main results, which are
[
CSZ23
, Lemma A.2-A.4]
. Note that we use
\partial_{k}
to denote the partial derivative with respect to
X_{k}
.

Lemma A.1
.

Let
X,Z,W_{t,u}^{k,l}
and
W_{s,t,u}^{k,l}
be defined as above. Let
h:{\mathbbm{R}}^{|{\mathbbm{T}}|}\to{\mathbbm{R}}
be a bounded and thrice differentiable function. Then
{\mathbbm{E}}[h(X)]-{\mathbbm{E}}[h(Z)]=I_{1}+I_{2}
,
where

(A.3)

\displaystyle I_{1}:=\frac{1}{2}\iiint_{[0,1]^{3}}

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{k\in{\mathbbm{T}},l\in A_{k},m\in A_{kl}}{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[X_{l}X_{k}X_{m}(s\mathbbm{1}_{\{m\in A_{K}\}})+\mathbbm{1}_{\{m\in A_{kl}\backslash A_{k}\}}\sqrt{t}\partial^{3}_{klm}h\big(W_{s,t,u}^{k,l}\big)\Big]{\mathrm{d}}s{\mathrm{d}}t{\mathrm{d}}u,

(A.4)

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle:=-\frac{1}{2}\iint_{[0,1]^{2}}\sum\limits_{k\in{\mathbbm{T}},l\in A_{k},m\in A_{kl}}\sigma_{kl}{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[X_{m}\sqrt{t}\partial^{3}_{klm}h\big(W_{t,u}^{k,l}\big)\Big]{\mathrm{d}}t{\mathrm{d}}u.

Lemma A.2
.

Let
Z=(Z_{i})_{i\in{\mathbbm{T}}}\sim({\mathcal{N}}(0,\sigma_{ii}))_{i\in{\mathbbm{T}}}
and
\widetilde{Z}=(\widetilde{Z}_{i})_{i\in{\mathbbm{T}}}\sim({\mathcal{N}}(0,\widetilde{\sigma}_{ii}))_{i\in{\mathbbm{T}}}
be two families of centered Gaussian random variables with covariance matrices
(\sigma_{ij})_{i,j\in{\mathbbm{T}}}
and
(\widetilde{\sigma}_{ij})_{i,j\in{\mathbbm{T}}}
respectively. For any bounded and twice differentiable function
h:{\mathbbm{R}}^{|{\mathbbm{T}}|}\to{\mathbbm{R}}
, we have that

(A.5)

{\mathbbm{E}}[h(Z)]-{\mathbbm{E}}[h(\widetilde{Z})]=I_{3}:=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k,l\in{\mathbbm{T}}}(\widetilde{\sigma}_{kl}-\sigma_{kl})\int_{0}^{1}{\mathbbm{E}}\big[\partial^{2}_{kl}h(W_{t})\big]{\mathrm{d}}t,

where
W_{t}:=\sqrt{t}\widetilde{Z}+\sqrt{1-t}Z
.

Lemma A.3
.

Let
f:{\mathbbm{R}}\to{\mathbbm{R}}
be a bounded function with bounded first three derivatives. Assume
h(X):=f(\Lambda(X))
with

\Lambda(X)=\sum\limits_{I\subset{\mathbbm{T}}}c_{I}\prod_{i\in I}x_{i}

a polynomial with coefficients
c_{I}\in{\mathbbm{R}}
. Furthermore, assume that

(A.6)

\forall~k\in{\mathbbm{T}},l\in A_{k},m\in A_{kl},\quad\partial_{km}^{2}\Lambda=\partial_{lm}^{2}\Lambda=\partial_{kl}^{2}\Lambda=0.

Then for
I_{1},I_{2}
and
I_{3}
in (
A.3
)-(
A.5
), we have that

(A.7)

\displaystyle|I_{1}|\leq\frac{1}{2}\|

\displaystyle f^{\prime\prime\prime}\|_{\infty}\sup_{k\in{\mathbbm{T}}}{\mathbbm{E}}\big[|X_{k}|^{3}\big]\sum\limits_{k\in{\mathbbm{T}},l\in A_{k},m\in A_{kl}}

\displaystyle\sup_{s,t,u}{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\big|\partial_{k}\Lambda(W_{s,t,u}^{k,l})\big|^{3}\Big]^{\frac{1}{3}}\sup_{s,t,u}{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\big|\partial_{l}\Lambda(W_{s,t,u}^{k,l})\big|^{3}\Big]^{\frac{1}{3}}\sup_{s,t,u}{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\big|\partial_{m}\Lambda(W_{s,t,u}^{k,l})\big|^{3}\Big]^{\frac{1}{3}},

(A.8)

\displaystyle|I_{2}|\leq\frac{1}{2}\|

\displaystyle f^{\prime\prime\prime}\|_{\infty}\sup_{k\in{\mathbbm{T}}}{\mathbbm{E}}\big[|X_{k}|^{3}\big]\sum\limits_{k\in{\mathbbm{T}},l\in A_{k},m\in A_{kl}}

\displaystyle\sup_{t,u}{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\big|\partial_{k}\Lambda(W_{t,u}^{k,l})\big|^{3}\Big]^{\frac{1}{3}}\sup_{t,u}{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\big|\partial_{l}\Lambda(W_{t,u}^{k,l})\big|^{3}\Big]^{\frac{1}{3}}\sup_{t,u}{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\big|\partial_{m}\Lambda(W_{t,u}^{k,l})\big|^{3}\Big]^{\frac{1}{3}},

(A.9)

\displaystyle|I_{3}|\leq\frac{1}{2}\|

\displaystyle f^{\prime\prime}\|_{\infty}\sum\limits_{k\in{\mathbbm{T}},l\in A_{k}}(\widetilde{\sigma}_{kl}-\sigma_{kl})\sup_{t}{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\big|\partial_{k}\Lambda(W_{t})\big|^{2}\Big]^{\frac{1}{2}}\sup_{t}{\mathbbm{E}}\Big[\big|\partial_{l}\Lambda(W_{t})\big|^{2}\Big]^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Remark A.4
.

Lemma
A.1
-
A.3
can be extended to the vector case. Suppose that in the above lemmas,

h(\cdot)=f(\Lambda_{1}(\cdot),\cdots,\Lambda_{k}(\cdot)),

where
k
is a finite positive integer and
f:{\mathbbm{R}}^{k}\to k
is a bounded function with bounded derivatives up to third order. Similar bounds as (
A.7
)-(
A.9
) still hold. For (
A.7
) and (
A.8
), we need to replace
\|f^{\prime\prime\prime}\|_{\infty}
by
\max_{1\leq i,j,l\leq k}\|\partial_{i,j,l}f\|_{\infty}
, replace the three occurrences of
\Lambda
by
\Lambda_{i},\Lambda_{j}
and
\Lambda_{l}
, and sum over
1\leq i,j,l\leq k
. For (
A.9
), we need to replace
\|f^{\prime\prime}\|_{\infty}
by
\max_{1\leq i,j\leq k}
, replace the two occurrences of
\Lambda
by
\Lambda_{i}
and
\Lambda_{j}
, and sum over
1\leq i,j\leq k
.
